#5943. Rubio takes aim at 'soulless global bureaucrats' pushing Marxism to undermine America

Federalist Society convention attendees weigh whether Rubio will offer a Republican roadmap to recovery, defend the administration’s foreign policy or signal his own political ambitions.

FIRST ON FOX: Secretary of State Marco Rubio is taking aim at "soulless global bureaucrats" using "rules-based order" arguments to undermine U.S. foreign policy in a speech Friday, warning that the "spirit of Marx" outlived the Soviet Union.

U.S. leaders of both parties defended the "rules-based international order," the network of postwar institutions America built. Rubio is set to argue the system has been turned against the country that built it, the same case he made at his January 2025 confirmation hearing, when he called the postwar order "a weapon being used against us."

In a preview of Rubio’s speech obtained by Fox News Digital, he outlines how Marxism still has a strong presence in Western society, a view which is relevant amid the rise of communist and Marxist beliefs that have driven protests against the administration and Western beliefs. The remarks are subject to change during his speech.

"For this global elite, 'international law' is not a neutral body of rules," Rubio is expected to say. "It's an instrument to advance their interests under the pretense of universal principles. They’re staunch defenders of democracy, until a particular election challenges their interests."

RUBIO PUTS ENTIRE WORLD ON NOTICE AGAINST RISE OF 'POISONOUS' FAR-LEFT TERROR 'MASKED AS EQUALITY'

U.S. President-elect Donald Trump’s nominee for Secretary of State, Sen. Marco Rubio testifies during his Senate Foreign Relations confirmation hearing at Dirksen Senate Office Building on Jan. 15, 2025, in Washington, D.C. (Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images)

"Even as we defeated the institution of Soviet communism, we did not triumph over the spirit of Marx," Rubio’s expected remarks read. "It lives on, in these new forms and doctrines, which would subordinate the national will to the menial consensus of the world. Marxism, in its traditional expression, sought to abolish the distinctions between men; the radicals of our time seek to abolish the distinctions between nations themselves."

Rubio is expected to address the Federalist Society at the National Lawyers Convention on Friday, when he will go after the International Criminal Court and other global bodies he says are using the law as a political weapon, not to deliver justice. The secretary of state argues that they're trying to tie America's hands and let unelected judges and bureaucrats overseas override decisions that belong to the American people, according to a State Department source.

RUBIO'S CALL TO COMBAT GLOBAL FUNDERS OF FAR-LEFT TERRORISM COULD PUT SINGHAM IN CROSSHAIRS

David Chung, organizing director at The People’s Forum, prepares for an anti-war demonstration in Union Square in New York City, left. Tech tycoon Neville Roy Singham attends The Red Party in New York City on Feb. 14, 2018, right. (Rashid Umar Abbasi for Fox News Digital; Mark Sagliocco/Getty Images)

The secretary of state is expected to defend the administration's sanctions on the ICC, which he calls "an unelected world tribunal." If it tries to enforce its "paper decrees" against Americans, he plans to say, the U.S. answer will echo the Roman general Pompey: "Stop quoting laws to men with swords."

He also will push back on those who say Americans live on "stolen land," arguing that critics who stand behind this belief deny the nation's right to exist, and cast the U.S. as "a nation of irrepressible ambition," according to the excerpts.

REAGAN-ERA OFFICIAL WARNS ‘RED-GREEN ALLIANCE’ HAS SHARED GOAL OF ‘DESTROYING’ AMERICA

Last week, Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed invoked "self-determination" and developed an argument about America’s role in the international system created after World War II, calling it a "chief violator" of international law.

"The beauty of this country is our ideals," El-Sayed said.

"We led the effort in this country to build a rules-based international order," he continued. "We built the United Nations and the World Health Organization and the International Court of Justice and the International Criminal Court, so that we could uphold the ideals of democracy. We did that. And then we proceeded to be the chief violator of exactly those same ideals. To call our country to those ideals became an open question, how could you?"

LAWMAKERS RAISE ALARM OVER NEVILLE ROY SINGHAM'S $278M NETWORK SPREADING CCP PROPAGANDA IN THE U.S.

El-Sayed’s words echoed language used late last year by Neville Roy Singham, whose financial network has supported CodePink and other left-wing organizations.

After his wedding to Evans in 2017 and the sale of ThoughtWorks, the technology company he founded, Singham directed $285 million to nonprofits and media organizations, according to a Fox News Digital investigation. The People’s Forum received $22.4 million from Singham, while CodePink received $1.33 million. BreakThrough BT Media Inc., which operates BreakThrough News, received $1.098 million, according to tax filings. Singham and his wife, CodePink co-founder Jodie Evans, live in Shanghai, China.

In a speech exactly one year ago, on Nov. 13, 2025, to the "Global South Academic Forum" at the Golden Tulip Hotel in Shanghai, Singham argued that the existing "international rules-based order" was built on a false narrative of World War II and called for a "new world order" based on multilateralism advanced by Chinese President Xi Jinping and the Communist Party of China.

JIM BANKS, GOP LAWMAKERS RALLY BEHIND DOJ PROBE INTO ALLEGED CCP-LINKED FUNDING NETWORK

"The international rules-based order was created by another lie," Singham said.

"If we want to therefore have a new world order that is based on the multilateralism that President Xi and CPC and China have proposed, we have to undo the ideological damage that has been done by the narrative of World War II," Singham had said.

RUBIO SANCTIONS CUBAN GROUPS WITH TIES TO US NONPROFIT NETWORK FUNDED BY COMMUNIST DONOR NEVILLE ROY SINGHAM

This hostility to the U.S. has infused itself in the anti-American work of far-left organizations.

At the People's Forum, Tricontinental and other groups funded by Singham, the programming includes Cuba solidarity campaigns, Venezuela advocacy, anti-Iran sanctions initiatives, opposition to NATO expansion and educational events on China, all framed within a broader narrative of "resistance" to U.S. influence.

Event flyers and social media posts repeatedly highlight Cuba, promoting teach-ins, cultural programming and delegations opposing U.S. policy.

CodePink, the People's Forum and the Party for Socialism and Liberation are part of a network that is organizing protests, messaging, rallies and other symbols of support for the Communist Party of Cuba. (X/CodePink, X/People's Forum, X/Party for Socialism and Liberation)

That narrative aligns with Singham’s rejection of the Western system.

Fox News Digital investigations have found that the "spirit of Marx" has storefronts, syllabi, fundraising apparatus in America’s nonprofit world and a critical role as the conduit for sending foot soldiers into America’s streets for protests to defend communist regimes across the world and promulgate anti-American propaganda.

US BASES, ICE FACILITIES TARGETED IN CUBA-LINKED 24-HOUR ACTIVIST MOBILIZATION PLAN

At the People’s Forum in Manhattan, its 1804 Books bookstore and press describes itself as a source of "socialist literature and revolutionary theory," selling books by Marx, including a coloring book for children. Fox News Digital’s investigation traced $22.44 million to the People’s Forum and $16.76 million to Tricontinental through Neville Roy Singham’s funding network.

The bookstore’s catalog offers a reading list for that worldview: Marx’s Critique of the Gotha Program, his Critique of Political Economy and The German Ideology, written with philosopher Friedrich Engels.

Within the Singham network, Tricontinental places Marxism in its institutional purpose. Its statement of mission and work identifies "national liberation Marxism" as its anchoring tradition and invokes Marx’s argument that philosophy should change the world.

Across the wider nonprofit landscape, other organizations carry Marx in their names or expressly embrace his ideas. The "Marxist Education Project" says it operates through the tax-exempt Movement Education Project Group Inc. and embraces Marxism as its framework. The People’s Forum once hosted its Capital, Volume II study group.

The "People Before Profits Education Fund," also known as the Chelsea Fund for Education, solicits support for "Marxist education" and lists the "Little Red Schoolhouse," a national Marxist school training young activists, among its projects. The "Marxists Internet Archive" describes itself as a nonprofit public library preserving Marxist writings.

Preston Mizell is a writer with Fox News Digital covering breaking news.

#5942. Will German Rearmament Save Europe?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: Will German Rearmament Save Europe?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

During NATO’s formative years, the U.S. military commitment to the old continent was a tenuous thing. Dwight Eisenhower, appointed the first supreme allied commander of Europe in 1950, argued—amid a vicious debate in Washington over committing additional divisions to Europe—that American forces should only act as a bridge until Europe stepped up to defend itself. If the United States still had troops in Europe 10 years hence, he said in 1951, then the trans-Atlantic project would be a failure.

Ike’s forecast, it seems, was wrong on both counts. Seventy-five years on, roughly 80,000 U.S. troops remain on the continent. Yet few would regard the alliance as a failure. The U.S. military commitment succeeded in guaranteeing Europe’s freedom and keeping the Soviets at bay. But Europe never fully shouldered the burden of its own defense.

During NATO’s formative years, the U.S. military commitment to the old continent was a tenuous thing. Dwight Eisenhower, appointed the first supreme allied commander of Europe in 1950, argued—amid a vicious debate in Washington over committing additional divisions to Europe—that American forces should only act as a bridge until Europe stepped up to defend itself. If the United States still had troops in Europe 10 years hence, he said in 1951, then the trans-Atlantic project would be a failure.

Germany Rearmed: The Return of War and the End of Illusions, Liana Fix, Oxford University Press, 216 pp., $34.99, November 2026

Ike’s forecast, it seems, was wrong on both counts. Seventy-five years on, roughly 80,000 U.S. troops remain on the continent. Yet few would regard the alliance as a failure. The U.S. military commitment succeeded in guaranteeing Europe’s freedom and keeping the Soviets at bay. But Europe never fully shouldered the burden of its own defense.

Old assumptions, however, are finally changing. Squeezed by both a volatile Washington and voracious Moscow, Europe has finally awoken to its security dilemma: Its defense spending has nearly doubled in six years in real terms, from 242 billion euros in 2020 to 454 billion euros in 2026. If by 2035 Europe fully meets its NATO military expenditure target—spending 3.5 percent of GDP on defense (compared to the little more than 2 percent that it spends today)—then the surge in annual spending will rival the scale of the Marshall Plan relative to Europe’s GDP.

As Council on Foreign Relations scholar Liana Fix argues in her incisive new book, Germany Rearmed, the central protagonist in this story is Berlin. Germany has doubled defense spending since 2021 and accounts for more than a quarter of the European Union’s increase over that period. Based on its current budget, by 2029, Germany will spend more on its military than France and the U.K. today spend combined.

But is a rearmed Germany, in fact, a good thing for Europe and the world? Fix makes a compelling case that Berlin shedding its “myth of pacifism” is in the best interest of both Europe and the United States. But risks abound. A militarily empowered Germany has centrifugal potential, threatening friction with prickly France and anxious neighbors in Central and Eastern Europe if the rearmament is not executed within a broader European framework. And if the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party eventually takes the reins of what will be one of the world’s most powerful militaries, it would reawaken historical nightmares.

Germany Rearmed gives proper airing to the consequences and challenges of German rearmament, often left unanalyzed amid general backslapping about surging European defense spending. The U.S. commitment to Europe is ultimately unsustainable at current levels as Washington focuses on growing threats in the Pacific—and given its isolationist spasms at home. And Europe, with Germany as its backbone, has the overwhelming means to check a revanchist Russia—Germany’s economy is roughly two times the size of Russia’s, and the EU’s is nearly 10 times its size.

But policymakers in Europe and Berlin should take heed of Fix’s advice. She argues that Germany needs to do a better job of Europeanizing its rearmament—in force structure, spending on a Europe-wide military industrial base, and joint funding—or it risks undermining European cohesion as the United States draws down.

West Germany participates for the first time as NATO’s 15th member in Paris, France, on May 9, 1955. AFP via Getty Images

Germany’s recent rearmament push, at first blush, may appear as a sudden break from the past. In popular perception, the allied occupation after World War II underwrote an enduring German aversion to military power, just as in Japan—whose own story of U.S. occupation and political transformation is beautifully told in John W. Dower’s Pulitzer-winning Embracing Defeat. The Western security architecture during the Cold War then reinforced German passivity: Lord Hastings Ismay, NATO’s first secretary-general, allegedly described NATO’s purpose as to “keep the Russians out, the Americans in, and the Germans down.”

This “myth of pacifism,” according to Fix, has permeated German history ever since: from Chancellor Willy Brandt’s Ostpolitik of the 1970s to the Wandel durch Handel (“change through trade”) approach to Russia and China adopted under both Chancellors Gerhard Schröder and Angela Merkel. At seemingly every opportunity, Germany prioritized diplomacy and economic engagement over military power, believing the former to be more attuned to the future. This belief hardened after 1989, when Germans concluded (correctly) that the Soviet collapse owed more to Mikhail Gorbachev’s reforms than to U.S. President Ronald Reagan’s military flexing.

Crucially, as Fix observes, the German body politic has no grand military tradition to lean on, unlike its European neighbors. The United Kingdom can look back nostalgically to its days of ruling the waves. France’s defiant Gaullist military tradition is having its moment in the sun in the wake of Julian Jackson’s masterful book De Gaulle, adapted into a recently released blockbuster biopic. Germany, for obvious reasons, can’t draw from its 20th-century military history; even Prussian militarism is viewed skeptically with the hindsight of what followed.

But Fix provides nuance to this myth of pacifism, arguing that Germany was in fact no stranger to hard power after WWII. West German defense spending surpassed 3 percent of GDP through the mid-1970s, and even after the Cold War, the Bundeswehr fought in out-of-area operations in both the Balkans and Afghanistan, where it lost 55 soldiers—a fact little remembered in Germany today.

Quibbles aside, Germany has undeniably entered a new relationship with its military. In addition to the doubling of defense spending over the past five years, polling from fall 2024 showed that 73 percent of Germans wanted to invest more money in European security and 65 percent supported a spending target of 3 percent of GDP or higher.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz and U.S. President Donald Trump speak at the G-7 summit, in Evian, France, on June 16.Christian Hartmann / POOL / AFP via Getty Images

While many commentators would gladly attribute this wake-up call to Russian President Vladimir Putin’s brutal invasion of Ukraine, Fix inconveniently but convincingly credits U.S. President Donald Trump’s return to the White House as the real catalyst.

After Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine, German Chancellor Olaf Scholz claimed grandiloquently in his “Zeitenwende” speech that “the world … will no longer be the same as before.” But while his 100 billion euro special defense fund was a good first step, disbursement was slow, and it wasn’t followed by other bold moves.

Only in 2025, with Chancellor Friedrich Merz facing a hostile Washington, did Germany finally turn a corner. Between 2025 and 2029, Germany plans to spend a total of 649 billion euros and reach annual spending of 3.5 percent of GDP—up from just 1.15 percent in 2015, the year after Russia’s annexation of Crimea.

Referring to Russia’s second invasion of Ukraine in 2022 and the United States’ reelection of Trump in 2024, Fix memorably writes that “history had to repeat itself twice for Germany to abandon the ‘myth of pacifism’ and to seriously begin rearmament.”

It’s a remarkable feature of our turbulent era that the breakneck rearmament of Germany is considered, in the main, a beneficent thing. Economists surveying the many challenges of slowing European growth and flagging competitiveness point to continental rearmament as a sole bright spot: a fiscal stimulus surpassing COVID-era spending that may boost sagging European productivity growth by 0.25 percent per every 1 percent of GDP spent on defense, if focused on research and development as well as equipment rather than military pensions and personnel, according to estimates from the Kiel Institute.

And Germany is undoubtedly the most consequential player in the story of Europe’s broader rearmament. With French bond markets quaking, Germany is one of the only countries with the fiscal headroom to spend, with a debt-to-GDP ratio of 64 percent. Germany’s contribution to increased spending will also invariably be Europe’s largest, given that its economy is a third larger than that of the U.K. and 50 percent larger than France. And its industrial prowess positions it well to play the role of Europe’s arsenal.

But what kind of military power will Germany be? Here Fix rightly throws up notes of caution. Thus far, Germany’s rearmament has largely been a national one, with spending at the German rather than EU level and funding largely going to German defense players. This trajectory risks fragmenting Europe’s military production, while also enhancing Germany’s own preeminent power within the continent—inviting suspicion and potentially exacerbating European divisions. It’s hard to envision outright conflict between Germany and its neighbors, but a more assertive Germany backed by hard power may more forcefully push its own foreign agenda over, say, Poland or France. The U.S. security blanket helped keep a lid on these rivalries since World War II; if Washington retreats from the continent, competing national priorities may sharpen with the steel of military spending.

The country’s cinema has recently taken a very dark turn.

Fix’s solution, which is a compelling one, is for Germany to rearm inside either a more European NATO or a broader European security architecture: spending at European level where possible on shared platforms, inside a more integrated European command, and across a continent-wide military industrial base. Such “Europeanized” spending would allay concerns about the contortions in power caused by German rearmament. It would also reassure allies wary of an AfD ascension in Berlin: tied into a NATO or European architecture, a remilitarized Germany would have a harder time pursuing an overtly nationalistic military agenda.

The question of Washington’s future role on the continent still presents a broad conundrum. Fix wrote Germany Rearmed in 2025 and early 2026, and the rawness of the Trump administration’s provocations on trade and Greenland shines through. She argues that Washington is “never going back” and that the future trans-Atlantic relationship under either Democrats or Republicans will resemble “a patron-client relationship more than an alliance, with no preferred treatment for Europeans.”

This likely over-indexes on the wrecking ball of the Trump administration’s first two years. After the Republicans’ drubbing in the midterms, and considering that Trump’s favoritism toward Putin’s Russia and Xi Jinping’s China is idiosyncratic even within his party, the possibilities of U.S.-European reconciliation are higher than she lets on. Brussels and Washington will still have overwhelming shared interests come a new U.S. administration in 2029, especially as Europe finally awakens to the threat of surging Chinese exports and barrels into a trade conflict with Beijing over the coming years. More likely is a United States that is less bullying and extractive, whether a Republican or Democratic administration, and remains committed to NATO and Article V—even if it pushes for Europe to shoulder more of its defense burden while gradually winding down its troop commitment.

New reservists of the Bundeswehr train at Camp Aulenbach near Baumholder, Germany, on Sept. 15. Andreas Rentz/Getty Images

This matters because it should inform how Europe rearms. Replacing core U.S. enablers, such as space-based intelligence and integrated air and missile defense systems, would be a costly endeavor that would eat up a tremendous amount of European capacity and take up to a decade. The International Institute for Strategic Studies puts the cost of fully replacing US capabilities at more than $1 trillion.

A more prudent wager is that a future United States will maintain its nuclear umbrella over Europe and the backbone of critical NATO capabilities while Europe builds capacity in complementary areas such as ground forces, missiles, unmanned systems, and munitions. Betting on even limited U.S. staying power may seem foolhardy at the moment, but the alternative would be an expensive distraction that Europe can ill afford.

The greater threat to European security and cohesion is more likely to come from within. The AfD now leads every national poll in Germany, averaging 29 percent to the Christian Democratic Union/Christian Socialist Union’s 19 percent. Its path to the chancellery as German elections approach in 2029 remains narrow given that Germany maintains a strong political firewall against the party in light of history. But the AfD agenda should terrify us: The party has an affinity for Russia and China, and it advocates for even greater defense spending of 5 percent of GDP in pursuit of national sovereignty.

However unlikely an AfD-run Germany may be, populist convulsions within Europe are certain. Fix spends little time on the rise in France of the far-right National Rally, which appears poised to win the French presidency in the spring. How a right-wing France in the engine room of Europe would affect broader rearmament is a question that should be top of mind in Brussels today.

The implications of German rearmament are vexing, and the recent surge in spending represents easy yards. But for a remilitarized Germany to truly enhance European security, it must surrender more of its own power, decision-making, and even defense-industrial spending to the rest of Europe. Otherwise, Europe will have answered Eisenhower’s challenge 75 years on only to produce a more fractured continent as the United States pulls back.

#5941. China’s AI Diplomacy Is Working

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DANIEL REMLER is a Senior Fellow with the Technology and National Security Program at the Center for a New American Security. He served as AI Policy Coordinator in the Office of the Special Envoy for Critical and Emerging Technology at the State Department from 2023 to 2025.

Can the United States and China simultaneously cooperate and compete on AI? That is the question driving debates in Washington about the wisdom of pursuing talks on the subject with Beijing. The Biden administration paired sweeping technology restrictions with a narrow dialogue on risk, and the Trump administration has kept a channel open even as it has downplayed safety. Some experts go further. They hope that cooperation on safety can be siloed off from competition entirely, a belief as appealing as it is fanciful. Any dialogue on AI risk takes place within the broader strategic competition between the United States and China.

Beijing has long appreciated this dynamic. Much of Washington has not. Whereas the United States has shifted its approach to AI competition and safety across and within presidential administrations, Beijing has kept its AI diplomacy focused on gaining competitive advantage over the United States. China has taken part in international AI safety discussions, but it has used them, along with talks with the United States, to paint U.S. export controls as the main obstacle to cooperation. It has cast itself as the champion of the developing world, promising a Chinese-led AI ecosystem without technological or financial barriers as an alternative to a restrictive U.S.-led AI order. The pitch is working. A growing number of states are embracing China’s message, and Chinese open-weight models are gaining users across the developing world.

If the United States wants to maintain its lead and make meaningful progress on safety, it needs to treat the dialogue as part of the competition with China. To do so, it must ramp up pressure on China outside the talks and offer countries still choosing sides a more compelling vision for AI adoption and governance. And when U.S. and Chinese officials next meet, in November, Washington should test Beijing’s seriousness about safety. In short, the United States should approach AI diplomacy with the same strategic clarity China has.

The Biden administration’s first round of advanced semiconductor export controls in October 2022 and the release of ChatGPT a month later signaled that the United States had taken an early lead in the AI competition and would act to weaken China’s AI industry. Washington and its allies also seized the diplomatic initiative, taking the first steps toward global AI governance. In May 2023, the G-7 began intense discussions on AI governance that would eventually produce a code of conduct for developers. Weeks later, the United Kingdom announced it would host the first global AI safety summit at Bletchley Park.

China responded by rolling out a diplomatic script it has been honing ever since. Its core tenets first appeared in the Global AI Governance Initiative, published in October 2023. It called for countries to “put equal emphasis on development and security” (though it left security to be defined by individual states) and contrasted China’s focus on cooperation and development with U.S. export controls, attacking Washington for “creating barriers and disrupting the global AI supply chain through technological monopolies and unilateral coercive measures.” Despite Beijing’s growing body of AI regulations at home, the initiative offered little more than slogans on the technology’s risks.

That basic formula—cooperation and development, set against U.S. restrictions, with little to say about risk—has guided China’s AI diplomacy for the last three years. In March 2024, the UN General Assembly adopted a U.S.-led resolution on safe, secure, and trustworthy AI intended to appeal to both developed and developing countries. China supported the resolution, then introduced another resolution centered on help for poorer countries and “emphasizing that member states should enjoy equal opportunities” to develop and use AI. At a UN Security Council meeting on AI chaired by the United States in December that year, China’s UN ambassador, Fu Cong, once again counterposed Beijing’s stated interest in cooperation against “discriminatory barriers based on ideological differences.” In thinly veiled terms, Fu was charging that U.S. restrictions on China harmed the developing world.

To win the support of these countries as they built out their AI infrastructure, China needed a bloc of its own. In July 2025, Premier Li Qiang proposed the World Artificial Intelligence Cooperation Organization (WAICO), which promised developing countries technology, training, and a greater say in writing global AI rules. In a speech marking its launch a year later, Chinese leader Xi Jinping called for “openness,” shorthand for access to U.S. chips and the adoption of Chinese open-weight models. After urging countries to take the risks of AI seriously, Xi added that they should, “in the meantime,” jointly oppose “overstretching the national security concept in the field of AI and placing one country’s security over that of others.” Cooperation on risks, in Xi’s telling, was subordinate to overcoming U.S. restrictions.

WAICO has quickly gained standing among its target audience. An early endorsement by UN Secretary-General António Guterres lent it immediate credibility in the eyes of developing countries, and Chinese officials have since promoted WAICO in frequent speeches and in bilateral meetings with countries as varied as Brazil, Pakistan, and Serbia. Beijing has signaled that membership will come with perks. Its draft export controls on leading models, released in October, promise to help WAICO’s 37 members build their AI capabilities, suggesting members could have easier access to models restricted for the rest of the world.

Xi personally led this diplomatic offensive in the lead-up to the Washington summit with U.S. President Donald Trump in September. During a speech in Kyrgyzstan, he pledged to expand a Chinese AI center for the Shanghai Cooperation Organization that helps members build local language models and train engineers. In Cairo, he and Egyptian President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi signed agreements on AI, data centers, and semiconductors, which have become a staple of Xi’s trips abroad. And in New Delhi, Xi proposed an open-source AI zone for BRICS members and told other leaders that a “small yard, high fences” approach would only “box oneself in and divide the world.” AI risks were nowhere on the agenda. The message instead was that U.S. restrictions would isolate Washington, not Beijing.

Much of the allure of China’s pitch rests on how the country’s developers share their technology. Chinese developers release their best models with open weights, which any government or company can download, run on its own hardware, and adapt to its own language. For governments wary of depending on American companies whose exports Washington can restrict, open-weight models promise a greater degree of control. Chinese developers rarely conduct or publish the results of any safety testing for these models, leaving others to discover after release whether, for example, they can be used to launch cyberattacks or build weapons. But their accessibility allows Beijing to argue that any effort to contain the risks of Chinese models is an attempt to deny AI to the developing world.

China’s approach to bilateral AI talks with the United States has deflected discussions of risk just as assiduously as its global AI diplomacy has. In May 2024, the United States and China held talks in Geneva, which I joined as part of the U.S. delegation. The U.S. objective of the talks was to test whether an in-depth technical exchange with China on AI risk was possible. The answer was no. Chinese diplomats steered the discussion away from risks for hours and declined to even explain China’s own AI regulations beyond a superficial overview, despite having technical experts from the relevant agencies in the room.

The Chinese position came through most clearly in the opening and closing remarks of the delegation’s most senior officials. They began with an appeal for cooperation on capacity-building projects in the developing world. They then cast U.S. technology restrictions as an attempt to contain China’s development and the U.S. emphasis on safety as a barrier preventing developing countries from accessing and using AI. The implication was clear—export controls were the primary impediment to cooperation on AI safety. The one concrete outcome, a November 2024 statement in which former President Joe Biden and Xi affirmed “the need to maintain human control over the decision to use nuclear weapons,” asked little of Beijing.

This September, after the summit between Trump and Xi in Washington, China’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs published an “eight-point consensus,” Beijing’s account of what the two sides had agreed to. It described the dialogue as an exchange on the “risks and benefits” of AI. But whereas the White House framed the dialogue as a security issue, listing it alongside nuclear arms control, China’s Ministry of Commerce stressed instead that talks had taken place, and would continue, under the two countries’ economic and trade mechanism. Folded into trade talks, a dialogue on AI risks and benefits leaves Beijing free to parry U.S. requests on risks, such as limits on openly releasing models with dangerous capabilities, by demanding that Washington relax export controls.

Xi did propose to “jointly prevent the misuse and abuse of AI,” conveniently eliding China’s own frequent use of AI against the United States in various ways, such as hacking campaigns and influence operations. He added that AI should remain under human control and be developed for good, a principle Beijing has repeated since 2023 but about which it has offered little further detail or follow-through. More pointed was his insistence that future dialogue should “draw on each other’s strengths, not guard against each other” and his request that Chinese companies be “treated fairly,” both implicit rebukes of U.S. restrictions.

This record suggests Beijing will come to future meetings, beginning with the next discussion in November, with proposals designed to put Washington in a bind. Although U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer has made clear that export controls are not on the table, Beijing will raise them anyway. If Washington engages, Chinese negotiators will claim a standing channel where U.S. restrictions on AI technology are up for negotiation. If it refuses, Beijing will continue to evade U.S. entreaties on safety while accusing Washington of stifling opportunity for developing countries. It hopes that every round of the dialogue will further cement an impression among allies such as Japan and the Netherlands that Washington will trade away restrictions over their heads, and that they should accommodate China’s AI ambitions, too.

But Washington is in a stronger position than its diplomacy has let on. The world’s leading models are still American, and, together with allies, the United States controls key chokepoints in the semiconductor supply chain. Export controls on those chokepoints are not a bargaining chip and should be strengthened. Chinese AI companies regularly lament their lack of high-end chips and have resorted to smuggling chips and illicitly distilling U.S. models. U.S. developers also conduct increasingly extensive and transparent safety testing, which Chinese developers avoid. Taken together, these strengths give Washington the means to press Beijing and an attractive offer for the rest of the world. It should use them, advertising its rigorously tested models to compete for global adoption while pressuring China on safety in the dialogue.

The first chance to apply that pressure is the next bilateral meeting. Washington should set two conditions for it and future meetings. Chinese developers must commit to publishing safety evaluations at least as extensive as those that leading U.S. developers publish today on equivalent benchmarks for the most serious risks, such as cyberattacks and biological weapons, and to disclosing previous and future incidents. The United States can make the same commitments, as its companies already publish such evaluations, increasingly disclose incidents, and open their models to independent and government evaluators before release. Otherwise, the incident channel to which the two sides agreed in September will run one way, with China free to point to U.S. incidents while reporting none of its own.

These conditions would change the terms of the dialogue. Since 2023, Washington has raised risks and Beijing has steered the conversation toward development. As long as Chinese companies face no scrutiny for the risks of their models, Beijing can tout them for their openness and innovation without having to answer questions from the rest of the world about their safety. Concordia AI, which tracks China’s AI ecosystem, found that only five of China’s ten leading developers published safety test results for any model over the past year, and none did so consistently. If China agrees, governments and companies choosing which models to use will be able to judge Chinese and American developers by the same criteria. If China refuses, the onus will be on Beijing to explain why fair treatment for Chinese companies should not extend to safety testing. Regardless, the U.S. government should ramp up its own tests of new Chinese models and publish the results. It should also avoid unforced errors, such as Trump’s waving away of AI safety concerns as a “hoax,” which hand Beijing an easy retort to U.S. appeals.

More broadly, effective AI diplomacy ultimately requires convincing other countries to join the American AI ecosystem. Countries that train their engineers, build their local language models, and run their data centers on Chinese technology will come to depend on it. To counter that dependence (and prevent it from turning into political alignment), Washington needs to present a positive vision of AI governance that pairs verifiable safety commitments—the kind Chinese developers avoid—with the access and investment developing countries want.

U.S. companies already offer the best technology, but Washington has yet to put real diplomatic weight behind them. It should draw from its evolving approach at home to rally partners around shared safety measures. Under the White House Accord on Super Intelligence, signed in September, six leading U.S. companies committed themselves to internal controls, external audits, and independent oversight. The G-7 Code of Conduct, which built on voluntary commitments secured under the Biden administration, could incorporate the accord’s measures and serve as a common standard for partners. Countries that agree to its measures could receive priority for technology and financing. Without such incentives, the AI competition may be determined by price and access alone, where China has the edge.

Much of Washington has treated talks with Beijing as a place to manage the shared risks of AI apart from the competition. China has always treated them as an arena of the larger rivalry. To maintain its lead and make real progress on safety, the United States should start doing the same.

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5939. Nvidia, Spies, and the Art of Economic Espionage

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Q&A: Nvidia, Spies, and the Art of Economic Espionage

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Prefer to listen? To hear this entire conversation, and more episodes in the weeks ahead, follow Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts.

The semiconductor manufacturer Nvidia, which had until recently been the most valuable company in the world, was thrown into turmoil this past week. Company founder and CEO Jensen Huang resigned upon allegations that he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy and subsequently intervened to sell Nvidia chips to her China-tied company, despite U.S. regulations in place to prevent China’s access to the technology. The scandal has put a spotlight on the widespread international practice of economic espionage—and also on the Trump administration’s susceptibility to corruption.

What is the difference between industrial espionage and economic espionage? Is the tech elite particularly vulnerable to this type of spying? And will China be successful at incorporating any AI technologies that it managed to steal?

The semiconductor manufacturer Nvidia, which had until recently been the most valuable company in the world, was thrown into turmoil this past week. Company founder and CEO Jensen Huang resigned upon allegations that he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy and subsequently intervened to sell Nvidia chips to her China-tied company, despite U.S. regulations in place to prevent China’s access to the technology. The scandal has put a spotlight on the widespread international practice of economic espionage—and also on the Trump administration’s susceptibility to corruption.

What is the difference between industrial espionage and economic espionage? Is the tech elite particularly vulnerable to this type of spying? And will China be successful at incorporating any AI technologies that it managed to steal?

Those are just a few of the questions that came up in my recent conversation with FP economics columnist Adam Tooze on the podcast we co-host, Ones and Tooze. What follows is an excerpt, edited for length and clarity. For the full conversation, look for Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts. And check out Adam’s Substack newsletter.

Cameron Abadi: What are the differences exactly between industrial espionage, economic intelligence, and economic espionage?

Adam Tooze: In general understanding, industrial espionage is above all targeted at particular businesses by, more often than not, other businesses but sometimes by governments. And it’s about accessing commercial secrets, business secrets, business strategies, business technologies. And it involves various types of subterfuge or illegal action.

Economic espionage is sort of blurred into that zone, because it also involves various types of subterfuge. But when we say economic espionage, one immediately thinks of governments. Why is that? I think because when we think of businesses, we think of private economic activity and profit. But when we use the word “economy,” we immediately start thinking about GDP and national interest. And so, the sorts of information that are being targeted there might be more to do with technologies of general strategic interest. So that will be different from industrial espionage or purely commercial espionage.

And then an example of economic intelligence would be, say, wartime efforts on the part of the British who are aiming a strategic bombing program against Germany and therefore need to understand how the German economy functions and built an economics intelligence wing of the British military war effort so as to organize that. And that ranged from people who were architectural historians who knew which cities would be inflammable if you dropped incendiary bombs on them all the way through to an understanding of the input-output diagram, the supply chains.

And it involves, more often than not, open-source information. It doesn’t involve any subterfuge at all. And so economic intelligence kind of blurs into the world that we’re in, of economic reporting, of newsletters, of regular updates. The Economist magazine has what it used to call the Economist Intelligence Unit, which was presumably very well-connected journalists doing studies.

And I think the allegation in the Nvidia case sits most squarely in the domain of economic espionage. Yes, it targets a particular business and the top man in that particular business, the charismatic figurehead, indeed, of the entire hardware side of AI. So it would seem that it sits somewhere on that boundary.

I don’t think the allegation, however, is that Jensen Huang’s lover was doing this with a view to commercially profiting herself. I think the suggestion is that it’s MSS, the Ministry of State Security, which is doing this on behalf of the Chinese state.

CA: Jensen Huang is said to have fallen prey to a honey-trap method of espionage, in which a spy creates a sexual relationship to later exploit it. Is our current economic elite in tech companies particularly susceptible to this form of espionage?

AT: It’s impossible not to speculate about the ego makeup of Silicon Valley engineers. It wouldn’t, after all, be surprising if there was a desire for compensation for a whole variety of inhibitions, which no doubt are introduced at various stages in many people’s careers during their extraordinary efforts in very recondite zones of technical expertise. So, the nerd gone wild at the head of a company, the loneliness of people in that kind of position, all of this, I think, no doubt plays a role.

But then I was thinking, actually sociologically, and I came to this strange realization, because I spend quite a lot of time in hotels because of this weird existence that I have. And I was thinking to myself, if a senior soldier goes to a hotel, on the face of it, you’re kind of wondering what the senior soldier is doing in a hotel, right? If a senior civil servant, pretty much whichever government they work for, goes to a really glamorous hotel, again, you’d be asking yourself, how can they afford that? That’s not really where they belong, right?

So suddenly, you realize that the whole zone of high-end, expensive, relatively exclusive hospitality that presumably provides the most attractive zone for this kind of entrapment, the actors for whom it arouses zero legitimacy issues, zero need for explanation at all, in fact it’s completely normal and taken for granted, are high-level business figures.

In fact, the entire conference circuit, where people stay, the blending in those spaces of leisure and business activity, including pools and gyms and eating and drinking as a matter of course, in fact, isa kind of mandate for many businesses. I’ve gone to a lot of these investor conferences, and it’s quite clear that networking is an absolutely required option, right? There’s no excuse for just withdrawing to your room and getting on with your life, which I’d want to do most of the time.

And so presumably, this is precisely the world in which this works. I mean, business people systematically expect to be wined and dined. They expect to be courted. They constantly struggle to draw the line between reasonable inducements and bribery. That is an essential part of their business. And many of them, of course, have very strict codes of ethics. But it’s a matter of record that those shift all the time.

And they’re much more like, in that sense, the Chinese Communist Party than they are like, you know, conventional civil service jobs in the West, where it’s just quite clear that accepting any kind of gift in any form is just off-limits. No one in the civil service is regularly invited to glamorous parties where there’s a mixture of more or less formally clad, in some cases scantily clad, different types of interaction. And so it’s a kind of recipe for stress on this fault line, I would have thought. And it’s purely sociological, it’s not a matter of psychology at all.

CA: There’s clearly a distinction between pursuing the acquisition of technologies through espionage of this kind versus productively absorbing that technology upon having acquired it. How does that distinction apply right now to China?

AT: I think this is the whole game, actually. The real reason to be worried is that China has a demonstrated and extraordinary capacity to incorporate. In other words, it’s a near-peer. And that’s precisely what makes it so alarming to Western security officials, is that there’s absolutely every reason to think they can, yeah. And furthermore, leapfrog, even. Give them half a chance and there’s every single reason to think that they can generalize it, make it cheaper, speed it up.

There are differences, obviously, because what Nvidia does or what Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company does or what the lithograph makers do is literally special source. Like, no one has ever done that anywhere, ever, in the entire world in the history of humanity. So whether you can replicate that is an open question. But that’s presumably what the Chinese aren’t going to do anyway. They’re going to make their own version that’s more suitable to their circumstances.

And it’s critical to know—and we don’t know what’s actually happened in this case—but there are certain critical problems that it’s interesting to know how other people solve them, because it helps you to think about how you should solve them in your own terms. And that is why I think the Chinese case is so significant, because they have a demonstrated capacity to do that. And it really varies—in regard to transport engineering, cars, railways, solar, batteries, there has already been hugely successful leapfrogging; the game is over. With regard to chips, it’s a slightly different story, and I think that speaks to the fact that the technological demands are just so mind-blowingly high.

But that does contrast very significantly with the Soviet Union, which at different periods in its history demonstrated greater- and lesser-degree capacity to incorporate technologies that it acquired through espionage. So around the atomic bomb program, there was a quite a high capacity to incorporate. Then of course in rocketry, they become world-leading. And they do that like the Americans by capturing German rocket scientists and then incorporating their knowledge into what was already a high-functioning military industrial system in the Soviet Union that was wasteful of people’s lives but was generative of a whole series of incredibly innovative, very, very powerful and effective weapons.

But more generally, it’s a remarkable instance of just how little can get transferred, because after all, the Soviet Union was the beneficiary, after ‘45, of a huge wave—this is the untold story of German reparations after World War II, absolutely gigantic, and they take the form principally of dismantling. So vast amounts of German equipment are shipped to the Soviet Union where, I don’t think it’s merely apocryphal to say, an awful lot of it just rusted and was not fully incorporated into the Soviet system.

Now, in earlier stages, they had done much better. So in the ‘30s, the Stalinist industrialization program is heavily dependent on American expertise and on German expertise funded in part by export guarantees from the Weimar Republic that helped to fund this flow.

So it wasn’t consistently rubbish at incorporating, but it certainly wasn’t the powerhouse of creative imitation, adaptation, modification, and improvement that China is, to an extent that nowhere else in the world can match, right?

That’s the extraordinary thing. They can iterate, they can modify, they could incorporate at a pace that no one else in the world—in almost all areas of production—no one else can match. And so, then if you feed that with privileged insider information, then you’d expect really large-scale effects.

Cameron Abadi is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. X: @CameronAbadi

Adam Tooze is a columnist at Foreign Policy and a history professor and the director of the European Institute at Columbia University. He is the author of Chartbook, a newsletter on economics, geopolitics, and history. X: @adam_tooze

From the Iran war to boat strikes to Ukraine, Democrats now have powerful leverage.

#5938. Trump Is Facing a Very Different North Korea

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

STEPHEN E. BIEGUN was Deputy Secretary of State from December 2019 to January 2021 and Special Representative for North Korea from 2018 to 2021. He is a Distinguished Fellow at the Asia Society Policy Institute.

EMMA CHANLETT-AVERY is Senior Director for Political-Security Affairs and Deputy Director of the Washington office of the Asia Society Policy Institute.

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

Donald Trump has Pyongyang on his mind. In recent months, the U.S. president has made comments to the media suggesting he is in communication with North Korean leader Kim Jong Un. In an August 17 Truth Social post, for instance, Trump announced that the United States would scale back joint military drills with South Korea. The decision, he wrote, was “based on my very good relationship with Kim Jong Un,” describing the dictator as “unthreatening and respectful.”

Trump has not been subtle about his desire to restart diplomacy with Kim. With an eye on his legacy and the war with Iran stalemated, he is eager to notch a foreign policy win. During his first term, Trump held two historic U.S.–North Korean summits aimed at denuclearizing the Korean Peninsula—but both foundered because of the countries’ disparate expectations for the terms of a deal. Since his reelection, Trump has been looking to reopen talks with Kim. The ball is now in Pyongyang’s court, and Kim has signaled, if not an eagerness for renewed engagement, at least an unwillingness to rule it out. In August, after Trump insisted for the second time that he was in touch with Pyongyang, Kim Yo Jong—the leader’s influential sister—released a statement that read: “Regarding the reports from Washington about recent communication between the top leaders of North Korea and the U.S., I am completely unaware of such matters. It has been stated on numerous occasions that our head of state holds fond personal memories and positive feelings toward Trump. The relationship between the leaders remains excellent.” Although this is far from an agreement to meet, Kim Jong Un is not closing the door.

If leader-to-leader talks materialize, Trump may believe his personal rapport with Kim will yield a deal. But he will be facing a very different North Korea than the one at their last meeting in 2019. The fallout from unfinished diplomacy during Trump’s first term, the COVID-19 pandemic, and global trends such as the sharp escalation in U.S.-Chinese strategic rivalry and Russia’s invasion of Ukraine have destabilized the security landscape and created formidable obstacles to peaceful engagement. Meanwhile, North Korea’s continued nuclear and conventional military expansion and the growing support it is receiving from Moscow have strengthened Kim’s hand. Facing an emboldened Pyongyang, Trump can no longer fall back on the same concessions and negotiation tactics he used in previous talks.

For a U.S.–North Korean summit to be successful, the United States must approach it with well-defined strategic goals and a clear understanding of what concessions it is willing to make. Washington should shift its focus away from denuclearization, at least in the short term, to concentrate on more achievable nuclear arms control and safety measures. The United States should also maintain a capable troop presence on the Korean Peninsula and reiterate its security assurances—particularly its nuclear-deterrence guarantees—to regional allies such as South Korea and Japan.

Although diplomacy with North Korea has historically been difficult, Trump is eager to meet with Kim, and both sides want to avoid pushing the Indo-Pacific toward nuclear proliferation or all-out war. Careful engagement with Pyongyang could defuse tensions on the Korean Peninsula and lay the groundwork for a more stable security landscape.

Since the U.S.–North Korean summit in 2019, the conflicts in Ukraine and Iran have reshaped the Indo-Pacific in ways that strengthen Kim’s hand. The U.S.-Israeli war with Iran has drawn Washington’s focus away from Asia as, for the third time in the past quarter century, U.S. strategic attention and military assets have surged to the Middle East. The last American aircraft carrier stationed in the Indo-Pacific departed for the Persian Gulf in August, and U.S. strikes on Iran have rapidly depleted American munitions stockpiles. Washington’s Indo-Pacific allies, already strained by Trump’s abrupt policy shifts and punishing tariffs, are now concerned about deteriorating deterrence. Asian economies—many of which are deeply dependent on Middle Eastern energy supplies—have contracted as the Strait of Hormuz has remained compromised. Amid signs of further American retrenchment, U.S. allies and partners are increasingly skeptical about Washington’s commitment to the region.

Even more consequential is Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, which has benefited North Korea in numerous ways. Moscow and Pyongyang’s once dormant relationship has flourished as Kim has sent thousands of munitions to replenish Russia’s depleted stockpiles and thousands of soldiers to fight and die in the ongoing ground war. Images of the new memorial to the Korean People’s Army soldiers who have perished in the invasion suggest an already significant loss of North Korean lives. In return, Pyongyang has received generous assistance from Moscow in violation of international sanctions, including abundant financial support for Kim’s regime and technology transfers that have enhanced North Korean military capabilities and may help Pyongyang more accurately deliver intercontinental ballistic missiles aimed at the United States. Russian patronage allows Kim to shower his regime’s elites with the economic benefits needed to cement their loyalty.

Still, it is worth questioning how durable this partnership is. After more than four years of grinding warfare with no end in sight, Moscow is increasingly isolated and may be in a poor position to continue supporting Pyongyang. Russia’s preoccupation with Ukraine has allowed China to expand its presence in Central Asia, for instance, and Turkey to deepen its influence in Azerbaijan. Russian aggression has spurred Armenia and Moldova to look westward, and hundreds of thousands of young Russian men have fled the country to avoid military conscription while over a thousand Western companies have left. International sanctions are starving Russia of badly needed capital and technology, and Russian war policies have created huge imbalances and inflation, possibly with worse to come for the Russian economy. If North Korea continues to actively support Moscow in this war, and Russia fails or is defeated, the world will become a much more hostile place for Pyongyang.

Russia and North Korea’s deepening ties have already begun to reshape China’s interests in the region. Beijing is still Pyongyang’s most important partner, but the dynamic has changed considerably in recent years. Beijing previously saw denuclearization as a top priority on the Korean Peninsula—a shared objective, and at times a rare area of cooperation, with the United States. It disliked the destabilizing impact that North Korea’s pursuit of nuclear weapons had on tensions between the North and South and expended political capital responding to international condemnation of Pyongyang. Between 2006 and 2017, Beijing agreed to a series of UN Security Council resolutions that imposed sanctions and other restrictions on Pyongyang in response to six North Korean nuclear tests. Now, pressed to maintain its influence in Pyongyang amid a growing Russian–North Korean security relationship—and with the rest of the world distracted by other geopolitical crises—China has gone silent on the issue. When Chinese leader Xi Jinping visited North Korea in June to reassert his influence, denuclearization was not publicly mentioned. China’s new approach will make U.S. engagement on this issue more challenging and risks driving other regional powers, such as South Korea and Japan, to reconsider their own policies on the deployment or development of nuclear weapons.

Whether Pyongyang reengages with the United States may also depend on how much North Korea relies on China for economic support. Much of North Korean commerce passes through China, and although Beijing and Pyongyang’s relationship appears fairly stable, it remains wary and transactional, with each side skeptical of the other’s motives. North Korea may seek to reengage with the United States to counterbalance its overdependence on China, or as a hedge against Russia’s failure in its war with Ukraine. Conversely, as Trump and Xi upgrade and stabilize U.S.-Chinese relations, China could step in to broker the next Trump-Kim meeting.

The Kim dynasty has long vexed U.S. policymakers. Washington has sought multiple deals with North Korea, including during Trump’s first-term summits with Kim, but missteps and mistrust on both sides derailed them. Pyongyang has proved resilient to both U.S. and international sanctions. Indeed, during the COVID-19 pandemic, North Korea sealed its borders and deprived itself far more severely than sanctions could, yet it still survived. U.S. congressional interventions on human rights and humanitarian aid, meanwhile, have mostly failed to change North Korea’s behavior. Pyongyang has also cushioned the impact of sanctions by engaging in cybertheft, such as its $6.3 billion heist against the cryptocurrency exchange Binance this October. All the while, Pyongyang has steadily advanced its military capabilities and expanded its nuclear arsenal.

Although Washington’s previous attempts to rein in North Korea have failed, Trump’s renewed eagerness to meet with Kim and his unorthodox approach to foreign policy could provide a new opening for diplomacy. Trump has cultivated a direct relationship with Kim, shunning the bureaucratic negotiations that usually precede a deal. While a performative summit alone would be of little benefit to U.S. interests, Washington could use reengagement with Pyongyang to thaw inter-Korean relations and stabilize the peninsula by slowing or freezing North Korea’s nuclear buildup. Those goals may seem conventional, but as regional tensions worsen and Pyongyang’s nuclear arsenal grows, they are essential to averting potentially catastrophic conflict.

Three questions cast a shadow over the possibility of renewed engagement between the United States and North Korea. First is the specific purpose of restarting engagement: What is the goal? Second is how to reopen communications. Despite Trump’s claims, there is no real proof that he has recently interacted with Kim. All evidence suggests the last contact between American and North Korean officials was in 2019, and it may be challenging to break a seven-year drought. Third is what are the incentives and tradeoffs the United States would be willing to consider as part of a negotiation—the same issue that bedeviled U.S. and North Korean officials in Hanoi.

One possible formula for progress is focusing, at least temporarily, on steady arms control and international safety controls rather than the elusive goal of denuclearization. Denuclearization should remain Washington’s ultimate aspiration. But in the meantime, a North Korea awash with even more nuclear weapons makes the world only more dangerous. This approach will require serious diplomacy to minimize second-order consequences, particularly to avoid weakening U.S. alliances in the Indo-Pacific or triggering a collapse of the nuclear nonproliferation regime among nations that might see their only recourse as acquiring their own nuclear weapons. China, and perhaps even Russia, may concur with Washington on this point, and a shared desire to avoid more nuclear states in East Asia could help restart engagement.

Kim has substantially more leverage now than in earlier encounters with Trump. Reopening communication will require using a mix of positive and negative assurances to convince him that he is unlikely to get a better chance to strike a deal. Kim may press the United States to scale back aspects of its cooperation with South Korea—such as joint military exercises and support for Seoul’s pursuit of nuclear-powered submarines—as preconditions for a meeting with Trump. Kim may even propose his own assurances of peace on the peninsula in exchange for a reduction of U.S. military forces in South Korea, although it is unclear whether Trump would agree to such conditions.

In the worst-case scenario, Kim may take advantage of Trump’s eagerness to make a deal to extract weighty concessions from the United States. Kim has little reason to meet with Trump now, unless he calculates that he can secure major terms such as de facto recognition as a nuclear state, reduced sanctions, or a complete withdrawal of U.S. troops from the peninsula. Kim would likely insist that there be no discussion of denuclearization, North Korea’s support for the invasion of Ukraine, Pyongyang’s human rights abuses, or a final accounting for Japanese citizens kidnapped by North Korea under the rule of Kim Il Sung. Granting such concessions would end the U.S.–South Korean alliance.

To ensure North Korea does not exploit the Trump administration, Washington must carefully consider what tradeoffs it is willing to make. Lifting sanctions, abandoning or delaying the denuclearization process, temporarily accepting North Korea as a nuclear state, and reducing the U.S. military presence in South Korea are all long-standing demands of Kim’s regime that are likely on the table. Although lifting international sanctions would require UN Security Council consensus, Trump may be willing to reduce unilateral U.S. sanctions, which he considers an obstacle to better bilateral relations and which have been ineffective in stemming Pyongyang’s nuclear advances.

Although denuclearization has been a major talking point for U.S. administrations since the early 1990s, the Trump administration may need to set it aside, at least in the short term. Pyongyang’s nuclear capabilities have increased since Trump’s 2018 and 2019 summits with Kim, suggesting that the stalemate caused by continued U.S. demands for the full-scale dismantlement of North Korea’s nuclear program may have given Kim time and space to grow his arsenal. Even if denuclearization remains a long-term U.S. priority—and it should—pragmatism is essential to reducing the threat posed by Kim’s regime. In exchange for stepping away from denuclearization, Trump should demand that Kim freeze any further expansion of his arsenal and explore possibilities for greater strategic stability, including renewed inter-Korean dialogue, transparency and confidence-building efforts, security measures that bolster nuclear facilities’ safety and guard against accidents, and strict adherence to nonproliferation. Trump must make it clear to Kim that an unconstrained expansion of North Korea’s nuclear arsenal will pressure South Korea and Japan to deploy nuclear weapons on their territory and possibly develop their own.

Regardless of Kim’s demands, the United States must preserve its troop presence on the Korean Peninsula and continue joint military exercises with South Korea as long as the threat of North Korean aggression persists. The mantra of U.S. troops in South Korea is “ready to fight tonight.” Joint military exercises keep U.S. and South Korean forces prepared for combat, which is essential to deterring North Korean attacks. It would be dangerous and irresponsible to send American soldiers, sailors, marines, and airmen to the peninsula without ensuring peak readiness. Instead of withdrawing troops, the United States should offer confidence-building measures to North Korea—practices that reduce the risk of conflict breaking out because of misjudgment. These could include military-to-military engagement and security reassurances among the United States, South Korea, and North Korea, or greater transparency around military exercises—allowing observers on a reciprocal basis, for example. Sustaining Washington’s alliance with South Korea and avoiding cascading nuclear proliferation are critical to U.S. interests in the region. American negotiators must remind Kim that the U.S. military presence and its extended nuclear-deterrence guarantees for Seoul and Tokyo hold back even larger conventional military—and possibly nuclear—buildups in the region.

Finally, North Korea and the United States—and perhaps, in time, South Korea and Japan—should sustain diplomatic engagement by agreeing to assign diplomats to each other’s capitals through reciprocal liaison offices, which would maintain relations without granting full diplomatic recognition. In 2018 and 2019, the two sides discussed housing such diplomats in the German Embassy in Pyongyang and the Swedish Embassy in Washington. Liaison offices would also stabilize and prolong the communication necessary to quietly work through complex negotiations.

If North Korea rejects Trump’s overtures, the United States is not without options. Washington could pressure the Kim regime by intensifying the scale and tempo of joint military exercises with South Korea; deploying American armaments, including short-range ballistic missiles, cruise missiles, and possibly nuclear weapons, to South Korea and Japan; and pursuing a new campaign to choke off North Korea’s economic resources through sanctions and interdiction. The United States could also deploy additional air defense assets to Japan and South Korea, including THAAD air defense radar systems and interceptors. Washington and its allies could dramatically expand efforts to undermine Kim’s rule by mounting sustained criticisms of North Korea’s dangerously large nuclear weapons arsenal and its use of international criminal enterprise to steal from and economically defraud other nations, which could spur other countries to curb their material or diplomatic support for North Korea or more aggressively sanction Kim’s regime.

Beyond short-term negotiations, a fourth question is less complicated but just as important: What role should Japan and South Korea play in U.S. engagements with North Korea? Many past administrations, both Republican and Democratic, have considered coordination with allies essential to establishing a durable solution on the Korean Peninsula. But Trump’s antipathy toward alliances suggests close consultation with South Korea and Japan is unlikely. Their absence from the negotiating table may actually accelerate U.S.–North Korean engagement, because coordination across allied governments can be unwieldy and slow, and Pyongyang has not been receptive to past overtures by Seoul and Tokyo. Although South Korean President Lee Jae-myung and Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi both rhetorically back engagement with North Korea, Pyongyang has bluntly rejected their outreach.

Even if Seoul and Tokyo are not directly engaged in U.S.–North Korean diplomacy, they would still likely welcome a U.S.-led deal with Kim if it protects their core security interests. But a deal with Kim that ignores American allies’ concerns would be dangerous. South Korea harbors an appetite to develop its own nuclear arsenal, particularly if it feels U.S. security protections are receding. Should Seoul move in that direction, Tokyo would almost certainly reconsider its own nonnuclear status, potentially igniting a nuclear arms race in Asia. Given North Korea’s long-standing hostility toward both Japan and South Korea, proliferation would destabilize the Indo-Pacific and leave the global nonproliferation regime in tatters.

Any deal, even one that addresses Seoul’s and Tokyo’s core interests, will likely need to be paired with American security reassurances to allies’ governments and publics. Japan and South Korea must continue building their own conventional defense capabilities while the United States sustains its commitment to defending its Asian allies—especially extended nuclear deterrence. The United States must pursue delicate, nuanced diplomacy to avoid creating a stronger, legitimized North Korea; straining its alliances with Seoul and Tokyo; and debilitating the international nonproliferation regime.

Despite the risks of U.S.–North Korean engagement, the time may be ripe to break the diplomatic paralysis that has allowed Pyongyang to advance its military capabilities for so long. The last two years of Trump’s presidency—particularly his summitry calendar in late 2026—will bring him to Asia, potentially facilitating a meeting with Kim. Such a visit would present the best chance for the kind of performative, high-profile meeting that both Trump and Kim favor.

Trump is the ultimate iconoclast in traditional great-power politics, as evidenced by his shifting relationships with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin, which could shape Washington’s engagement with Pyongyang. Still, there may be a way to leverage Trump’s affection for Kim, and Kim’s recent restraint in criticizing the United States, to outline a deal that arrests the growing threat of North Korean nuclear weapons.

The bruising Republican defeat in the U.S. midterm elections could also change North Korea’s diplomatic calculus, especially because it suggests Trump’s political standing in the United States has faltered, and he may struggle to deliver the results Kim seeks. Trump may force the issue, however, and propose a meeting with Kim after the November Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation Summit in Shenzhen, China. It would not be surprising for Trump to suggest that this meeting take place in Pyongyang, putting Kim on the spot. If leader-to-leader talks occur, both sides should remember the central lesson from Trump’s first-term summits: engagement without sufficient preparation and clearly defined goals is not enough to strengthen security and stability on the Korean Peninsula.

Growing tensions in the Indo-Pacific raise the prospect of a much darker future, where conditions on the Korean Peninsula remain unchanged and Asia’s entanglements in European and Middle Eastern conflicts metastasize into a new world war—perhaps ignited in Taiwan, the South China Sea, or the Korean Peninsula. Even if no war breaks out, in just a few years the United States could find itself in a world where Asia’s five current nuclear states are joined by two, three, or more new players.

But this future is not inevitable. All the leaders engaged in North Korean security debates—from Lee and Takaichi to Xi and Putin—want to avoid such escalation. Even the North Koreans surely recognize this is the most dangerous path. This shared goal means diplomacy is still possible, despite formidable challenges. For all Trump’s idiosyncrasies, one should take his desire to peacefully end tensions on the Korean Peninsula at face value. He has made his intentions clear. What happens next is up to Chairman Kim.
