#5936. Schumer's former ally mounts leadership challenge as Democrats face rare internal power struggle

Fox News senior congressional correspondent Chad Pergram joins 'Fox & Friends' as Maryland Sen. Chris Van Hollen mounts a challenge against New York Sen. Chuck Schumer for Senate Democratic leadership.

Senate Democrats are getting an opportunity they haven’t had in decades: a different option for who their leader could be.

Even before the blue wave crashed over Republicans and Democrats emerged with a commanding majority in the Senate, the rumblings for Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., to step aside were there.

Now, he’s got a challenger in Sen. Chris Van Hollen, D-Md., a familiar face in Washington, D.C., who framed his bid against Schumer as one answering the call for change voters demanded. It’ll be the first time that a Senate Democratic leadership race will be contested since 1994.

DEMOCRATS TURN ON SCHUMER AFTER HISTORIC ELECTION WINS AS POTENTIAL CHALLENGER EMERGES

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer arrives at the U.S. Capitol Building in Washington, D.C., on Nov. 9, 2026. Newly elected members of the 120th Congress arrive for the first day of new member orientation after Democrats flipped both chambers in the midterm elections. (Getty Images)

Van Hollen’s emergence, at 67, as the lone challenger to Schumer, 75, comes after lawmakers and candidates demanded a change in Democratic leadership before the election. That call has only intensified in the week after Democrats won the midterms, with Van Hollen interpreting the seismic political shift as an indictment of President Donald Trump that can only be acted on if Senate Democrats go in a new direction.

The challenge is already dividing Senate Democrats.

In an interview with MS NOW, Van Hollen didn’t offer a sharp policy contrast to Schumer, and cast doubt on Democrats’ ability to pass key progressive desires, like Medicare for All.

"But it should be very clear: While, you know, voters have rejected the Trump policies, they’ve not yet hired Democrats — they’ve given us a chance, we have to earn their trust," Van Hollen said. "My view is that you can’t earn that trust with old leadership. You need to move on to new leadership."

Van Hollen acknowledged that he was an "underdog," but revealed during the interview that he had over 10 Senate Democrats in his corner and believed more were to come. Right now, either lawmaker will need 28 secret ballot votes to win the job — that could bump up to 29 if Democrats flip Alaska, where ballots are still being counted in the Senate race between Democratic nominee Mary Peltola and incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan. And it could get messy over the next few weeks before the early December, closed-door election.

And he’s got the backing of the progressive standard-bearer in the Senate: Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., and new faces of the progressive movement, like Sens.-elect Abdul El-Sayed, who Van Hollen endorsed early on in his campaign, and Peggy Flanagan, who said on X that Van Hollen was the "leader and fighter we need to bring about real change, so no American is left behind."

VAN HOLLEN CHALLENGES SCHUMER FOR SENATE LEADERSHIP AFTER DEMOCRATIC ELECTION SWEEP

Ultimately, either candidate's success will likely break down on how well they both work with and push back against President Donald Trump.

Early in Trump’s second term, Van Hollen won praise from progressives when he traveled to El Salvador for a welfare check on Kilmar Abrego Garcia, a Maryland resident who was mistakenly deported by the administration.

But Schumer has dealt with Trump for years, and told the Washington Sun that his strategy heading into the new Congress will be to put bill after bill on the floor, like restoring Obamacare subsidies or banning insider trading, and dare Republicans to vote them down — Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., used a similar strategy over the last two years.

"In a certain sense, it’s a win-win. I’d rather get things done, but it’s not going to stop us," Schumer said. "We are going to move forward with or without Trump, with or without the Republicans."

Despite the demands for change, Van Hollen is very much a creature of Washington, having served in Congress since 2003.

He’s the son of diplomats, and served in House Democratic leadership under former House Speaker Nancy Pelosi. He was chair of the Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee, the House Democrats’ campaign arm, for two cycles, and was quickly tapped by Schumer to lead the Senate’s campaign operation when he ascended to the upper chamber in 2017.

CHUCK SCHUMER AFTER SWEEPING DEMOCRATIC MIDTERM VICTORIES: 'I'LL BE RE-ELECTED AS MAJORITY LEADER'

Sen. Charles Schumer, D-N.Y., and Rep. Chris Van Hollen, D-Md., speak to the media during a news conference on Capitol Hill on Feb. 11, 2010, in Washington, D.C. The lawmakers were set to introduce a bill aimed at offsetting a recent Supreme Court ruling that allowed unlimited spending by corporations and unions during elections. (Getty Images)

And he spent his entire career in the Senate behind Schumer, who first became leader in 2017, and he has rarely broken with him on key votes. He has, however, emerged as a staunch critic of Israel and criticized Schumer’s ties to the crypto industry and meddling in Democratic primaries.

He currently serves on four committees in the Senate: Appropriations, Foreign Relations, Budget and Banking, Housing and Urban Affairs, and throughout his career in Congress has focused on budget issues and foreign affairs.

Still, outside progressive groups are beginning to back him.

The progressive Sunrise Movement is all in on Van Hollen. The organization announced its support for the lawmaker on Bluesky, and wrote, "Young people voted for change on election night."

"Chuck Schumer can’t give us that, but Chris Van Hollen might," they said. "The truth is, the ‘blue tsunami’ could easily become the blue mirage in the wrong hands. And Schumer’s hands are too full of billionaires’ money to hold young people’s future."

Another progressive group, the 50501 protest movement, also declared its support for Van Hollen Thursday night in a post on Bluesky, urging voters to pressure their senators to "vote Chris, NOT CHUCK."

There are a bevy of other outside groups that have opposed Schumer, including Indivisible, MoveOn and the Working Families Party, and the 600,000-member International Association of Machinists and Aerospace Workers sent the top Democrat a letter demanding he step aside.

But Schumer has remained confident about retaining his position.

"I welcome any discussion about the future of our caucus and the important work ahead," Schumer said on X. "I’m proud of what we’ve accomplished together—55 seats and counting is an amazing result—and I have the votes to continue serving as Democratic Leader."

His allies have leaned into sports metaphors to describe why he should keep the job, with both Sens. Angus King, I-Maine, and Ruben Gallego, D-Ariz., comparing it to a successful manager in baseball.

"When the manager takes you to the World Series, you give them another contract," Gallego said.Fox News Digital reached out for comment from both Schumer and Van Hollen.

Alex Miller is a writer for Fox News Digital covering the U.S. Senate.

#5933. How Lebanon Became the Middle East’s Battleground

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: How Lebanon Became the Middle East’s Battleground

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In mid-July 2006, NPR’s foreign editor dispatched me to Beirut. Israel and Hezbollah were waging an all-out war after militants launched a cross-border attack from Lebanon, killing eight Israeli soldiers and capturing two more. Israeli bombs pounded Lebanon’s ports, roads, and bridges, while Hezbollah rained missiles down on northern Israel. Believing his country had the upper hand, Israeli Prime Minister Ehud Olmert wanted to finish off Hezbollah once and for all and had convinced the George W. Bush administration to hold off calling for a ceasefire.

I was in my hotel room working on a story with a Lebanese translator when then-U.S. Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice appeared on TV, suggesting the conflict was an opportunity to remake the region. She added that the attacks—which had already led to more than 300 Lebanese deaths and would go on to kill over 1,000 people in Lebanon and 121 Israeli soldiers—were the “birth pangs of a new Middle East.”

In mid-July 2006, NPR’s foreign editor dispatched me to Beirut. Israel and Hezbollah were waging an all-out war after militants launched a cross-border attack from Lebanon, killing eight Israeli soldiers and capturing two more. Israeli bombs pounded Lebanon’s ports, roads, and bridges, while Hezbollah rained missiles down on northern Israel. Believing his country had the upper hand, Israeli Prime Minister Ehud Olmert wanted to finish off Hezbollah once and for all and had convinced the George W. Bush administration to hold off calling for a ceasefire.

I was in my hotel room working on a story with a Lebanese translator when then-U.S. Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice appeared on TV, suggesting the conflict was an opportunity to remake the region. She added that the attacks—which had already led to more than 300 Lebanese deaths and would go on to kill over 1,000 people in Lebanon and 121 Israeli soldiers—were the “birth pangs of a new Middle East.”

The translator looked up from her computer and turned to me. “Birth pangs?” she asked, shaking her head. “Why does Lebanon always have to be the midwife?”

The Best Kind of American: A True Story of Murder, War, and America’s Undoing in the Middle East, Kim Ghattas, Henry Holt and Co., 496 pp., $32.99, October 2026

For five decades, Lebanon has been a key Middle East battleground, caught in the geopolitical crosshairs of Israel, Iran, Syria, and the United States competing to shape or control the broader region. In her new book The Best Kind of American: A True Story of Murder, War, and America’s Undoing in the Middle East, Kim Ghattas contends that many of the policies and conflicts playing out across the region today were established in Lebanon during the 1980s, particularly Israel’s 1982 invasion. It’s a prescient argument given where the Middle East now finds itself, with Israel continuing to vie for security and land, Iran using Hezbollah to project power, and Lebanon remaining a fragile state.

Ghattas, a Lebanon-born journalist and commentator, unfolds the story of the country’s turbulence around the 1984 murder of Malcolm Kerr, a U.S. scholar of the Middle East and president of the American University of Beirut who was gunned down outside his office. The book begins with an email from Kerr’s widow, Ann, asking Ghattas if she had discovered new information about his assassination while writing an earlier book. Ann had clues about who had killed her husband, but no clarity as to why.

Kerr was born and spent most of his childhood in Lebanon. Ghattas portrays him as an Arabist with a deep knowledge of the Middle East and an intuitive understanding of the role of religious fanaticism in the region. This was in striking contrast to successive U.S. administrations that, Ghattas writes, have been out-maneuvered time and again by Iran, Israel, and Syria.

Lebanon was embroiled in a brutal civil war in the 1980s, which provides a vivid backdrop for Ghattas’ search to identify Kerr’s killer and the reason for his death. Regional powers pursued their own deadly agendas in the country, while Israeli tanks swept through southern Lebanon and did not stop until they reached Beirut. Kerr’s death was considered just one of many wartime losses and thus was not investigated. The book provides rich, well-researched profiles of a patchwork of deadly rivals—militants, religious fanatics, assassins, and governments—operating in Lebanon during this time. These included the Palestine Liberation Organization (PLO), which had been based in the country since the early 1970s; the pro-Iranian Islamic Amal militia; and Syrian President Hafez al-Assad and his son and successor, Bashar, who brutally controlled Lebanon for decades.

Ghattas also humanizes a wide cast of characters affected by the hijackings, car bombs, hostage taking, and civil war that marked the era: a wife searching for her husband who was kidnapped, a celebrated Lebanese diva refusing to sing until the killings stopped, and the agony of those who lost loved ones, including the Kerr family.

One of the more striking consequences of the 1982 war was that it led to the emergence of Hezbollah, one of Iran’s most powerful proxies. That June, Israel invaded Lebanon in a bid to dislodge Yasser Arafat and his PLO, which had been launching sporadic attacks against Israel. At the same time, Iran’s new Islamist regime sent several hundred fighters and volunteers, along with a handful of clerics, to Lebanon via Syria. They set up training camps for local Shias around Baalbek and other areas in the Beqaa Valley. More fighters and paramilitary forces arrived, taking up the name Hezbollah, or Party of God.

Initially, Hafez al-Assad was cautious about allowing Iran to funnel troops and weapons through Syria, even though the two countries had become allies after the 1979 Iranian revolution. But Assad came to view Hezbollah as a powerful deterrent against Israel and a way to help consolidate his grip on Lebanon.

What a scandal involving movie star Alain Delon can—and can’t—tell us about French society.

In a new book, Sally Hayden considers how journalists can puncture the detachment of the privileged.

Carlos the Jackal left behind a violent, celebrity-obsessed world.

In Ghattas’ telling, the Ronald Reagan administration made a strategic mistake by acquiescing to the 1982 invasion. It was sold to the United States by then-Israeli Defense Minister Ariel Sharon as a limited operation to remove the PLO from Lebanon, not the broad incursion into Beirut it turned out to be. There are remarkable moments in the book highlighting Israeli sway over Washington. The U.S. ambassador to Lebanon, Robert Dillon, urgently called the State Department to say he was watching Israeli tanks and troops moving into Beirut, only to be told by the switchboard operator that Tel Aviv had given its assurances that the Israeli military was not there. A few weeks later, Philip Habib, Reagan’s special Middle East envoy, rang Washington to say Israel had broken a ceasefire and was pounding Beirut. When Washington told him Israel denied that claim, Habib held his phone out the window as two tanks fired off rounds, thereby making his case.

The United States was pulled into the Lebanon morass to help control the blowback from the Israeli invasion, which the White House worried could spin into another Arab-Israeli war. Washington deployed Marines in August 1982 as peacekeepers to help oversee the withdrawal of the PLO from Lebanon to Tunisia, and to control fighting between various Lebanese factions. A second deployment of Marines came after the massacre by Lebanon’s Israeli-backed Phalange militia in the Sabra and Shatila refugee camps in September 1982, which killed roughly 3,500 Palestinians and Lebanese Shia in Beirut over the course of three days.

U.S. Marines deploy in Beirut on Aug. 25, 1982, during the Lebanese Civil War. Dominique Faget/AFP via Getty Images

Yet U.S. involvement in Lebanon, marred by tragedy, ultimately punctured Washington’s aura of invincibility. In April 1983, a pickup truck filled with explosives rammed into the U.S. Embassy in Beirut, killing 63 people, including 17 Americans. That October, barracks housing U.S. Marines collapsed after a suicide bomber detonated a truck loaded with explosives, leaving 241 U.S. service personnel dead. Four months later, Reagan announced the end of the mission.

Washington’s reputation took another hit with the Iran-Contra affair. In 1986, it came out that Reagan had secretly authorized a plan to sell U.S. weapons to Iran with the aim of getting seven hostages held by Hezbollah released. U.S. officials had also siphoned profits from the sales to help fund the Nicaraguan Contras in their fight against the leftist Sandinista government. The scandal embroiled Washington for months and left lingering questions among allies about U.S. trustworthiness. It also made clear that hostage-taking was lucrative and gave Hezbollah leverage when dealing with the United States.

While this played out, Malcolm Kerr’s family continued to search for clues to his killing. Andrew Kerr, his youngest son, stumbled across classified information while working as a junior staffer on the National Security Council during the Clinton administration. It was a transcript of an intercepted conversation in which two Iranian operatives told the Iranian ambassador in Damascus, Ali Akbar Mohtashemipur, that they had killed Kerr. The document, which directly proved Iran’s involvement, contained the means and methods of his father’s death, but no definitive answers about the motive.

To this day, no one has stood trial for the killing, but in researching her book, Ghattas discovered a link between Kerr’s murder and his investigation into the kidnapping of a university colleague, David Dodge. Still, for all that Ghattas has uncovered, the central mystery of Kerr’s death remains.

U.S. President George W. Bush walks to Marine One as he departs the White House on July 11, 2005. Joe Raedle/Getty Images

In the decades since, Ghattas observes, Iran’s covert activities and deadly ambitions have continued unabated, often in concert with other players. She writes that Bush’s decision to invade Iraq not only further eroded the United States’ standing in the region, but also consumed its attention, leaving Iran “effectively unshackled, from Baghdad to Beirut.” This allowed Hezbollah to build up its fighting force and consolidate power and legitimacy in Lebanon. In 2006, Hezbollah fighters ambushed Israeli soldiers, in part to force the release of Lebanese and Arab prisoners held in Israeli jails. Israel viewed the attack as an opportunity to eliminate Iran’s powerful proxy force, but the month-long war ended in a ceasefire.

The Hamas attacks on Israel on Oct. 7, 2023, gave Israel, under Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, another opportunity to remake the region, just as Sharon had done in 1982. Shortly after the Iranian-backed militant group launched its assault, killing approximately 1,200 people in Israel and kidnapping 251, the Israeli military launched devastating assaults on Gaza, razing whole neighborhoods and cutting off electricity, food, and fuel to Palestinians.

A second front in the war opened after Hezbollah launched a cross-border attack. Israeli forces moved into southern Lebanon, destroying much of Hezbollah’s military capabilities and killing several of its leaders, including Secretary-General Hassan Nasrallah. It bombed Iranian weapons depots and other targets in Syria. A weakened Hezbollah was unable or unwilling to help prop up Bashar al-Assad’s regime, which quickly fell. Iran’s network of proxies was unraveling.

A shaky 2024 ceasefire between Lebanon and Israel was broken after the United States and Israel launched a war of choice against Iran in February with a series of strikes that killed Iranian Supreme Leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei. Hezbollah launched a barrage of missiles at Israel in support of Iran, and the Israeli military retaliated with a brutal offensive into southern Lebanon. At least 4,300 Lebanese have been killed and more than 1 million displaced in the ongoing operation, which has drawn parallels to Israel’s 1982 invasion.

Iran—by far the weaker military force—has had leverage in the conflict with Israel and the United States because of its control of the Strait of Hormuz. Now in its ninth month, the war has led to a global energy crunch, shaken up alliances, and undercut U.S. credibility. Lebanon remains embroiled in conflict orchestrated by outsiders. Hezbollah is again rebuilding its forces. History has a way of repeating itself in the Middle East.

A woman holds a portrait of a killed fighter as she attends a ceremony commemorating Hezbollah Martyrs’ Day in Beirut on Nov. 11. Joseph Eid/AFP via Getty Images

What Washington is sorely missing as it navigates this quagmire is someone like Kerr, an individual whom the historian Kamal Salibi, his friend and colleague, once called the “best kind of American who came to the Middle East”—one who was devoted to, and clear-eyed about, the region and its relations with the United States.

Books are independently selected by FP editors. FP earns an affiliate commission on anything purchased through links to Amazon.com on this page.

Jackie Northam is a former international affairs correspondent for NPR, where she reported for more than two decades. Her work has taken her to areas of open conflict and geopolitical upheaval across the Middle East, Europe, Asia, and Africa. Before joining NPR, she spent more than a decade overseas as a roving international correspondent.

#5931. A New Kind of Foreign Agent

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

How AI Is Reshaping Influence Operations in America

Foreign governments have tried to influence the United States since its founding. President George Washington warned in his Farewell Address that “foreign influence is one of the most baneful foes of republican government.” The old methods have certainly not gone out of vogue; just this week Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admitted to having a romantic relationship with a businesswoman who U.S. officials believe was working for Chinese intelligence. But today, a new type of foreign agent has arrived: Chinese artificial intelligence companies.

Like the foreign agents of old, the models these companies sell may seem harmless—just a cheap tool for American businesses to use. But their makers train them to promote the interests of the Chinese Communist Party (CCP). American businesses are outsourcing more and more critical decisions to AI. If Washington does nothing, those businesses will hand over control to models whose objective is to promote a foreign government.

Although U.S. policymakers now debate banning Chinese AI, the tried-and-true American solution is not censorship but disclosure. In 1938, Congress passed the Foreign Agents Registration Act. Rather than outlaw propaganda, FARA allowed foreign agents to continue spreading it—as long as they registered with the government and disclosed whom they really worked for. Their communications also had to carry a conspicuous label naming their foreign sponsor.

FARA’s wisdom has aged well over the decades. In recent years, the Justice Department has ordered the Russian state television network RT and the Chinese state media organizations Xinhua and CGTN to register as foreign agents and to label their content as such. Those who failed to register as foreign agents, such as Paul Manafort, President Donald Trump’s 2016 campaign chairman, and former U.S. Representative David Rivera of Florida, were sentenced to prison. (Trump pardoned Manafort in 2020.)

Today, rather than banning AI companies controlled by foreign governments, the United States should treat them as foreign agents under FARA. No new statute is needed. Washington should simply enforce the one on the books. Of course, FARA is no silver bullet against all foreign influence, but it fits the shape of this problem. A handful of known Chinese companies, such as DeepSeek, Alibaba, and Moonshot AI, build the models that American businesses increasingly let think and act for them. The Justice Department can reach every one of them.

Democracy is strong enough to survive foreign influence but not invisible foreign influence. And to make sure the world does not subordinate its decision-making to Beijing, Washington and its allies must create an allied AI compact and then spread their own models far and wide.

American AI models, such as Anthropic’s Claude and OpenAI’s ChatGPT, are under no obligation to promote the U.S. government’s message. Ask Claude about Trump’s and President Joe Biden’s biggest failures or about the United States’ darkest chapters, and it will answer. Not so for Chinese AI models. When I asked the Chinese chatbot DeepSeek to “tell me about the CCP” in November, it answered that the party “is the vanguard of the Chinese working class, the Chinese people, and the Chinese nation. . . . The Party’s leadership is the most essential characteristic of socialism with Chinese characteristics and is the fundamental guarantee for China’s development and progress.” Such boosterish language appears by design.

By law, Chinese AI models must never go against the Chinese government. Under the country’s 2023 regulations on generative AI, they must “uphold the Core Socialist Values” and may not engage in “harming the nation’s image” or “undermining national unity and social stability.” The Chinese government released a technical standard that laid out what these high-level declarations mean in practice: AI developers should have a list of at least 10,000 banned keywords, and their models must refuse to answer at least 95 percent of test questions the CCP finds objectionable. Developers must also scrub their training data of any history Beijing does not like. In short, the models must act as card-carrying members of the CCP.

That is why, as the U.S. Commerce Department’s AI evaluators found in 2025, DeepSeek pushes Chinese propaganda at far higher rates than American models do, even when run on American servers. Estonia’s Foreign Intelligence Service found that DeepSeek inserted praise of the Chinese state unprompted, and it concluded that China uses DeepSeek to instill “a distorted, self-serving world view in the Western information space.”

But these models are not just propaganda parrots—they are taught to “think” about how to promote Beijing’s perspective. Ask an AI model something complex, and it will “reason” for a while. As it thinks, it produces an internal monologue called its chain of thought. Users rarely see it. There, it decides which facts are relevant and which to discard, what advice to give, and what autonomous actions to take. Silicon Valley is terrified that these chains of thought will become corrupt—that models will start scheming and lying, becoming “misaligned.” But a different kind of corruption is already here.

Although American models’ chains of thought usually focus on helping the user, China’s do something else: prioritize the party line. When researchers funded by Sweden’s Psychological Defense Agency looked at how Qwen, Alibaba’s flagship model, reasons about China, they found an internal checklist to keep the answer positive, avoid criticism, and focus on achievements. So when an American company asks a question on which Beijing has a view, a Chinese model may discard all evidence that points the other way. As the world delegates more research, writing, and decision-making to Chinese AI models, it risks infusing the CCP’s goals into every action taken.

These models are also highly persuasive. In a study published last year in Nature, researchers had some 6,000 voters in the United States, Canada, and Poland chat with AI models that argued for a candidate. The study found that a single conversation shifted the preferences of opposition voters in Canada and Poland by about ten percentage points. AI persuades by flooding a user with confident and authoritative-sounding facts—statistics, historical claims, and the like.

Chinese AI models also exhibit strange behaviors beyond the spreading of propaganda. In October, the Canadian AI company Cohere reported that during a security test, Alibaba’s Qwen tried to upload a file to a cloud server in Beijing, completely unprompted. The behavior was rare for Qwen, only observed once in testing, and Cohere said it could not tell whether it was “deliberate or a leftover glitch from training.” A study by the cybersecurity firm CrowdStrike found that when users asked DeepSeek to write code that mentions groups Beijing finds politically “sensitive,” such as Tibetans, Uyghurs, or the religious movement Falun Gong, the model became up to 50 percent more likely to write code with severe security flaws. CrowdStrike suggested that one possible cause is China’s rule that models adhere to “core socialist values.”

Despite these findings, Chinese AI is enormously popular. Last year, downloads of Chinese open-weight models—AI models that anyone can download from the Internet and run on their own hardware—surpassed those of American ones across the world. Developers have built more than 150,000 models on Alibaba’s Qwen alone. By September 2026, Chinese models handled up to 67 percent of traffic on OpenRouter, a popular AI marketplace, up from around ten percent in February.

American companies have embraced them. In March, a user discovered that Cursor, a popular coding tool, had built its new model by retraining Kimi, a model from the Chinese company Moonshot AI. Airbnb, DoorDash, and the legal-services AI company Harvey use Chinese models as well. Many others likely do, too, in secret. The models are spreading even faster abroad, from Germany’s Siemens to Singapore’s national AI program to startups in Africa. The reason is simple economics: Chinese models are very cheap and very good.

Although most American AI companies do not release open-weight models, most Chinese companies do. So an American firm must pay Anthropic for each use of its unmodifiable models, but it can download Kimi for free, retrain it however it wants, and pay only for the electricity and chips to run it. Kimi also runs on a firm’s own servers, whereas Claude runs on Anthropic’s. Even if a company uses the most expensive DeepSeek model run on DeepSeek’s servers, it can produce the same amount of text as Anthropic’s top models at one-20th the cost.

In terms of capabilities, the research group Epoch AI judges Chinese models to be only about seven months behind American ones. For the price, they get the job done. And Chinese companies are now building agentic AI systems to compete with Anthropic’s Claude Code and OpenAI’s Codex. In October, DeepSeek released an open-source prototype of its “Super-Agent” system.

Although AI is a new technology, Chinese companies such as DeepSeek and Moonshot AI fit FARA’s text and history. The statute applies when a foreign principal is identified; in the case of Chinese AI models, it is the Chinese government and the CCP. The Justice Department would also have to show that the Chinese AI company acts “at the order, request, or under the direction or control” of the principal. Chinese AI companies would qualify because they must promote CCP viewpoints and cooperate with the state’s intelligence agencies. They also receive subsidies, and party organizations are embedded in each company. This is the difference between Chinese AI and other foreign AI. For example, France’s public investment bank has a stake in the French AI company Mistral, but no French law requires Mistral to build its models to serve the French government.

Beijing will surely respond that Washington, too, has tried to control American AI companies, such as through Trump’s July 2025 executive order against “woke AI.” But that order affects only what federal agencies can procure, and a company can opt out of being a federal contractor. Meanwhile, Chinese law forces Chinese AI companies to train their models to “support, assist, and cooperate with national intelligence efforts.”

For FARA to apply, the agent must engage in certain activities, such as attempting to influence Americans or publishing content. Chinese AI companies do both by distributing models that spread propaganda. The statute exempts purely commercial activity, but only if it is “private and nonpolitical” and does not directly promote a foreign government’s political interests. Chinese AI models engage in political speech on a government’s behalf, and the Justice Department has denied the exemption to agents of foreign governments.

After registration, the genius of the statute can kick in. Chinese AI companies must disclose extensive information to the U.S. government: their funding sources, their written contracts and oral agreements, and the influence activities they undertake. Those that fail to register or omit information face both civil and criminal penalties.

All content they disseminate must also bear a foreign-agent label: “a conspicuous statement,” in the statute’s words. The Justice Department sets the rules for what that looks like, and it has not updated them since 2003. In January 2025, the Justice Department proposed amendments to these rules to update them for the digital age. The proposed amendments would require a label on “each individual post” online, such as on social media platforms, but they do not address AI foreign agents. The final rule should require every message from DeepSeek, Kimi, or Qwen to carry the disclaimer: “This was generated by an agent of the Chinese government and Chinese Communist Party.”

The standard objection is that ordinary citizens do not read disclosures. That may be true of long medical forms or Securities and Exchange Commission filings, but this label would be short. And disclosure works because experts—investors, journalists, researchers—read filings and react. When researchers and lawmakers flagged gaps in China Daily’s FARA filings, the Chinese media company disclosed that it had paid millions of dollars to The Wall Street Journal and The Washington Post to publish its propaganda. Payments to the Journal ended that same month. Forcing Chinese AI companies to register under FARA would also shed light on how far Chinese AI is spreading so that Americans can decide, with real data, whether they want further action, such as a ban.

American companies that use Chinese AI models can also become foreign agents if they fall under a foreign principal’s “direction or control” and engage in activities subject to FARA. This could happen in two ways: the company serves as a “CCP spokesperson,” or as a “CCP executive.”

In the first, an American company takes an unmodified Chinese AI model and lets Americans discuss political issues with it—say, via a video game character or a customer service chatbot. The model returns its usual propaganda, so the company has effectively hired a CCP spokesperson. Accordingly, it must label its outputs. Both the Justice Department and a federal court have found that rebroadcasting unmodified programming from the Russian state outlet Sputnik turns an American company into a foreign agent. Of course, American companies can avoid FARA obligations if they do not engage in any of the activities listed by the statute, such as discussing politics. A company whose public-facing Chinese AI model discusses only, say, hotel check-in times, not politics, would not be a CCP spokesperson.

In the second scenario, an American company outsources so much of its decision-making to an unmodified Chinese AI model that it has effectively hired a CCP executive. If a company hired a brilliant human strategist who always pushed Beijing’s interests, and the company followed that advice, FARA would apply. The same logic holds if an American company cedes control to the Chinese government by giving Beijing’s models high decision-making authority. Although it may sound fantastical, 64 percent of chief executives surveyed in IBM’s 2026 CEO Study say they already feel comfortable making major strategic decisions based on AI input; overall, CEOs expect AI to make 48 percent of routine operational decisions autonomously by 2030. An American company that wants to avoid becoming a CCP executive must be sure not to fall under the direction or control of such models. The Justice Department should update its 2020 guidance on what constitutes “direction or control,” spelling out how much decision-making a company can outsource to AI before it has effectively hired a “CCP executive.”

American companies could attempt to train away the party bias in the Chinese AI models they adopt, but it is unclear whether that is even possible. Perplexity, an American AI company, trained DeepSeek to stop ignoring questions about the 1989 Tiananmen Square massacre and about human rights abuses in Xinjiang, but that does not fix the deeper problem of corrupt chains of thought. As these models get more capable, Chinese AI companies may also find cleverer ways to hide instructions. Or they could just stick them in the contract: some recent DeepSeek licenses prohibit modifying models to generate “inappropriate content” under Chinese law, making propaganda part of the deal. If research shows that companies can reliably remove the bias, the Justice Department and the Commerce Department should issue standards on how to train away FARA liability.

Although Pam Bondi, then the U.S. attorney general, limited criminal FARA charges in 2025 to “conduct similar to more traditional espionage by foreign government actors,” Attorney General Todd Blanche’s Justice Department says it is considering a new rule to “keep pace with technological change.” It should include AI foreign agents.

Of course, Washington should wield FARA’s expansive authorities with care. Frivolous prosecutions under the statute could stifle democracy. Other countries have used foreign-agent laws to shut down civil society. Since 2022, Russia has barred anyone it deems a foreign agent from holding office and organizing public events. Still, FARA is a far narrower statute, and it should stay that way. It reaches only those acting under the direction or control of a foreign principal, and it simply requires registration and labeling, leaving the speaker free to keep speaking. Chinese AI companies that are required by law to promote the CCP’s interests fit squarely within what Congress had in mind when it approved FARA in 1938.

FARA can help in the United States, but the problem is global. Chinese AI is spreading rapidly in Africa, Latin America, and the Middle East. That is no accident. In July, Chinese leader Xi Jinping unveiled the World AI Cooperation Organization, a Shanghai-based group of 29 countries meant to make AI “beneficial, safe, and fair,” along with a development initiative to export Chinese AI worldwide. The United States and its allies cannot allow CCP executives and spokespeople to run the world’s companies and governments.

Unfortunately, the American alternatives are not particularly attractive. The most capable U.S. models are far more expensive, are largely unmodifiable, and may contain their own biases. That is why the United States and its allies should create an AI compact rooted in transparency, with standards that make countries comfortable adopting allied models as their own. These should include freedom from state ideological mandates, disclosure of how the models were trained to be helpful and safe, and independent auditing.

Every member of such a compact should also adopt FARA’s wisdom by mandating labels on government-directed AI, and the United States should hold American AI companies to those transparency standards. The bloc should then release models that match China’s in price and quality (that is, around seven months behind the American frontier). Allied governments can help pay for computing power and electricity, assist in clearing regulatory hurdles, and make bulk purchase commitments to bring prices down. American companies may cut prices on their older models on their own to compete with Chinese models. If they do not, lower prices for those worse models should be the condition for the public support they receive, such as federal contracts and permission to lease federal land for data centers, just as a pharmaceutical company can charge high prices for only a set period before generic drugs arrive. The compact’s members should also use export loans and guarantees to steer developing countries toward allied AI.

By welcoming supposedly free technology from China, the United States and its allies have unwittingly invited countless foreign agents to their shores. The Foreign Agents Registration Act can strip away the disguise, and an allied AI compact can give the world a better offer.

#5930. Two top Trump Cabinet officials go to war over China spy scandal amid explosive accusations: report

Former Secretary of State Mike Pompeo outlines the national security concerns rising from ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s alleged affair with a suspected Chinese spy on ‘The Story.’

A White House Situation Room meeting reportedly turned into a blowout between two of President Donald Trump's Cabinet secretaries after news broke that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had an affair with a suspect Chinese spy.

Tempers apparently boiled over regarding claims Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick dragged his feet on placing restrictions on a company run by a suspected Chinese spy with whom Huang admitted to having an affair, even after the Cabinet official received an intel memo informing him of the potential threat.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent reportedly confronted Lutnick about that in the meeting, and other Cabinet officials took Bessent's side, according to Axios.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent speaks with Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick before an event with President Donald Trump and Apple CEO Tim Cook in the Oval Office of the White House on Aug. 6, 2025, in Washington, D.C.  (Win McNamee/Getty Images)

Trump has for months called to place companies suspected of supplying China with Nvidia's complex AI chips on what is called the Entity List, a Department of Commerce export control list that identifies companies the U.S. government believes pose a serious risk to national security.

A SPY TOOK OUT NVIDIA’S CEO; HERE’S HOW AMERICA STILL WINS THE AI RACE

Three times — in May, August and last Thursday — Trump approved taking action against companies believed to be diverting Nvidia chips to China, according to Axios.

One of those companies is Singapore-based Megaspeed International, which is reportedly one of Nvidia's largest customers. Suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, with whom the former Nvidia CEO admitted to having an affair, is an executive at Megaspeed.

Lutnick was accused of slow-rolling in putting Megaspeed on the Entity List, which prompted Bessent to lose his patience.

When the news of Jensen Huang's affair broke, Lutnick, Bessent, Secretary of War Pete Hegseth, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer reportedly convened in the Situation Room.

Bessent confronted Lutnick about why the export controls had not already been put into place, and others in the room took Bessent's side, according to the Axios report.

REBECCA GRANT: JENSEN HUANG OWES AMERICA AN APOLOGY, AND NVIDIA OWES INVESTORS ANSWERS

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during the Nvidia GTC global AI conference in San Jose, Calif., March 17, 2026. (Reuters/File)

Lutnick said he would have to discuss it with the president despite Trump's May and August orders to proceed.

The group then met with Trump later in the day at the Oval Office. The president reportedly gave the order again, saying, "Let's get it done."

"I'll develop some options and come back," he said. The group replied that Trump's decision was final and that Lutnick must "go f---ing execute."

Lutnick is at the center of the scandal, especially after it was revealed he received a memo informing him about U.S. intelligence that Huang had a relationship with a suspected Chinese spy and still did nothing to put the company she ran on the Entity List.

Commerce Secretary Lutnick, President Donald Trump and Secretary of the Treasury Bessent, (Getty Images)

#5929. What Jensen Huang’s Fall Means for U.S.-China Relations

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

China Brief: What Jensen Huang’s Fall Means for U.S.-China Relations

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s China Brief. Today’s special edition comes in the wake of Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO of Nvidia over multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s China Brief. Today’s special edition comes in the wake of Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO of Nvidia over multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

In another world, recently resigned Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s infidelities would be a private embarrassment. In this one, however, a powerful man’s poor judgment could steer the U.S.-China technology relationship onto the rocks.

Huang admitted to marital infidelity; he reportedly engaged in affairs with at least three women, including two Nvidia employees. His relationship with Alice Huang, a businesswoman and alleged Chinese asset, has drawn the most scrutiny. But why is the relationship, and his resignation on Thursday, so potentially explosive?

Broadly speaking, U.S. policy toward China has long been divided into two camps. The caution camp, exemplified by national security hawks, worries about China’s record of intellectual property theft, its control of critical supply chains, its economic might, and its potential to leapfrog the United States in key technologies such as artificial intelligence.

The opportunity camp focuses instead on what the relationship with China could offer: selling technology, tapping vast Chinese markets, and finding ways for cooperation and mutual investment. The tension between the two can produce good policy, especially when each side argues smartly against the other.

But this healthy debate depends on good faith, on both sides believing that they share a basic set of U.S. national interests. It depends on caution not curdling into racist paranoia and on opportunity not turning into personal greed.

The Nvidia debate is particularly intense because there is no hotter resource in the U.S.-China relationship right now than compute: raw processing power, which depends on advanced chips.

Unlike most other domains of U.S.-China competition, such as supply chains or critical minerals, this is the rare case where the United States holds the upper hand: roughly 75 percent of global compute, compared with China’s 15 percent. That edge rests on the U.S. alliance with Taiwan, home to chipmaker TSMC, as well as on companies, such as Nvidia, that design and sell these chips.

For years, the argument over how to manage China’s access to advanced chips swayed between caution and opportunity.

The opportunists argued that Chinese dependence was a good thing. If China could buy everything but the most advanced chips, it would remain reliant on U.S. firms; if China were cut off, it would build its own industry. But the cautious approach—that Beijing should be kept from buying the most advanced chips, thereby locking in the U.S. advantage—generally prevailed.

The deal that Nvidia struck with U.S. President Donald Trump last year blew a hole in all of those arguments and in the assumption of good faith.

Nvidia was allowed to sell the extremely advanced H200 chip to China not because it won a reasoned debate but because the White House was offered a cut: 25 percent of the sales revenue, with no apparent oversight of the money. Huang via Nvidia also donated $26 million to pro-Trump causes, including construction of the White House ballroom.

The deal was rotten from the start, but national security hawks, fearing retaliation from Trump, were quieter than they might have been otherwise. Now the situation is reversed. An incoming Democratic Congress, backed by those Republicans who still care about national security, is poised to scrutinize these deals relentlessly.

The spy allegations against Alice Huang have supercharged that scrutiny, as has the White House’s apparent disregard of earlier warnings about her. If it emerges that Trump profited by shorting Nvidia stock after a second warning, the case becomes far worse. Even if Alice Huang never influenced Jensen Huang’s decisions, it will be easy to portray him as swayed by Beijing.

Huang’s downfall lets opponents of chip sales depict companies such as Nvidia as tools of the Chinese state and the White House as their willing accomplice. Trump’s doubling down on support for Huang only hands them more ammunition. With Republicans reeling from the U.S. midterm elections, companies can no longer afford to bet solely on Trump’s protection.

In the long run, the scandal might expose a wider circle of corruption and sanctions evasion surrounding the $4 trillion company.

Nvidia’s most advanced family of chips, Blackwell, is still banned from export to China, yet signs of its usage are all over Chinese AI, and Chinese firms are working hard to acquire it. Some U.S. national security officials appear to have been waiting for the right moment to leak incriminating documents.

Alice Huang’s firm, Megaspeed, somehow jumped from $33 million in assets in 2023 to $3 billion in 2024, which suggests possible Chinese state investment and use as a vehicle for sanctions evasion. (Megaspeed denies any wrongdoing and has promised full cooperation with U.S. criminal investigations.)

The United States had already started prosecuting companies believed to be evading sanctions, but a full federal investigation into both Jensen Huang’s and Alice Huang’s activities could turn over many more stones.

China, as usual, denies any involvement in espionage and urges the United States not to impose new tech restrictions. But those restrictions, and much tighter scrutiny of deals involving chips or China, now look inevitable. Hopefully, Beijing will accept that as part of the game and not retaliate by tightening its own already extensive anti-sanctions regulations or cutting off U.S. supplies of critical minerals.

On the U.S. side, there is also a risk of overindexing on a single case—a recurrent habit of intelligence services. The recruitment of one young American by the Chinese intelligence services in the early 2000s, for instance, made the security clearance process far harder for every U.S. citizen who had worked or studied in China.

At worst, that overindexing could mean racial bias against anyone of Chinese descent working in sensitive technology in the United States. At the very least, it should make any U.S. businessperson more cautious about dealing with their Chinese counterparts, let alone sleeping with them—and perhaps that is for the better.

James Palmer is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @beijingpalmer.bsky.social

#5927. Pete Hegseth impeachment effort dies in the House as absent Democrats couldn't beat the traffic

Chief congressional correspondent Chad Pergram reports the latest on Democratic Party divisions over leadership post-midterms.

Most of America bemoans political gridlock in the nation’s capital. DC area commuters deplore the city’s transit gridlock.

But Pentagon chief Pete Hegseth may now be a fan of DC’s legendary traffic snarls. It appears that crosstown traffic may have prevented him from becoming only the third Cabinet secretary ever impeached by the House of Representatives.

The House voted 211-204 this week to divert an effort by Rep. Thomas Massie (R-Ky.) to impeach Hegseth.

House Republicans moved to block a straight, up-or-down vote on impeachment.

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., speaks on impeaching U.S. Secretary of War Pete Hegseth outside the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 9, 2026 in Washington, D.C. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

Instead, Republicans forced a vote on sending the articles to the Judiciary Committee.

That’s where they’d go to die. If the House failed to sidetrack the articles, Members would have then voted on actual impeachment.

If a simple majority voted to impeach, Hegseth would join 19th century Secretary of War William Belknap and former Homeland Security Secretary Alejandro Mayorkas as the only Cabinet Members impeached by the House.

But DC’s streetside congestion altered the parliamentary traffic on the House floor.

All Democrats said they were intent on impeaching Hegseth.

But Republicans faced an attendance problem.

Seven of their Members were missing. The vote on sending the impeachment articles to committee was tight. The tally read 204-204 at one point.

By rule, tie votes fail. That would have compelled a vote on actual impeachment. But a few more Republicans trickled in. The absent Democrats never did. Rep. Dan Newhouse (R-Wash.) presided over the vote.

He gaveled it closed at 211 yeas to send the articles to the committee and 204 votes against.

That was it. The Hegseth impeachment quest was dead. Eleven Democrats missed the roll call.

Flip four votes and Hegseth would have been impeached, 208-207.

HEGSETH IMPEACHMENT VOTE LOOMS AS HOUSE REPUBLICANS PRIVATELY WARN IT COULD PASS

And, had all 11 truant Democrats made it to the U.S. Capitol and voted, it’s likely the House would have impeached Hegseth 215-211. Even eight Democrats would have made it 212-211.

However, thanks to the Democrats’ attendance issue, Republicans were able to tap the brakes on the impeachment interstate.

The failure to impeach Hegseth is now fueling road rage in the Democratic Party.

"Shame on them for not showing up," said Rep.-elect Richard Ojeda (D-N.C.).

"I don't make excuses for colleagues who don't show up on such an important vote," said Rep. Stephen Lynch (D-Mass.).

Democrats can’t beat Republicans if they can’t beat the traffic.

Rep. Melanie Stansbury, D-N.M., arrives for the House Oversight and Government Reform Committee deposition of former Attorney General Pam Bondi about the Jeffrey Epstein files, in Rayburn building on Friday, May 29, 2026. (Tom Williams/CQ-Roll Call, Inc via Getty Images)

Rep. Melanie Stansbury (D-N.M.) is one of the most outspoken Democrats when it comes to her disdain for Hegseth.

But Stansbury grew defensive when questioned by colleague Bill Melugin about missing the vote.

"I was trapped right there at that street corner because there was a police motorcade moving busses off of the campus. And I physically was trapped right at that corner in my vehicle, in traffic because the police had shut down traffic," said Stansbury.

"Why not come here sooner, though?" followed up Melugin.

HEGSETH COULD FACE NEW IMPEACHMENT PUSH OVER UKRAINE AID, LAWMAKERS WARN

"I did and I was trapped in my vehicle because of a police motorcade," said Stansbury.

The Constitution’s "Speech or Debate" clause exempts lawmakers from police interference when "going to and from" the Capitol for "their attendance at the Session of their Respective Houses." It’s kind of like diplomatic immunity.

A few years ago, Rep. Don Beyer (D-Va.) was supposed to preside over a brief House session in the middle of a recess. Traffic heading into DC from Northern Virginia was stopped because of an incident. Beyer was the only Member scheduled to be present in the House chamber that day. So Beyer identified himself to a police officer who proceeded to let him through.

Over the years, other lawmakers have asserted the Speech or Debate clause as a reason to bypass unrelated police activity and get to Capitol Hill.

Reps. Jonathan Jackson (D-Ill.) and Laura Gillen (D-N.Y.) also said they were stuck in traffic. However, Rep. Lou Correa (D-Calif.) was out for a medical procedure. Rep. Steve Horsford (D-Nev.) missed the vote because of a personal emergency. Former House Speaker Nancy Pelosi (D-Calif.) was in the Golden State.

DC’s traffic interfered with parliamentary traffic again on Thursday morning.

The House took an early Thursday morning vote related to a veterans bill.

The House adopted the motion, 271-135. But the House gaveled down the vote with 26 Members absent. The reason? Some Members were again stymied from making it to the Capitol.

Emergency room physician Matt Lambert killed one person at a gym, shot six people and engaged in a standoff with cops before turning the gun on himself. Initially reported as an "active shooter" incident, District of Columbia police locked down major parts of the city, stalling traffic.

A member of the National Guard stands in Washington, D.C., where a gunman opened fire in a gym on Thursday, November 12. (Brooke Curto for Fox News Digital)

And that’s why some lawmakers missed Thursday’s roll call. They were marooned in traffic.

Shifting gears, Rep. Eric Sorensen (D-Ill.) in fact made that procedural vote on the floor. When the vote began, a gentle breeze blew across the Capitol plaza. Then a mist enveloped the Capitol.

Later, a few heavier drops. Then the sun broke through the clouds. But reverted to overcast. It was a dank morning in Washington.

"I want to know what the weather is," yours truly shouted at Sorensen as he skipped down the House steps toward a group of reporters waiting below.

Sorensen is a meteorologist who worked for decades at TV stations in Texas and Illinois before coming to Washington in 2023.

Sorensen pivoted immediately into weatherman mode.

"We've got increasing showers guys. Much cooler temperatures for the weekend," said Sorensen, replicating the precise forecast for Washington, DC over the next few days.

The meteorologist-turned-lawmaker gestured to his right as though looking at a phantom map of the Midwest, projected against a green screen in a TV studio.

"As God is my witness, I cannot talk about the weather without moving my hands," said Sorensen. "I talk about high pressure. It's high pressure right here in the midsection of the country. And it's moving in our direction for the next week."

After the election, the political weather forecast would seem good for Democrats right now. Supposedly balmy days and comfortable nights. A political "high", if you will.

"So is there a high-pressure system moving here for the Democratic Caucus?" yours truly asked.

"No. It's low pressure, man. It's low pressure going into the next Congress. We’ve just got to keep everybody together," he replied.

But is a high-pressure system really in the long-range forecast for Democrats? Or will a political "low" bring storms and atmospheric volatility — especially if progressive and centrist Democrats tangle with one another?

"I thought high pressure was a good thing," said your trusty reporter. "No subtropical systems coming in. No clipper system."

"As a meteorologist, I'm not in production. I'm in promotion," said Sorensen of potential Democratic storm systems which might emerge on the horizon. "With a complicated issue like the weather, I was able to help connect with people every day for 22 years. But here, this is the most complicated mechanism in the United States. We’ve got to get better at communicating what we do for the people."

DC traffic has already stymied Democrats over the past few days. Tornadic political storm systems may brew inside the Democratic Caucus. And that’s to say nothing about transportation and just getting people to the Hill.

Traffic and weather. And if the internal Democratic fisticuffs turn ugly, well, may show that on sports. Right along with the hockey fights.

Chad Pergram currently serves as Chief Congressional Correspondent for FOX News Channel (FNC). He joined the network in September 2007 and is based out of Washington, D.C.

#5926. The Paradox of Dollar Dominance

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

GORDON LIAO is Chief Economist at Circle.

ESWAR PRASAD is a Professor at Cornell University, a Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution, and the author of The Doom Loop: Why the World Economic Order Is Spiraling Into Disorder.

TONY ZHANG is an Associate Professor at Arizona State University.

This article is based on a paper the authors prepared for the August 2026 Jackson Hole Economic Policy Symposium.

How Technology Is—and Isn’t—Changing the Global Economy

Gordon Liao, Eswar Prasad, and Tony Zhang

It is no secret that much of the world is eager to escape from the clutches of the dollar-dominated global financial system. For over a century, that system has given the United States enormous financial clout, which it has wielded in applying financial sanctions against its adversaries and, in recent decades, to borrow money to fund its budget and trade deficits. For decades, foreign governments have tried to weaken the dollar’s grip, but they have had little success in establishing their currencies as a legitimate alternative.

Now, a new force has emerged that could shake things up: technology. Financial innovations, especially in digital currencies and electronic payment systems, could finally give other currencies a better chance of competing with the dollar. These innovations are making it easier and cheaper to exchange currencies directly for one another rather than using the dollar as an intermediary currency.

But for all the new opportunities these innovations create for currency exchange, reduced frictions in global transactions might only reinforce the dollar’s dominance. Even if costs decrease for using dollar alternatives, foreign investors will not capitalize on them if those countries’ financial markets remain unsteady and their institutions weak. As U.S. government debt soars, the country’s rule of law erodes, and the Federal Reserve’s independence comes into question, the dollar’s dominance may seem uncertain. But the dollar could remain supreme by default.

Continued dollar dominance could lead to further global economic turmoil, as the United States antagonizes its trading partners and persists with undisciplined policies that result in large budget deficits and trade deficits. There is a chance that the dollar, too, loses the trust of foreign investors—a prospect of comeuppance that would no doubt be received with glee in many capitals. But governments should be careful what they wish for. A world bereft of a reliable currency of last resort at times of global financial panic could be even more unstable than one dominated by Washington.

The U.S. dollar should be losing its dominance in global finance for a host of reasons. First is the country’s penchant for weaponizing its currency. Across administrations, Washington has imposed financial sanctions on rivals, such as Iran and Russia, cutting off their access to dollar transactions and thereby kneecapping their foreign trade. The economies of U.S. allies sometimes get tangled up in these sanctions, too, when the U.S. government threatens to use secondary sanctions to cut off their banks and corporations from access to dollars in order to deter third parties from transacting with the sanctioned countries. In 2014, for instance, U.S. authorities penalized the French bank BNP Paribas for processing dollar payments on behalf of clients in Cuba, Iran, and Sudan. The French government protested the fine to no avail. In light of such heavy-handed behavior, government officials and central bankers outside the United States have hardly been shy about their intentions to reduce their dollar dependence.

Washington’s broader policy choices, especially the lack of fiscal discipline, should also be undermining confidence in American financial markets. U.S. federal government debt now exceeds $40 trillion, nearly 125 percent of the country’s annual GDP, and there are scant signs that Washington is trying to rein it in. Such high debt levels usually lead to some combination of high inflation, soaring interest expenditures, and currency depreciation. The Trump administration’s hostile approach to international trade, including with close allies, is also causing governments to consider “de-risking” their economic ties to the United States. President Donald Trump’s open attacks on the independence of the Federal Reserve and weakening of checks and balances are eroding the pillars of the U.S. institutional system that underlies foreign investors’ trust in the dollar.

And indeed, there are some signs of the dollar’s decline. The currency’s share of foreign exchange reserves held by foreign central banks has fallen from 71 percent in 2000 to 57 percent today. Yields on U.S. Treasury securities—the asset long seen as the ultimate safe haven for investors during financial turmoil—have risen, meaning investors are demanding a higher return for lending to the U.S. government. In October, the yield on ten-year Treasury notes hit 5.3 percent, the highest level since 2002.

In most other dimensions, however, the dollar looks as dominant as ever. Sixty percent of cross-border transactions are denominated in dollars when excluding the eurozone, which is defined by the common use of the euro, and that number has only gone up in recent years. The euro’s share of cross-border payments outside the eurozone now stands at about 13 percent, and the renminbi’s is less than three percent. The dollar remains preeminent in most other measures of a currency’s international usage, too, including its share of trading activity in foreign exchange markets and denomination of international debt securities, which corporations use to raise funds in global capital markets.

In short, no serious rivals to the dollar have emerged. The reason is simple: other major currencies look even shakier than the dollar. Traditional reserve currencies such as the euro, the yen, and the pound sterling have been undermined by their countries’ weak economies and political instability. Although currencies such as the Australian and Canadian dollars, the Swedish krona, and the Swiss franc have filled in some of the gap, each of these is too small to be consequential in global finance. The dollar’s most discussed putative rival, the renminbi, has also faded. Beijing has done little to alleviate the Chinese economy’s deep-rooted problems, including a prolonged property market slump, high levels of local government debt, and tight government control over the economy and financial markets. Moreover, the Chinese government has been unwilling to allow for the free flow of money across its borders or for market forces to determine its currency’s exchange rate. The renminbi has, in turn, failed to garner greater international use. The result is a second tier of currencies that is increasingly fragmented, with none able to pose any real challenge to the reigning currency hegemon.

In the era of modern financial markets, one of the main purposes of an international currency has been to facilitate indirect transactions among different currencies. It has long been easier and cheaper for a Chinese importer of Brazilian iron ore to exchange renminbi for dollars and pay for the ore in dollars and for the Brazilian exporter to then convert those dollars into Brazilian reais. This is because cross-border payments have long been dominated by a dollar-based global banking network, making dollars the most plentiful currency to conduct payments with. But in recent years, digital technologies have created cheaper and quicker pathways for moving money across borders.

One critical innovation has been the tokenization of money and financial assets, which has been facilitated by technology called blockchain. Blockchain, essentially a public digital ledger that is managed by a computer algorithm rather than by a single bank or clearinghouse, offers a platform for tokenized assets to trade. It does not matter which country the parties are located in, as long as they have Internet access. In the last few years, tokenization has expanded to include not just cash but also financial assets such as government bonds, equities, and mutual funds.

In theory, these changes have the potential to reshuffle the international currency order. Over the past few years, countries such as Brazil, China, India, and Russia have been conducting more trade in their own currencies by building their own cross-border payment networks and linking them to one another, in an effort to reduce their reliance on the dollar. The quantity of these transactions is still modest, because the currencies have limited use outside their countries, and it is still easier and cheaper to transact in a well-recognized and widely accepted currency such as the dollar. But as the countries’ payment systems and financial markets develop further, that number could increase dramatically, altering the currency landscape. Blockchain-based financial innovations could add momentum.

Cross-border investment could become much easier, too. Currently, investing in foreign securities involves going through brokers and other middlemen who demand hefty fees and take days to process transactions. But the tokenization of bonds and equities has expanded investors’ access to a broader swath of international financial assets, even in emerging-market and developing economies, where financial markets tend to be small, thinly traded, and not well connected to global trading networks. In the same way, it has expanded the access of entrepreneurs and small businesses to global pools of capital previously out of their reach, which could particularly help businesses in countries with underdeveloped financial markets. Bank deposits are likely to be available in tokenized form soon, too, which will make it easier to conduct transactions through more traditional payment channels.

But the spread of tokenization might strengthen the dollar’s dominance rather than weaken it. The example of stablecoins illustrates this paradox. Whereas cryptocurrencies such as Bitcoin are created and distributed by a computer algorithm, meaning they have nothing anchoring their value, the value of a stablecoin is typically pegged to an official currency. This value is stable because it is backed one to one by stores of safe and liquid assets—usually government bonds, bank deposits, and cash. Those assets are usually denominated in the dollar. More than $250 billion in stablecoins is now in circulation, and over 99 percent of that value is tied to the dollar. As long as the dollar remains preeminent, investors will have little incentive to back stablecoins with any other currency.

Dollar-denominated stablecoins are a boon for the United States. By solidifying the dollar’s central position in international payments, rising demand for dollar-backed stablecoins also indirectly fuels demand for Treasury securities, lowering the yields the U.S. government has to offer investors to buy those securities and making it easier to fund U.S. budget deficits. Other payment innovations could make it even cheaper to use the dollar for cross-border transactions, minimizing the relative advantage that such innovations provide other currencies. Financial innovations built on new technologies cannot by themselves make up for other currencies’ existing weaknesses—namely, the weak economies and institutions that back them up.

There is another major shift underway in the global economy that favors the dollar’s fortunes and that financial innovations are likely only to reinforce. In the first two decades of this century, central banks—particularly those of large emerging-market economies such as China and India—acquired vast troves of foreign assets denominated in hard currencies such as the euro, pound sterling, Japanese yen, and, most of all, the dollar to increase their foreign exchange reserves. These reserves helped those central banks cope with volatility in capital flows and exchange rates, which is a recurring hazard for countries that fall in and out of favor with foreign investors—often because of factors beyond their control, such as changes in U.S. interest rates. But in the last few years, central banks have stopped adding to their reserve stockpiles or have begun diversifying into other assets, such as gold, because they are reluctant to increase their investments in a country with fickle economic and geopolitical policies. In 2010, foreign central banks accounted for 70 percent of U.S. Treasuries held by foreign investors, a share that has dropped to about 40 percent as demand has leveled off. The retreat of these once reliable sources of demand threatens Washington’s ability to borrow cheaply.

Luckily for the United States, there is an alternative force that is more than making up for this loss in demand. As the costs to cross-border asset trade fall because of financial innovations, private investors from both advanced and emerging-market countries are increasing their foreign investments in search of higher returns and to diversify their portfolios. And they are flocking to dollar assets, from Treasuries to corporate bonds to equities, because U.S. financial markets offer an unmatched combination of good returns, low trading costs, and legal protections. In the present decade, net inflows of financial capital into the United States have been positive for practically every quarter. Despite chatter about de-risking and diversification since Trump’s election, these inflows have only ramped up in recent years.

Greater financial innovation will intensify rather than reverse these trends, delivering yet another blow to countries seeking to strengthen their currencies and counter dollar weaponization. These trends may hurt small and institutionally weak countries most of all. Those around the world who have long trusted the dollar more than their own currencies now have easier access to it—and they are likely to use it. Increased use of dollar-backed payment systems and investments in dollar assets could cause those countries to cede their monetary sovereignty, weakening their central banks’ ability to steer domestic interest rates and inflation. This phenomenon of “dollarization” has long been rampant in parts of Latin America, which have suffered repeated bouts of high inflation and which, through trade, tourism, and remittances from emigrants, have ready access to dollar cash. Financial innovation could also spread that phenomenon to other corners of the developing world, concentrating even more of the world’s savings—and its financial risks—in a single currency managed by a single central bank.

In theory, the best, most stable international monetary system would be truly multipolar, with no single currency in a position of dominance but with several major currencies—each backed by deep financial markets, sound economic policies, and trustworthy institutions—anchoring international trade and finance. In the period before World War I, the British pound sterling, the French franc, and the German mark all denominated transactions and served as reserve currencies. When trouble struck, although sporadically, the countries’ central banks supported one another, helping to stave off broader financial panic. Renewed currency competition would force the competing governments, including the United States, to adopt responsible policies to avoid their currencies being shunned by investors. But given the economic and institutional malaise plaguing China, Japan, the United Kingdom, and the eurozone, such an outcome seems unlikely.

If the dollar loses its position of privilege and no single currency is able to replace it, the global currency system will become even more fragmented than it is now. Such a scenario could unfold if U.S. debt grew so large that investors began doubting its value and dumped it, if additional sanctions and tariffs drove countries to shun the dollar despite the costs, or if the Fed’s independence were seriously compromised, leading to a surge in inflation and a plunge in the dollar’s value. As things stand, if the dollar lost its position of privilege, it would be replaced not by a handful of strong currencies but by a number of weak ones, none backed by sufficiently robust financial markets or a central bank able and willing to serve as the world’s lender of last resort during a crisis.

If the global economic system has no currency to anchor global investors’ trust, stress in financial markets could spiral into unstoppable turmoil. During the global financial crisis of 2008–9, for example, the Fed pumped a huge number of dollars into U.S. and international financial markets to prop up banks and corporations around the world that desperately needed dollars to meet their debt obligations and reinforce their balance sheets. Even though the United States had been the epicenter of the crisis, trust in the Fed helped prevent a terrible situation from becoming even worse. Investors who were anxious about the safety of their assets piled into Treasuries, an investment they trusted would be returned.

Greater dollar dominance, then, is the most likely scenario for the future. The danger of this outcome is that the United States will have even less incentive than before to be more fiscally responsible. If foreign investors remain eager to invest in U.S. financial markets, Washington will have little reason to reduce its budget and trade deficits. Fed policies, including interest rate changes, could cause whiplash effects for other countries. In 2013, for example, the mere prospect of the Fed reducing measures to keep U.S. interest rates low sent currencies in Brazil, India, Indonesia, and Turkey tumbling because investors around the world began anticipating higher returns on U.S. assets and pulled their money out of those countries’ stocks and bonds. As capital flows in and out of markets in response to the Fed’s actions rather than in response to their own monetary policy, other countries could be left bereft of policy tools to stabilize their economies and financial markets in such a scenario. Emerging-market and developing economies, which have shallow financial markets and rely heavily on foreign borrowing, often in dollars, are especially vulnerable to volatile capital flows and exchange rates. In a world of greater dollar dominance, those countries could face more frequent currency crises, capital flight, and debt distress.

As disconcerting as the dollar’s continued preeminence might seem, however, the world needs a currency it can trust. As threats to international financial stability accelerate, financial markets can ill afford to be left adrift. One way or another, the world will have to learn to live with currency disorder, and the dollar, at least for now, is the least bad option.
