#5917. La última oportunidad para la paz

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PEDRO SÁNCHEZ es presidente del Gobierno de España.

En algún momento de la última década, el mundo empezó a aceptar la guerra como algo inevitable. Las guerras de agresión, como las de Ucrania, el Líbano o Irán, aumentan y siembran horror, ruina y el temor a una confrontación global. Los gobiernos piensan cada vez más en la guerra como algo para lo que hay que prepararse y presupuestar. El gasto militar no deja de crecer, las carreras tecnológicas y armamentísticas se intensifican, y cada vez más países se plantean si deberían dotarse de un arsenal nuclear disuasorio que garantice su seguridad.

No hay nada más peligroso que normalizar la guerra. Supone regresar a una norma que imperó durante la mayor parte de la historia de la humanidad. La guerra era el eje vertebrador del sistema internacional. Era la vía natural por la que los Estados zanjaban sus disputas o ensanchaban su territorio a costa de vecinos más débiles.

Pero la inmensa devastación que produjeron las dos guerras mundiales en la primera mitad del siglo XX hizo inaceptable ese statu quo. Los países se unieron en un empeño común: que la guerra dejara de ser un instrumento legítimo de la política de Estado y pasara a ser un acto ilegal. Las guerras no terminaron en 1945, pero durante años la comunidad internacional las condenó de forma abrumadora.

En las últimas décadas, sin embargo, el compromiso de los gobiernos con la causa de la paz se ha debilitado. En 2003, millones de personas salieron a las calles en cientos de ciudades para oponerse a la invasión de Irak liderada por Estados Unidos. Y, aun así, la guerra siguió adelante. Iniciada a partir de premisas falsas y en violación del derecho internacional, arrasó Irak, agravó la inestabilidad regional y avivó el resentimiento y el terrorismo contra los países occidentales. Aquel conflicto marcó, además, un punto de inflexión. Desde entonces, la búsqueda de la paz ha ido retrocediendo en la agenda internacional, arrinconada por la creciente competencia geopolítica y la desatención o la indiferencia hacia los esfuerzos multilaterales que durante décadas pusieron freno a la guerra.

Este camino conduce a una nueva guerra mundial: la catástrofe que el orden del siglo XX se propuso evitar. Ante ese futuro sombrío en el horizonte, los gobiernos deben reaprender el arte de oponerse a la guerra. Todos los países, incluido el mío, España, tienen la responsabilidad de actuar para limitar la capacidad de otros de librar la guerra y para fortalecer las instituciones internacionales que han de ser garantes de la paz.

El éxito de este proyecto dependerá de un multilateralismo expansivo, al que yo llamo «maxilateralismo»: una cooperación que abarque al mayor número posible de países, en lugar de limitarse a pequeñas coaliciones ad hoc. Su ambición es que la guerra resulte cada vez más inviable, incluso inimaginable. En 2003, manifestantes por toda España coreaban «¡No a la guerra!». En 2026, el mundo no puede volver a ignorarlos.

Los esfuerzos internacionales por desterrar la guerra como instrumento de política nacional se remontan al Pacto Briand-Kellogg de 1928, un tratado firmado en París por 15 países, al que posteriormente se sumaron decenas más. No bastó para impedir la Segunda Guerra Mundial, pero plantó una semilla. Cuando representantes de 50 países se reunieron en San Francisco en 1945 para crear las Naciones Unidas, recuperaron el espíritu y los principios del Pacto y añadieron lo que había faltado: estructura institucional. La Carta de Naciones Unidas prohibió recurrir a la amenaza o al uso de la fuerza contra la integridad territorial o la independencia política de otro Estado. Y, a diferencia de su predecesor de 1928, respaldó esa prohibición con instituciones facultadas para hacerla cumplir.

En las décadas siguientes, estas instituciones levantaron un orden que castigaba las violaciones al derecho internacional e incentivaba la cooperación y la diplomacia. Al mismo tiempo, el rechazo popular a la guerra y al militarismo de la Guerra Fría cobraba fuerza en todo el mundo. Se hizo visible en la resistencia a la guerra de Vietnam y en los movimientos de masas contra las armas nucleares. El pacifismo alcanzó su punto álgido en los años noventa, cuando una sociedad civil global pujante se movilizó en defensa de causas humanitarias y Naciones Unidas tenía autoridad para abanderar una ambiciosa agenda de paz.

Hoy, sin embargo, hay más conflictos armados que en ningún otro momento desde 1946. Según el Programa de Datos sobre Conflictos de Uppsala, en 2025 hubo 65 conflictos activos con participación estatal, el doble que en 2010. Se calcula que en estos conflictos murieron 250.000 personas: la cifra anual más alta desde el genocidio de Ruanda hace más de 30 años. Cerca de 120 millones de personas en todo el mundo siguen desplazadas por la guerra.

Y lo más inquietante: las guerras de agresión, que violan la Carta de Naciones Unidas, están de vuelta. Rusia invadió Ucrania. Estados Unidos e Israel atacaron Irán. Y mientras los combates se prolongan año tras año en lugares como el Líbano, Sudán o Yemen, resulta evidente que la guerra está siendo aceptada de nuevo como algo normal.

Nada indica que esta tendencia vaya a frenarse. Los presupuestos militares se han disparado hasta niveles no vistos en generaciones, en detrimento del gasto social y de desarrollo. Solo en la última década, el gasto mundial en defensa ha aumentado más de un 40 %, y muchos países han duplicado sus presupuestos militares. Los casi 3 billones de dólares que el mundo destina cada año a defensa se acercan al producto interior bruto de todo el continente africano. Incluso países con una larga tradición de contención militar, como Alemania, Dinamarca, Japón y Noruega, se están rearmando a toda velocidad, y algunos están recuperando el servicio militar.

Aún más estremecedor es el regreso de la amenaza de guerra nuclear. Los nueve Estados con armas nucleares están modernizando sus arsenales y aumentando su capacidad destructiva. Se calcula que el arsenal de China ha pasado de entre unas 200-300 cabezas nucleares a finales de la década de 2010 a 620 este año. Corea del Norte también ha ampliado el suyo, de entre 10-20 cabezas estimadas en 2018 a unas 60 este año, y ha desarrollado plataformas de lanzamiento cada vez más sofisticadas. En marzo, Francia anunció el primer aumento de su número de cabezas nucleares desde 1992. Estados Unidos y Rusia, las dos mayores potencias nucleares del mundo, no han sido capaces de preservar el último tratado que ponía límites a sus arsenales estratégicos. Y cada vez más potencias no nucleares han empezado a plantearse adquirir armas nucleares propias.

Al mismo tiempo, la inteligencia artificial (IA) está dotando a la guerra de armas cada vez más peligrosas. La supervisión humana se reduce progresivamente a medida que la IA permite automatizar ciberataques contra redes eléctricas, hospitales y sistemas financieros. Hay sistemas de IA que ya han logrado infiltrarse en organismos públicos y han hecho posible la producción masiva de deepfakes y de campañas de desinformación.

La integración de la IA en las tecnologías nucleares es una amenaza existencial: al acelerar la toma de decisiones, comprime el tiempo disponible para el juicio humano. Del mismo modo, el uso de la IA en biotecnología podría rebajar drásticamente las barreras a la experimentación nociva y al desarrollo de armas biológicas. En ambos terrenos, esta tecnología está elevando a niveles sin precedentes el riesgo de errores de cálculo, de escaladas involuntarias y de destrucción masiva.

En conjunto, estas dinámicas multiplican las probabilidades de que cualquier crisis desemboque rápidamente en la aniquilación del planeta. Por desgracia, los países están fabricando los instrumentos de la catástrofe a la velocidad vertiginosa de la innovación, mientras que los instrumentos de control avanzan al ritmo, mucho más lento, de la diplomacia.

Los cambios políticos están alejando al mundo de la paz. Lo empujan hacia la militarización y la confrontación. Un mundo multipolar no es, por definición, más peligroso. Pero la erosión de las normas y de las instituciones que deberían encauzar las tensiones entre los distintos polos está provocando que el mundo se vuelva especialmente volátil. La competencia entre grandes potencias se intensifica: las ansias de hegemonía alientan políticas exteriores agresivas y los Estados revisionistas desafían las normas y las fronteras establecidas. El nacionalismo beligerante crece. Los movimientos de extrema derecha promueven una visión del mundo en la que las relaciones internacionales son un juego de suma cero y los países deben competir en lugar de cooperar. Incluso dirigentes de centro están adoptando la retórica de la guerra. Aunque no pretendan iniciar una, sus palabras abonan una mentalidad militarista peligrosa.

En este clima de rivalidad, las guerras de agresión se envuelven en discursos de necesidad, liberación, legítima defensa o justicia histórica. Los agresores presentan sus operaciones como inevitables, después de haber demonizado a sus adversarios para justificar un ataque preventivo. Venden cada guerra como la respuesta a la inseguridad: la guerra que acabará con todas las guerras.

Pero estas guerras no harán del mundo un lugar más seguro. Cada vez que un Estado desata una guerra de agresión, erosiona las normas que protegen a todos los países frente al uso arbitrario de la fuerza, incluido el propio Estado agresor.

Las guerras tienen un coste inmenso. Destrozan sociedades y truncan vidas. En 2025, según los registros de Naciones Unidas, al menos un civil murió en conflicto armado cada 14 minutos, y más de cinco millones de personas se vieron obligadas a huir de sus países. El coste económico también es altísimo. La guerra que enfrenta a Estados Unidos e Israel con Irán ha alterado gravemente el comercio marítimo y el tráfico aéreo y ha disparado los precios de la energía, los fertilizantes y otras materias primas. Millones de personas en todo el mundo están sufriendo las consecuencias.

La guerra genera, además, nuevos peligros. Una vez que empiezan los combates, su rumbo es imprevisible. Los conflictos tienden a arrastrar a nuevos actores y a desbordarse hacia los países vecinos. La guerra de Irán vuelve a ser un ejemplo claro. Desde febrero de 2026, Irán ha atacado objetivos en varios países del Golfo y de su entorno, acciones que España ha condenado sin excepción. Los ataques contra buques civiles y mercantes amenazan la estabilidad del conjunto de la región. Y la guerra ha reavivado los conflictos de Irak, el Líbano y Yemen.

Por último, las guerras rara vez traen una paz duradera. Al contrario: ahondan los agravios y enquistan las divisiones que alimentan la violencia del mañana. La guerra de Rusia en Ucrania, por ejemplo, ha dinamitado la confianza en Moscú. El genocidio en Gaza ha exacerbado la indignación contra Israel, y eso podría desembocar en nuevas oleadas de terrorismo. Y la guerra contra Irán ha desestabilizado aún más la región y ha lastrado la cooperación entre sus países.

El mundo tiene la responsabilidad de resistirse a la normalización de la guerra. Callar es un acto de cobardía y de complicidad. La sociedad española está dejando clara su posición: tres de cada cuatro españoles señalan la guerra como su mayor temor. Y un número aún mayor cree que se cierne otra guerra mundial.

Pero no basta con condenar la guerra en abstracto. La ambición de acabar con ella debe ir acompañada de acciones concretas que hagan cada vez más difícil que los países inicien, sostengan y justifiquen un conflicto armado.

España ha procurado actuar en esa dirección. Ha defendido las normas internacionales que marcan la diferencia entre un orden basado en reglas y otro gobernado por la fuerza. Y ha denunciado siempre los ataques armados que violan la Carta de Naciones Unidas.

Desde el primer día de la guerra en Gaza, España ha reclamado un alto el fuego inmediato y permanente, la liberación de todos los rehenes y la entrada sin restricciones de la ayuda humanitaria. En mayo de 2024, España reconoció el Estado de Palestina con la esperanza de que este paso contribuyera a avanzar hacia una solución negociada y duradera del conflicto. Y ha apoyado activamente los esfuerzos de la Corte Internacional de Justicia y de la Corte Penal Internacional para que los dirigentes israelíes rindan cuentas por sus violaciones del derecho internacional humanitario. Israel tenía derecho a la legítima defensa tras los atentados terroristas del 7 de octubre. Pero ese derecho no justifica el genocidio en Gaza ni la expansión de la ocupación ilegal de Cisjordania.

Disuadir la guerra exige también elevar el coste de la agresión. El resto del mundo debe asegurarse de que quien elija hacer la guerra no pueda hacerlo sin pagar un precio. A lo largo de la invasión rusa de Ucrania, por ejemplo, la Unión Europea ha aprobado sucesivas rondas de sanciones contra Rusia que España ha respaldado sin titubeos. España también ha prestado a Ucrania ayuda financiera, humanitaria y militar para que pueda defender su territorio y negarle a Rusia el botín de guerra.

Los países deben negarse a ayudar a las partes a extender los conflictos en curso. España, por ejemplo, mantiene un embargo de armas a Israel desde 2023. Este año, además, negó a Estados Unidos el uso de su territorio y de sus instalaciones para la guerra contra Irán. La decisión de España no fue un acto de hostilidad hacia un aliado. Tampoco fue una expresión de neutralidad ni de simpatía hacia el régimen iraní. Fue el fruto de una valoración estratégica: implicarse habría favorecido la escalada del conflicto y agravado la inseguridad regional y mundial. Para ser fiel a su compromiso con la paz, España tenía que estar dispuesta a decir no, aunque eso tuviera un coste político. Si más países hubieran actuado igual, las guerras de Gaza y de Irán habrían sido mucho más difíciles y costosas de librar.

Invertir en prosperidad es igualmente esencial. La seguridad crece cuando las sociedades son resilientes, las desigualdades se reducen y la ciudadanía puede confiar en las instituciones públicas. La decisión de España, el año pasado, de no asumir el objetivo de gasto en defensa del 5 % del PIB propuesto por la OTAN refleja esta visión. No nace de una falta de compromiso con la defensa europea. De hecho, mi Gobierno ha sido uno de los principales impulsores de la autonomía estratégica de la Unión Europea, triplicando el gasto neto en defensa en los ocho años que llevo en el cargo. Pero la disuasión militar y el bienestar social deben reforzarse mutuamente. Al mantener un gasto en defensa proporcionado, España ha conservado el margen fiscal necesario para invertir 145.000 millones de euros adicionales en protección social, vivienda, sanidad y educación, y más de 30.000 millones en la transición ecológica. Son políticas que cohesionan la sociedad y, por tanto, la hacen más segura.

Los esfuerzos de un solo país tienen, sin embargo, un alcance limitado. Cortarle el oxígeno a la guerra exige la implicación de todos: un esfuerzo colectivo basado en un multilateralismo genuino. Esta es la única estrategia que ha funcionado en el pasado. El sistema multilateral construido tras la Segunda Guerra Mundial abarcaba todo el planeta y contribuyó a preservar la paz entre las grandes potencias y a evitar una tercera guerra mundial.

Reforzarlo no será tarea fácil. La pugna geopolítica y una desconfianza generalizada han debilitado a Naciones Unidas, los organismos financieros internacionales, las organizaciones regionales y otras instituciones multilaterales. Muchos países las perciben como ineficaces y atrapadas en una crisis perpetua, de modo que están recurriendo a marcos alternativos para intentar poner fin a las guerras.

Algunos han optado por enfoques unilaterales, persiguiendo acuerdos transaccionales que acaban beneficiando a élites económicas y políticas y dejan al margen a las poblaciones afectadas por el conflicto. Los altos el fuego o los acuerdos de paz que logran pueden ser preferibles a la continuación de la guerra, pero cualquier paz impuesta por la ley del más fuerte, y no por el derecho, se asienta sobre cimientos frágiles.

Otros países, frustrados con el multilateralismo, han intentado formar «coaliciones de voluntarios» u otros grupos «minilaterales» para garantizar su seguridad o alcanzar acuerdos ad hoc en materia de comercio o tecnología. Este enfoque puede ser una solución temporal a la parálisis de las instituciones tradicionales, pero su carácter excluyente fragmenta aún más un orden internacional ya dividido. Lo que necesitamos no es minilateralismo, sino maxilateralismo. La cooperación internacional alcanza su verdadera fuerza cuando es—o aspira a ser—universal. Dejar a una parte del mundo fuera de estos esfuerzos es, además, incubar la confrontación de mañana.

El sistema internacional del siglo XX no era perfecto. Sus normas se han aplicado de manera desigual, sus principios se han defendido de forma inconsistente y sus procedimientos han sido desesperantemente lentos. Pero es innegable que, sin él, el mundo sería un polvorín aún mayor. Basta un ejemplo: fue una red de tratados internacionales—entre ellos, el Tratado sobre la No Proliferación de las Armas Nucleares, el Tratado sobre Fuerzas Nucleares de Alcance Intermedio y el Tratado de Prohibición Parcial de los Ensayos Nucleares—la que puso fin a una carrera armamentística desbocada y extraordinariamente costosa que estaba llevando al mundo al borde de una guerra nuclear.

Toda esta arquitectura institucional se está resquebrajando. Y sin ella, aspirar a preservar la paz es una quimera.

Fortalecer las instituciones multilaterales debe ser el eje central de una campaña global para relegar la guerra a los libros de historia. Para ello será necesario acometer reformas de calado que les devuelvan la legitimidad y refuercen su capacidad de actuación, dando prioridad a las medidas que más contribuyan a la paz y la seguridad.

La primera pasa por aumentar la representación en las Naciones Unidas y las instituciones de Bretton Woods. Actualizar la estructura del Consejo de Seguridad de Naciones Unidas es especialmente importante, dada su responsabilidad principal de mantener la paz internacional. Un Consejo de Seguridad con asientos permanentes y derecho de veto para las cinco potencias vencedoras de la Segunda Guerra Mundial, que en conjunto suman apenas una cuarta parte de la población mundial, no refleja el mundo de hoy. Debe ampliarse el número de miembros electos del Consejo y alargar su mandato más allá de los dos años actuales. Los nuevos asientos deben otorgarse principalmente a países de regiones infrarrepresentadas, en particular África y América Latina. Organizaciones regionales como la Unión Europea y la Unión Africana también deberían estar representadas en el Consejo, posiblemente ocupando puestos que hoy corresponden a Estados. Un Consejo de Seguridad más representativo no solo sería más justo, sino también más eficaz. Los países defienden y hacen cumplir con más empeño las normas internacionales cuando se sienten autores de ellas.

También hay que restringir estrictamente el poder de veto de los miembros permanentes, en especial cuando el Consejo de Seguridad delibere sobre casos de atrocidades masivas, genocidio, crímenes de lesa humanidad y crímenes de guerra. En última instancia, el veto debe abolirse por completo. Solo entonces podrá el Consejo responder a todos los actos de agresión sin quedar secuestrado por las objeciones de un solo Estado.

Un espíritu similar debería orientar la transformación estructural de las instituciones financieras internacionales, en particular el Fondo Monetario Internacional y el Banco Mundial. La distribución del poder de voto en ambas sigue reflejando los equilibrios económicos del siglo XX y, por convención, un europeo está siempre al frente del FMI y un estadounidense, del Banco Mundial. Esto debe terminar. Las economías emergentes necesitan una representación más adecuada, y los puestos de liderazgo deben estar abiertos a todos los candidatos, sea cual sea su nacionalidad. También deberán evolucionar las responsabilidades que asumen en instituciones como la Organización Mundial del Comercio los países antes considerados «en desarrollo», a medida que crecen y se incorporan al grupo de las economías más ricas. Una gobernanza económica más justa aliviaría la desigualdad y el resentimiento que con tanta frecuencia atizan la inestabilidad y los conflictos.

Otra reforma esencial consiste en revitalizar el marco internacional que contiene el riesgo de escalada nuclear. Parte de ese trabajo depende de las potencias nucleares. Corresponde a Estados Unidos y a Rusia negociar un tratado que suceda al acuerdo bilateral Nuevo START y que preserve topes verificables al despliegue de armas nucleares. Y, esta vez, China debería participar en las conversaciones.

Pero el esfuerzo más amplio exige el compromiso de todos los países. Entre sus prioridades deben figurar la ratificación del Tratado de Prohibición Completa de los Ensayos Nucleares, para impedir que se reanuden las pruebas nucleares, y el avance de las negociaciones sobre un Tratado de Prohibición de la Producción de Material Fisionable, que prohibiría la producción de uranio altamente enriquecido y plutonio para armas nucleares. Todos deben también trabajar conjuntamente para regular el uso de la IA en el ámbito militar, especialmente en lo que respecta a las armas nucleares, a fin de preservar el control humano sobre las decisiones de lanzamiento y minimizar el riesgo de errores fatales. Los países no nucleares deben comprometerse a no adquirir armas nucleares y deben emplear todos los medios a su alcance para instar a las potencias nucleares a reducir sus arsenales, reforzar los mecanismos de verificación y reconstruir las salvaguardas diplomáticas que durante décadas han ayudado a evitar una catástrofe nuclear.

La tecnología puede contribuir en parte a esta tarea. Los avances en imágenes por satélite, sensores remotos y análisis de datos pueden aumentar la transparencia y la supervisión del cumplimiento de los acuerdos de control de armamentos. Pero también hay que hacer mucho más para evitar situaciones que puedan precipitar una guerra. Si los Estados con armas nucleares acuerdan reducir su disponibilidad operacional y reforzar la comunicación en momentos de tensión, los gobiernos contarán con más tiempo y más medios para desactivar las crisis. Cada país debe proteger sus sistemas de mando y control frente a los ciberataques y la manipulación informativa impulsada por la IA, de modo que una falsa alarma o un error de cálculo no desencadenen jamás una guerra nuclear.

Por último, es prioritario trabajar para garantizar que el sistema de justicia internacional pueda hacer cumplir las decisiones de los tribunales. Desmantelar la Corte Penal Internacional, como pretenden algunos países, solo aseguraría la impunidad de los crímenes más atroces. La CPI no solo debe investigar y juzgar los casos de genocidio, agresión, crímenes de guerra y crímenes de lesa humanidad, sino también contar con la autoridad necesaria para llevar ante la justicia a los responsables de esos crímenes. Cuando un Estado pasa a ser parte del Estatuto de Roma, el tratado que creó la CPI, acepta someterse a la jurisdicción de la Corte. Sin embargo, si ese Estado se niega a cooperar o a entregar a un sospechoso, no existe una sanción efectiva, sino apenas una llamada de atención. Esto debe cambiar. Los Estados que den cobijo a personas imputadas por la CPI deben afrontar consecuencias legales, diplomáticas y financieras. Iniciar y librar una guerra debe tener un coste real.

La reforma de las instituciones internacionales lleva años estancada, pero la próxima elección para la Secretaría General de Naciones Unidas ofrece una ocasión excepcional para darle un nuevo impulso. Que Naciones Unidas nombrara a su primera secretaria general sería un poderoso símbolo de renovación, inclusión y adaptación institucional. La organización no puede afirmar verdaderamente que habla en nombre de toda la humanidad mientras no la haya dirigido una mujer. Alcanzar este hito podría despertar una nueva ambición y reforzar la confianza en la capacidad de las instituciones internacionales para reformarse y para que recuperen su papel a la hora de poner freno a la guerra.

Frenar la deriva del mundo hacia la guerra exigirá el compromiso más amplio posible. Ningún país, grande o pequeño, puede mirar hacia otro lado ni sumarse a las acciones que aviven la escalada. Todos debemos defender las normas internacionales que hoy ponen coto a la guerra y reformar y dotar de poder a las instituciones que mañana la harán imposible.

En última instancia, lo que hará inimaginable la guerra es una agenda de paz positiva que aborde las causas profundas de la inseguridad. Cada paso hacia la erradicación de la pobreza, la ampliación del acceso a una educación de calidad, el avance de la igualdad de género, el fortalecimiento de las instituciones democráticas y el aumento de la resiliencia ante los desastres naturales atenúa también las condiciones que engendran la violencia.

Las instituciones financieras internacionales deben ser capaces de proporcionar financiación con rapidez, a gran escala y a un coste razonable, para que ningún país en desarrollo se vea obligado a elegir entre atender su deuda e invertir en su población. Los bancos multilaterales de desarrollo deben poder movilizar fondos para las transiciones ecológica y digital, de modo que las migraciones climáticas y la disputa por los recursos no se conviertan en detonantes de conflictos entre Estados. Y el Acuerdo sobre Pandemias de la Organización Mundial de la Salud debe ultimarse y entrar en vigor para garantizar que todos los países puedan acceder a vacunas y tratamientos cuando llegue la próxima emergencia sanitaria mundial.

Por eso España ha procurado situar el desarrollo en el centro de su política exterior. El Gobierno español ha sido un firme defensor de los Objetivos de Desarrollo Sostenible de la ONU para 2030 y, el año pasado, la ciudad de Sevilla acogió la Cuarta Conferencia Internacional sobre la Financiación para el Desarrollo, donde la comunidad internacional se comprometió a aligerar la carga de la deuda de los países vulnerables y a desbloquear inversiones para el desarrollo sostenible. España también ha promovido iniciativas como el Centro Global para Canjes de Deuda por Desarrollo, lanzado junto con el Banco Mundial; la Alianza multilateral por las Cláusulas de Suspensión de Deuda, que permite a los países vulnerables suspender el pago de su deuda tras una catástrofe; y un mecanismo propio para ofrecer hasta 60 millones de euros anuales en alivio de deuda a países en desarrollo. Además, España ha respaldado, junto con instituciones multilaterales y organizaciones internacionales, iniciativas de financiación climática, salud global, igualdad de género, seguridad alimentaria y protección social. Estas políticas de desarrollo son también políticas de construcción de paz.

Un proyecto ambicioso de movilización global en favor del derecho internacional y de un multilateralismo genuino puede parecer poco realista. Pero ya hemos visto lo que ocurre cuando la normalización de la guerra arrastra a la humanidad al infierno. Los hombres y las mujeres que se levantaron de entre las ruinas de la Segunda Guerra Mundial, el capítulo más oscuro de nuestra historia, encontraron la manera de construir un orden mundial multilateral. La pregunta a la que se enfrenta nuestra generación es si seremos capaces de preservar el imperio de la ley o permitiremos el regreso de la ley de la jungla. Si construiremos el mundo del mañana con visión de futuro y cooperación, o a través del caos y del dolor de otra guerra mundial. Debemos elegir bien mientras la decisión siga estando en nuestras manos.

#5916. The EU’s First AI Summit

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: The EU’s First AI Summit

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the European Union’s artificial intelligence ambitions, dual car bombings in Russia, and an alleged Iranian strike on a U.S. radar system.

European Union leaders convened in Dublin on Friday for the bloc’s first-ever summit on artificial intelligence. With the continent expected to fall short of its chip-manufacturing goals, the conference may be a pivotal opportunity for the EU to develop a unified policy that aims to catch up with the world’s AI powerhouses: the United States and China.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the European Union’s artificial intelligence ambitions, dual car bombings in Russia, and an alleged Iranian strike on a U.S. radar system.

European Union leaders convened in Dublin on Friday for the bloc’s first-ever summit on artificial intelligence. With the continent expected to fall short of its chip-manufacturing goals, the conference may be a pivotal opportunity for the EU to develop a unified policy that aims to catch up with the world’s AI powerhouses: the United States and China.

The 2023 European Chips Act sought to double the continent’s semiconductor production global market share from roughly 10 percent to 20 percent by 2030 and attract billions of dollars in foreign tech investment. This legislation was a way for Europeans to say, “we want to be serious about this, and the only way to be competitive is to engage in the same practices, because otherwise you’re just never going to get manufacturing back to your shores,” Bruce Andrews, then-Intel’s corporate vice president, told FP’s Rishi Iyengar in 2024.

Since then, though, the EU has emerged as a more passive actor in AI’s advancement. A March report by Eurostat, the bloc’s statistics office, found that 1 in 5 EU enterprises used at least one AI technology in 2025, compared to less than 1 in 10 businesses a few years ago. Yet, in installed data center capacity alone, the bloc, with its 11.92 gigawatts, remains double-digits behind the United States (53.73 gigawatts) and China (31.9 gigawatts).

Such stagnation has EU leaders concerned that the continent is being left behind. “AI is the most important technology of our time,” European Council ‌President António Costa wrote on X on Friday. “Europe must make the most of its benefits, from higher productivity to better public services. We must also manage the risks and changes it will bring—and help set global standards for responsible governance and safe, human-centric AI.”

Yet the summit’s AI agenda may be derailed by other issues—particularly, who will represent European interests in future Russia-Ukraine peace talks.

On Thursday, Russian President Vladimir Putin told U.S. special envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner that he would allow European officials to join the next round of negotiations. Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, who is also in Dublin this week for talks with EU leadership, applauded Putin’s concession. However, who will represent the continent’s delegation remains unclear.

“If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiation,” Kushner said on Thursday. Among the names floated as possible options are Costa, EU foreign-policy chief Kaja Kallas, Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk, and Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni.

The resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is also likely to steal the summit’s attention. Huang stepped down on Thursday over his alleged extramarital affairs, including one that he admitted to with a suspected Chinese spy. Nvidia is the world’s leading producer of the advanced semiconductor chips used to power AI and data centers.

Assassination attempts. Russian authorities are investigating a series of assassination attempts this week, after two separate car bombings in as many days targeted senior pro-Kremlin officials. It is unclear who was responsible for the incidents; however, Ukraine’s intelligence agency has previously used similar operations to attack dozens of Russian officers and operatives. Kyiv has claimed responsibility for a few of these operations, including the killing of Lt. Gen. Igor Kirillov in December 2024, but it has not commented on this week’s bombings.

On Thursday, local media reported an explosion in St. Petersburg’s Pushkinsky District, an area known for housing Russian military personnel. The attack killed a senior Russian military official believed to be a colonel in the country’s air force. The following day, a car bomb in Moscow severely wounded Sergei Zavorotniy, a former senior aide to exiled former Ukrainian Prime Minister Mykola Azarov, who fled Kyiv in 2014 after Ukraine’s Maidan revolution toppled the country’s pro-Russian president.

Both attacks occurred following a new round of U.S.-mediated Russia-Ukraine peace talks in Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates. Those negotiations made little headway, apart from an agreement to exchange prisoners. However, Putin later expressed his willingness to hold a future round of dialogue with European representatives present.

Alleged U.S.-Iran strikes. Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) claimed on Friday to have destroyed a U.S. early-warning radar system in Bahrain in what would be Tehran’s first direct attack on a U.S. military installation in months. The IRGC said the operation was in retaliation for a recent U.S. airstrike on an Iranian communications tower overlooking the Strait of Hormuz. U.S. Central Command, however, denied that its radar system was damaged, instead claiming to have intercepted two Iranian drones that were heading toward Bahrain.

The alleged radar system strike came the same day that a tanker carrying liquified natural gas (LNG) reportedly hit a naval mine in the strait. The IRGC navy told state media that the ship was using an “unauthorized and dangerous route” when it came into contact with the underwater explosive and that it had ignored repeated warnings to avoid the area.

As traffic through the critical passageway has started to return, the number of maritime incidents has also climbed. October was the busiest month for LNG exports from the Persian Gulf region since war first broke out in late February. According to the Washington Institute for Near East Policy, though, last month also saw the highest number of attacks on ships in the strait.

Vance’s new mandate. U.S. President Donald Trump directed Vice President J.D. Vance to move off the Iran portfolio on Thursday and instead focus his “complete and total attention” on alleged corruption in Democratic Party-run states and cities. The shift comes after Vance blamed the administration’s handling of the Iran war for Democrats’ sweeping wins in recent midterm elections.

Vance has long had a heavy hand in U.S.-Iran negotiations. Despite being a skeptic of the war, the vice president has helped shape the conflict’s objectives, led the charge for a precarious memorandum of understanding, and engaged with top Iranian hard-liners to try to revive peace talks. Moving him off the Iran agenda therefore marks a significant shift in how Trump appears to want to handle the conflict.

“We don’t need a deal with Iran,” Trump wrote on Truth Social on Thursday. “Those days have long since passed. We’ve got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we’ve got their Strait!”

The change-up also signals the White House’s concerns about a looming Democrat-run Congress. Incoming Democratic lawmakers have vowed to investigate Trump’s business dealings and other potential avenues of corruption when they take power in January. In response on Thursday, Trump accused Democratic Party leadership—particularly in the states of California, Illinois, and Minnesota—of stealing billions of dollars every month, without citing evidence.

On Sunday, South African authorities announced the results from Nov. 4 local elections. Why was the election a historic one for the African National Congress party?

A. The party won a majority in every province except one. B. It marked the party’s worst-ever results. C. The party won a majority in the Western Cape province for the first time. D. Two-thirds of the party’s election winners were women.

Finland already has a reputation for being the happiest country in the world. Now, Helsinki is hoping to share that wealth with 16 lucky singles from around the world. In February, Finland will host a four-day, food-oriented group date alongside local Finnish residents also looking for love. Participants will taste some of the country’s most iconic dishes, from sautéed reindeer to bread cheese; relax in traditional saunas; and explore the Nordic nation’s cultural landmarks.

FP’s World Brief writer can personally attest that the Finnish sauna experience alone provides an almost unparalleled level of relaxation. Just follow the golden rule: What is said in the sauna stays in the sauna.

B. It marked the party’s worst-ever results.

No party won an outright majority, a further sign that South Africa’s political landscape is fragmenting, FP’s Nosmot Gbadamosi reports in Africa Brief.

#5913. Democrat flips Alaska governor's mansion after Trump-backed GOP infighting

After a midterm rally in Alaska, President Donald Trump told Fox News' Matt Finn "we are looking into" Dan Sullivan, an independent challenger who goes by the same name as the Republican incumbent, Sen. Dan Sullivan.

Democrat Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins has won Alaska's gubernatorial election, defeating Trump-endorsed Republican Bernadette Wilson and flipping the governor's mansion.

The Associated Press called the race for Kreiss-Tomkins, a former state lawmaker, Friday evening local time. He is the first Democratic governor in Alaska since 1998.

Alaska's gubernatorial election became an unusual contest as Republicans battled among themselves over who should succeed Gov. Mike Dunleavy, who was barred from seeking a third consecutive term.

A BLUE WAVE IN GOVERNORS' MANSIONS COULD HURT FAMILIES MORE THAN A BLUE CONGRESS

Former State Representative Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, a Democrat from Alaska, during the What's Next conference in Denver, Colorado, on Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. (Chet Strange/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Wilson, a conservative activist and former political operative, received President Donald Trump's endorsement during a campaign in which multiple Republicans competed for the governor's office.

Trump publicly criticized fellow Republicans Dave Bronson and Treg Taylor, accusing them of dividing conservative voters and potentially jeopardizing the party's hold on the governorship.

Bronson, a former Anchorage mayor, and Taylor, a former Alaska attorney general, remained part of the Republican field, creating a complicated general-election contest.

ALASKAN POLITICAL SCION HOPES TO HOLD ONTO STATE'S SINGLE HOUSE SEAT

Alaska gubernatorial candidate Bernadette Wilson was the only Trump-endorsed candidate in the race, despite being one of three Republican options. (Courtesy: Bernadette Wilson for Governor)

Kreiss-Tomkins, a former state representative from Sitka, emerged as the Democratic contender, seeking to return his party to the governor's mansion after Dunleavy's two terms.

The election unfolded under Alaska's ranked-choice voting system, which allows voters to rank candidates in order of preference.

If no candidate receives a majority of first-choice votes, the lowest-performing candidate is eliminated and those ballots are redistributed according to voters' next preferences. The process continues until a candidate secures a majority among continuing ballots.

The system has added an unusual dynamic to Alaska politics, allowing multiple candidates from the same party to compete in the general election without a traditional partisan runoff.

Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy speaks at a rally in Wasilla, Alaska, in October 2026. President Trump endorsed GOP gubernatorial candidate Bernadette Wilson at this rally, but Dunleavy did not offer an endorsement for any candidate. (Nathaniel Wilder/Getty Images)

The contest also came as Alaska remained a reliably Republican state in presidential elections, despite occasionally electing Democrats and independents to statewide office.

#5911. The World’s Last Chance for Peace

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PEDRO SÁNCHEZ is President of the Government of Spain.

At some point during the last decade, the world started accepting war as inevitable. Wars of aggression, from Ukraine to Lebanon to Iran, are on the rise, bringing horror, ruin, and the specter of a wider international confrontation. Governments increasingly treat war as something to budget and prepare for. Military spending is climbing, technological and arms races are intensifying, and more and more countries are considering whether they should acquire a nuclear deterrent to guarantee their security.

Nothing is more dangerous than the normalization of war. It represents a return to a norm that persisted for most of human history, when war was the main organizing principle of the international system. Armed conflict was the natural way for states to settle disputes or enlarge their territory at the expense of weaker neighbors.

Yet the vast wreckage of World Wars I and II in the first half of the twentieth century made the status quo unacceptable. Countries united behind an effort to turn war from a legitimate instrument of statecraft into an outlawed act. Wars did not end in 1945, but for years after they were overwhelmingly condemned by the international community.

In recent decades, however, governments’ commitment to the cause of peace has weakened. Millions of people took to the streets in hundreds of cities to oppose the U.S.-led invasion of Iraq in 2003, but the war proceeded nonetheless. Fought on false premises and in violation of international law, it brought destruction to Iraq, fed regional instability, and fueled resentment and terrorism directed at Western countries. The conflict also marked a turning point. Ever since, the pursuit of peace has steadily receded from the international agenda amid deepening geopolitical competition and inattention or indifference to the multilateral efforts that for decades discouraged acts of war.

At the end of this path lies another world war—the catastrophe the twentieth-century order was designed to prevent. With that dire future looming, governments must relearn the art of opposing war. It is the responsibility of every country, including my own, Spain, to take direct action that constrains others’ ability to wage war and to strengthen the international institutions that will serve as guarantors of peace.

The success of this project will depend on an expansive multilateralism that I call “maxilateralism”—cooperation that embraces as many countries as possible, rather than being limited to small, ad hoc groupings. Its ambition is to make war increasingly impractical, even unimaginable. In 2003, protesters across Spain chanted “No a la guerra!’’—“No to war!” The world cannot afford to ignore them again.

International efforts to reject war as an instrument of national policy date back to the Kellogg–Briand Pact of 1928, a treaty signed in Paris by 15 countries and joined by dozens more later. It was insufficient to prevent World War II, but it planted a seed. When representatives of 50 countries gathered in San Francisco in 1945 to create the United Nations, they revived the spirit and principles of Kellogg–Briand and gave them what they had lacked: institutional form. The UN Charter prohibited the threat or use of force against the territorial integrity or political independence of another state. And, unlike its 1928 predecessor, it backed up the prohibition with institutions empowered to enforce it.

In the decades that followed, these institutions established an order that penalized violations and created incentives for diplomacy and cooperation. At the same time, public opposition to war was growing stronger around the world. Popular defiance of Cold War militarism was visible in worldwide resistance to the Vietnam War and in mass movements against nuclear weapons. Pacifism reached its high-water mark in the 1990s, when a vibrant global civil society mobilized to defend humanitarian causes and when the United Nations held sufficient authority to champion a sweeping peace agenda.

Today, however, there is more armed conflict than at any time since 1946. According to the Uppsala Conflict Data Program, there were 65 active conflicts involving governments in 2025, double the number in 2010. An estimated 250,000 people died in those conflicts—the highest annual death toll in 30 years, since the Rwandan genocide. Close to 120 million people worldwide have been forcibly displaced by war.

Worryingly, wars of aggression—which violate the UN Charter—are back. Russia invaded Ukraine. The United States and Israel attacked Iran. And as fighting continues year after year in places such as Lebanon, Sudan, and Yemen, it is becoming clear that war is being accepted as a normal occurrence.

There is no sign of the trend slowing down. Military budgets have surged to levels not seen in generations, at the expense of social and development spending. In the past decade alone, global defense spending has increased by more than 40 percent, with many countries doubling their military budgets. The nearly $3 trillion the world spends annually on defense is close to the GDP of the entire African continent. Even countries with a long tradition of military restraint, such as Denmark, Germany, Japan, and Norway, are rearming rapidly. Some are reviving military service programs.

A renewed threat of nuclear war is cause for still greater dread. All nine nuclear-armed states are modernizing their arsenals, boosting their destructive capabilities. China’s nuclear arsenal is estimated to have increased from 200 to 300 warheads in the late 2010s to 620 this year. North Korea has also expanded its arsenal from an estimated ten to 20 warheads in 2018 to around 60 this year, and it has developed increasingly sophisticated launch platforms. In March, France announced the first increase in its nuclear warhead stockpile since 1992. The United States and Russia, the world’s two largest nuclear powers, have failed to preserve the last remaining treaty constraining their strategic arsenals. And a growing number of nonnuclear powers have started to consider acquiring arsenals of their own.

Simultaneously, artificial intelligence is powering ever more dangerous weapons of war. Human oversight is being progressively reduced as AI makes it possible to automate cyberattacks against power grids, hospitals, and financial systems. AI systems have hacked government bodies and enabled the mass production of deepfakes and disinformation campaigns.

The integration of AI into nuclear technologies poses an existential threat: as AI speeds up decision-making, it reduces the time available for human judgment. Similarly, the use of AI in biotechnology could dramatically lower barriers to harmful experimentation and the development of bioweapons. In both areas, this technology is elevating the risk of miscalculation, unintended escalation, and mass destruction to a level higher than ever before.

Together, these dynamics are multiplying the chances that any crisis will quickly spiral into planetary annihilation. Countries are building the instruments of catastrophe at the blinding speed of innovation while the instruments of control move at the much slower pace of diplomacy.

Political change is pushing the world away from peace and toward confrontation and militarization. A multipolar world is not, by definition, a more dangerous one, but the erosion of rules and institutions that manage tensions between poles has made today’s world particularly volatile. Competition among great powers is increasing, with hegemonic ambitions encouraging assertive foreign policies and revisionist states challenging established rules and borders. Belligerent nationalism is on the rise, and far-right movements are advancing a worldview in which international relations is a zero-sum game and countries must compete rather than cooperate. Even centrist leaders are embracing the language of war preparedness. Although they do not intend to start a war, their words feed a dangerous militaristic mindset.

In this context of rivalry, wars of aggression come wrapped in narratives of necessity, liberation, self-defense, or historical justice. The attackers present their operations as inevitable, having demonized their targets to justify preemptive action against them. They describe each war as an answer to insecurity—a war to end all wars.

But these wars will not make a safer world. Every time a state embarks on a war of aggression, it erodes the norms that protect all countries from the arbitrary use of force—including the aggressor itself.

Wars exact immense costs. They shatter societies and interrupt lives. In 2025, at least one civilian died in an armed conflict every 14 minutes, according to UN records, and more than five million people were forced to flee their countries. The economic costs are high, too; the U.S. and Israeli war with Iran has severely disrupted maritime trade and air traffic, driving up the prices of energy, fertilizer, and other commodities, and millions of people across the world are suffering the consequences.

War also creates new dangers. Once the fighting begins, its course is unpredictable. Conflict tends to draw in new actors and spill into neighboring countries. The war in Iran serves, again, as a prime example. Since February 2026, Iran has struck targets in several Gulf countries and other neighbors—actions Spain has consistently condemned. Attacks on civilian and commercial vessels threaten broader regional stability. And the war has reignited conflicts in Iraq, Lebanon, and Yemen.

Finally, wars rarely produce lasting peace. Instead, they deepen grievances and entrench divisions that fuel future violence. Russia’s war in Ukraine, for instance, has destroyed trust in Moscow. The genocide in Gaza has inflamed anger toward Israel, potentially leading to new waves of terrorism. And the war against Iran has further destabilized the Middle East, hindering cooperation across the region.

The world has a responsibility to resist the normalization of wars. Remaining silent is an act of cowardice and complicity. Spanish society is making its position clear: three of four Spaniards identify war as their greatest fear, and an even greater number believe another world war is on the horizon.

But it is not enough to simply condemn war in principle. The ambition of ending war must be matched with action that makes it increasingly difficult for countries to start, sustain, and justify armed conflict.

Spain has endeavored to take such action. It has defended the international laws that stand between an order governed by rules and one governed by force. Spain has consistently denounced armed attacks that violate the UN Charter.

From day one of the war in Gaza, Spain has been calling for an immediate and permanent cease-fire, the release of all hostages, and the unrestricted delivery of humanitarian assistance. In May 2024, Spain recognized the state of Palestine in the hope that this step would help advance a negotiated and lasting solution to the conflict. And Spain has actively supported the efforts of the International Court of Justice and the International Criminal Court to hold Israeli leaders accountable for violations of international humanitarian law. Israel had the right to self-defense after the October 7 terrorist attacks, but that right does not justify genocide in Gaza or the expansion of Israel’s illegal occupation of the West Bank.

Deterring war also requires raising the costs of aggression. The rest of the world must ensure that those who choose to wage war cannot do so without paying a price. Throughout Russia’s war in Ukraine, for example, the European Union has introduced a series of sanctions on Russia, which Spain has consistently supported. Spain has also provided financial, humanitarian, and military assistance to Ukraine, helping it defend its territory and deny Russia the spoils of war.

Countries must refuse to help belligerents expand existing conflicts. Spain, for instance, has maintained an arms embargo on Israel since 2023. This year, it also denied the United States the use of its territory and facilities for the war against Iran. Spain’s decision was not an act of hostility toward an ally. Nor was it an expression of neutrality or sympathy for the Iranian regime. It was, rather, a judgment that getting involved would enable escalation of the conflict, deepening regional and global insecurity. To keep its commitment to peace, Spain had to be willing to say no, even when doing so carried a political cost. Had more countries done the same, the wars in Gaza and Iran would have been far harder and costlier to wage.

Investing in prosperity is also essential. Security is strengthened when societies are resilient, inequalities are reduced, and citizens can rely on public institutions. Spain’s decision last year to opt out of NATO’s proposed defense spending target of five percent of GDP reflected this understanding. This position did not stem from a lack of commitment to European defense—in fact, my government has championed Europe’s push for strategic autonomy and has tripled net defense spending over the eight years I have been in office. But military deterrence and social welfare must reinforce each other. By keeping defense spending proportionate, Spain has preserved the fiscal space to invest an additional 145 billion euros in social protection, housing, health care, and education, as well as more than 30 billion euros in the green transition—programs that make society more cohesive and adaptable, and therefore more secure.

Yet one country’s efforts can accomplish only so much. Starving war of its lifelines requires all hands on deck, a collective effort rooted in genuine multilateralism. This is the only strategy that has worked before. The multilateral system built after World War II spanned the globe, and it helped keep peace among great powers and prevent a third world war.

Reinforcing it will not be an easy task. Geopolitical competition and widespread mistrust have weakened the United Nations, international financial bodies, regional organizations, and other multilateral institutions. Many countries perceive them as ineffective and trapped in perpetual crisis, so they have turned to alternative frameworks to try to end wars.

Some have tried unilateral approaches, pursuing transactional bargains that end up benefiting economic and political elites while sidelining the populations affected by the conflict. The cease-fires or peace deals they achieve may be preferable to continued war, but any peace imposed through power politics rather than law rests on shaky foundations.

Other countries, frustrated with multilateralism, have tried recruiting coalitions of the willing, or other “minilateral” groups, to provide security guarantees or reach ad hoc arrangements on matters of trade and technology. This approach might be a temporary solution to the paralysis of traditional institutions, but its exclusivity further fragments an already divided international order. What is needed is not minilateralism but maxilateralism. International cooperation is powerful when it is, or aspires to be, universal. Leaving part of the world outside of multilateral efforts, moreover, is a recipe for confrontation down the road.

The twentieth-century system was not perfect. The application of its rules has been uneven, its standards inconsistently upheld, its procedures frustratingly slow. But it is undeniable that the world would be much more dangerous without it. To take just one example, it was a lattice of international treaties—including the Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty, the Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty, and the Limited Test Ban Treaty—that ended a wildly expensive arms race that was driving the world to the brink of nuclear war.

All of this institutional architecture is now eroding. Yet without it, the world cannot aspire to preserve peace.

Strengthening multilateral institutions must be at the center of a global campaign to relegate war to history. This will require substantial reform to restore these institutions’ legitimacy and their capacity to act, prioritizing measures that will contribute the most to peace and security.

The first is increasing representation at the United Nations and the Bretton Woods institutions. An update to the structure of the UN Security Council is particularly important, given that body’s primary responsibility for the maintenance of international peace. A Security Council that gives permanent seats and a right to veto only to the five victors of World War II, which together account for barely a quarter of the global population, does not reflect today’s world. The council’s elected membership should be expanded and the terms of those members should be extended beyond the current two years. The new seats should be given primarily to countries in underrepresented regions, particularly Africa and Latin America. Regional organizations such as the European Union and the African Union should also be represented in the council, potentially taking over seats currently occupied by states. A more representative Security Council would not merely be fairer; it would also be more effective. Countries are more likely to defend and enforce international rules when they consider themselves to be authors of those rules.

Permanent members’ veto power must also be strictly limited, particularly when the Security Council considers cases of mass atrocities, genocide, crimes against humanity, and war crimes. Ultimately, the veto should be abolished outright. Only then can the council respond to all acts of aggression without being paralyzed by the objections of a single state.

A similar spirit should guide structural change at international financial institutions, particularly the International Monetary Fund and the World Bank. The distribution of voting power in both still reflects twentieth-century economic balances, and by convention, a European always leads the IMF and an American leads the World Bank. This must end. Emerging economies need better representation, and leadership positions should be open to all candidates regardless of nationality. Responsibilities within institutions such as the World Trade Organization should also evolve as economic growth moves countries that were once categorized as “developing” into the ranks of the wealthy. Fairer economic governance would ease the inequality and resentment that so often feed instability and conflict.

Another key reform will involve reinvigorating the international framework that limits the risk of nuclear escalation. Some of that work depends on the nuclear powers; it is up to the United States and Russia, for example, to negotiate a successor to the bilateral New START treaty that preserves verifiable limits on strategic nuclear deployments. And this time, China should be part of the discussions.

Yet the broader effort requires the engagement of all countries. Their priorities should include ratifying the Comprehensive Nuclear Test Ban Treaty to prevent a resumption of nuclear testing and advancing negotiations on a Fissile Material Cutoff Treaty, which would ban production of highly enriched uranium and plutonium for nuclear weapons. All countries must also work together to regulate the use of AI in the military domain, especially when it comes to nuclear weapons, to preserve human control over launch decisions and reduce the risk of catastrophic errors. Nonnuclear powers should commit to not acquiring nuclear weapons themselves, and they should use every tool at their disposal to encourage nuclear powers to reduce their arsenals, strengthen verification mechanisms, and rebuild the diplomatic guardrails that for decades have helped prevent nuclear catastrophe.

Technology can help with some of this. Advances in satellite imagery, remote sensors, and data analytics can increase transparency and make compliance with arms control agreements more observable. But more must be done to avoid situations that can escalate into war. If nuclear-armed states agree to lower operational readiness and strengthen crisis communication, that can help ensure that governments have the time and the means to defuse dangerous situations. Each country must protect its command-and-control systems from cyberattacks and AI-driven manipulation of information, so that a false alarm or miscalculation never leads to nuclear war.

The final priority is to ensure that the international justice system can enforce courts’ decisions. Dismantling the International Criminal Court, as some countries are trying to do, would only ensure that the world’s gravest crimes go unpunished. The ICC should not only prosecute cases of genocide, aggression, war crimes, and crimes against humanity but also have the authority to bring to justice those who are responsible for these crimes. When a state becomes a party to the Rome Statute, the treaty that created the ICC, it agrees to submit itself to the court’s jurisdiction. Yet if that state refuses to cooperate or surrender a suspect, there is no real punishment, only an embarrassing censure. This must change. States that shelter individuals indicted by the ICC should face legal, diplomatic, and financial consequences. Starting and waging war should carry a real cost.

Proposals to reform international institutions have long struggled to gain traction, but the coming election of a new UN secretary-general offers a rare opportunity to inject fresh momentum into these efforts. Were the UN to appoint its first female leader, it would be a powerful symbol of renewal, inclusion, and institutional adaptation. The organization cannot truly claim to speak on behalf of all humanity until it has had a woman leading it. Reaching this milestone could kindle a new sense of ambition and instill greater confidence in the capacity of international institutions to reform themselves—and to reclaim their role in constraining war.

Halting the world’s march toward war will require the broadest possible commitment. All countries, large and small, must refuse to look away or to participate in escalatory action. They must stand by the international norms that constrain war today, and reform and empower the institutions that will make it impossible tomorrow.

Ultimately, what will make war unimaginable is a positive peace agenda that addresses the root causes of insecurity. Every step toward eradicating poverty, expanding access to quality education, advancing gender equality, strengthening democratic institutions, and increasing disaster resilience also eases the conditions that produce violence.

International financial institutions must be able to deliver financing quickly, at scale, and at reasonable cost, so that no developing country is forced to choose between servicing its debt and investing in its people. Multilateral development banks must be able to mobilize funds for the green and digital transitions, so that climate migration and competition over resources do not become drivers of interstate conflict. And the World Health Organization’s Pandemic Agreement must be finalized and put into force to ensure that all countries will be able to access vaccines and treatments when the next global health emergency strikes.

This is why Spain has sought to place development at the heart of its foreign policy. The Spanish government has championed the UN’s Sustainable Development Goals for 2030, and last year the city of Seville hosted the Fourth International Conference on Financing for Development, where delegates committed to easing the debt burden of vulnerable countries and unlocking investment for sustainable development. Spain has also promoted initiatives such as the Global Hub on Debt for Development Swaps, launched with the World Bank; the multilateral Debt Suspension Clause Alliance, which allows vulnerable countries to pause debt payments after disasters; and its own mechanism to provide up to 60 million euros in debt relief for developing countries each year. In addition, Spain has backed climate finance, global health, gender equality, food security, and social protection initiatives in partnership with multilateral institutions and international organizations. These development policies are peacebuilding policies, too.

A larger project to rally the world behind legal norms and genuine multilateralism may seem unrealistic. But we have seen what happens when the normalization of war drags humanity into hell. The men and women who emerged from the ruins of World War II, the darkest chapter in human history, found a way to construct a multilateral world order. The question before our generation is whether we will preserve the rule of law or allow a return to rule by force. Whether we build the world of tomorrow through foresight and cooperation, or through the chaos and pain of another world war. We must choose wisely while the choice is still ours to make.

#5909. Blue wave meets ballot box surprises as voters split on abortion, voter ID and billionaire taxes

Americans from across the country shared their midterm voting decisions with Fox News Digital, driven by everything from President Donald Trump to abortion, immigration enforcement and gun rights. (Credit: Nicholas Ballasy for Fox News Digital)

While Democrats’ midterm sweep dominated Election Night, voters in several states delivered consequential — and sometimes crosscutting — verdicts on voter ID, abortion access, taxes and English-language laws.

But the down-ballot decisions largely unfolded beneath the shadow of the GOP’s crushing losses, offering a separate look at the policy priorities voters chose to elevate in their states.

Read on to learn more about some of the most significant ballot measure results that will be shaping the lives of thousands of Americans.

More than a dozen states are holding ballot measures on Election Day that are related to voting. (Paul Steinhauser/Fox News)

DEMOCRATS ‘DIVORCED FROM VOTERS’ AS 5 STATES BACK CITIZEN-ONLY VOTING, BOLSTERING GOP PUSH

Voters in Nevada, North Carolina and Ohio all voted to pass voter ID requirements, even as talks among federal lawmakers have stalled on the issue, showcasing that the issue remains a point of public interest.

In North Carolina, the state has required photo ID for in-person voting since 2018. But with the adoption of a constitutional amendment in a 59.6% to 40.3% vote, the Tar Heel State expanded the requirement to all forms of voting.

Similarly, in Ohio, voters passed Issue 3 in a 65.3% to 34.6% vote. Although Ohio already had pre-existing in-person voter-ID laws, Issue 3 codified the requirement in the state’s constitution, making it harder to overturn later.

Finally, in Nevada, Question 7 passed in a 62.1% to 37.8% vote, establishing a constitutional amendment to enact the state’s first voter-ID requirement. It compels voters to show ID when voting in person and, for mail and absentee ballots, asks voters to provide the last four digits of their driver's license or Social Security number.

The Idaho state capitol building in Boise, Idaho. (Getty Images)

EVERYTHING TO KNOW ABOUT HOUSE AND SENATE CONTROL, BALLOT MEASURES AND 2026 ELECTION DAY POLL CLOSING

In Idaho, voters approved HJR6 in a 63.1% to 36.8% outcome, a measure that makes English the official language of the state, codifying it for use in public proceedings with a constitutional amendment.

English had already been made Idaho’s official language through statute since 2007.

State Rep. Dale Hawkins, one of the champions behind HJR6, called it reflective of efforts from the Trump administration to make English the official language of the United States through executive order.

"Our current administration signed an executive order doing this for the entire United States so we are just in line with what’s going on," Hawkins said in public remarks.

In an interview with local media, Hawkins also cited a concern that many newcomers to the U.S. had, in his view, overlooked the country’s linguistic identity.

"One of the things that unites us all as a culture, as a country, as a state, is language. And what used to be the desired effect, which was assimilation — learn the language, learn the culture, learn the history — those things have slipped away. And we don’t see assimilation anymore. And I believe that if we have a common language, then our culture will survive," Hawkins told the Idaho Capital Sun.

Marjorie Dannenfelser, president of the Susan B. Anthony List, speaks as she introduces former U.S. Vice President Mike Pence at the National Press Club on November 30, 2021 in Washington, D.C. (Drew Angerer/Getty Images)

DEM GOV MAURA HEALEY SIGNS SWEEPING MASSACHUSETTS BILL ALLOWING ABORTIONS UP TO BIRTH

Alongside their English language constitutional amendment, voters in Idaho rejected Proposition 1, which would have created a right to abortion up to the point of fetal viability, except in cases of medical emergency.

Proposition 1 failed in a 44.0% to 55.9% outcome.

Had it been successful, the measure would have greatly reduced the state’s current limits on abortion except to save the life of a pregnant woman or in cases of rape or incest.

To onlookers like Marjorie Dannenfelser, president of Susan B. Anthony Pro-Life America, the outcome demonstrates that protecting life in the womb remains a priority and an ongoing contest at the state level.

"We are incredibly grateful for Stop Prop 1 and all who stood up to defend the unborn. This is a hard-fought win and an example to pro-life voters, candidates and elected officials across the country… Voters were not fooled because pro-life leaders spoke up boldly to expose this initiative," Dannenfelser said in a statement.

California Gov. Gavin Newsom, left, pictured alongside a group of DSA supporters holding a banner, right. (Charly Triballeau/ AFP via Getty Images; Spencer Platt/Getty Images)

STATE OF PLAY: BLUE WAVE SWEEPS DEMOCRATS INTO POWER, WITH KEY RACES STILL HANGING IN BALANCE

In California, voters rejected the implementation of a 5% billionaire tax that carried the backing of the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA). The measure, Proposition 40, failed in a 47.5% to 52.4% loss.

The proposition would have applied a 5% tax on assets like businesses, securities, art, collectibles and intellectual property while excluding select pensions, real estate and retirement accounts.

To prevent billionaires from simply leaving the state, Proposition 40 was designed to retroactively apply to billionaires who were residents of California on Jan. 1, 2026.

The DSA framed the measure as a way to distribute wealth and as a kind of scaled-up version of a progressive tax, a tax that scales relative to income.

"We have the money in California to pay for the quality schools and healthcare that we need. It just needs to be transferred from the wrong pockets to the right ones. We can do that by taxing the rich," the slideshow reads.

Notably, Democratic Gov. Gavin Newsom opposed Proposition 40, arguing that it would lessen California’s competitiveness relative to other states.

California Gov. Gavin Newsom speaks while campaigning for then-Senate candidate Chris Pappas at Exeter Town Hall on Oct. 17, 2026, in Exeter, N.H. (John Tully/Getty Images)

Alongside Proposition 40, voters in California also rejected Proposition 39 — a proposed constitutional amendment that would have required ID for in-person voting and checks for the state’s voter registration lists.

Like Nevada's Question 7, it also would have asked absentee voters to provide the last four digits of a unique government-issued ID number before securing a mail-in ballot.

The measure failed in a 39.4% to 60.5% vote.

Ahead of Election Day, Gov. Newsom characterized Proposition 39 as an extension of efforts to restrict access to voting rights.

"Trump and his MAGA allies tried to rig our elections once, and California voters shut them down with Prop 50. Prop 39 is just their latest attempt to silence us, because they know they can’t win in California in a fair fight. It’s voter suppression, full stop, and Californians will vote it down in November," Newsom said in a statement.

Leo Briceno is a politics reporter for the congressional team at Fox News Digital. He was previously a reporter with World Magazine.
