#5908. The Case for a Trump-Xi-Putin Summit

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

CHARLES KUPCHAN is Professor of International Affairs at Georgetown University and a Senior Fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations. He is the author of Bringing Order to Anarchy: Governing the World to Come.

In just a few days’ time, U.S. President Donald Trump, Chinese leader Xi Jinping, and Russian President Vladimir Putin could be holding a three-way summit on the sidelines of next week’s Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation meeting in Shenzhen, China. According to The Wall Street Journal, Putin floated the idea of a trialogue last month as an opportunity to discuss ending the war in Ukraine. No firm plan has yet been announced, but even the prospect of this meeting has provoked widespread consternation. Polish Foreign Minister Radoslaw Sikorski warned against a repeat of the Yalta Conference of 1945, when U.S. President Franklin Roosevelt and British Prime Minister Winston Churchill capitulated to Soviet leader Joseph Stalin’s push for the Soviet Union to dominate Eastern Europe. U.S. Senator Chris Coons posted on X that “there can be no talks about Ukraine’s future without Ukraine at the table, and there can be no real negotiations as long as Trump signals weakness and servitude to Xi and Putin at every opportunity.”

These worries are entirely justified; Trump has a track record of cozying up to autocrats and turning his back on democratic allies. Should he sit down with Xi and Putin, he must avoid selling out European and Asian partners who will not be at the table. And given the three leaders’ headstrong personalities and competing national interests that pit the United States against what Xi calls a “no limits” partnership between China and Russia, the meeting might ultimately yield little progress on major issues.

Nevertheless, Trump would be right to join a summit with Xi and Putin. No U.S. president has ever held a trilateral meeting with the leaders of China and Russia, and the proposed talks would break the ice and begin to normalize much-needed dialogue. Authoritarianism is not going away, so autocracies and democracies must learn not only to live comfortably alongside one another but also to work together to tame geopolitical rivalry and tackle global challenges in an interdependent world. Americans will not find it easy to get along with the likes of Russia and China, whose political systems threaten the U.S. commitment to spreading democracy. But Trump is taking a bold step in the right direction by engaging with the heads of the world’s leading autocracies.

If he meets with Xi and Putin, Trump should aim low and focus on making meaningful, if limited, progress on a small number of core issues that could benefit from a three-way conversation: the flash points of the Korean Peninsula, Ukraine, and Iran, as well as arms control and strategic stability. But even if the summit yields no concrete results, it will nonetheless have been worthwhile. Trump, Xi, and Putin are paving the way for their successors to advance great-power cooperation across ideological lines and stabilize a fragmented world.

I have argued previously, including in a 2021 Foreign Affairs essay with Richard Haass, that a global concert—an informal steering group of great powers modeled on the nineteenth-century Concert of Europe—offers the best hope of curbing geopolitical competition and enabling collective action in a multipolar world. In addition to the United States, China, and Russia, a new concert would include India, Japan, the European Union, and representatives from Africa, Latin America, the Middle East, and Southeast Asia. Concerts sacrifice representation for efficacy and give only the geopolitically influential states that must be there a seat at the table. They set aside ideological differences and bring democracies and autocracies together to focus on international cooperation. Because concerts eschew binding, enforceable procedures in favor of informal consensus building, they can succeed when formal institutions such as the UN Security Council are paralyzed and when the United States wavers in its commitment to rules-based multilateralism.

But the time has not come for a global concert. Concerts function effectively only when their members are committed to the territorial status quo, are willing to exercise strategic restraint and pursue mutual benefits, and are ready to settle disputes diplomatically. Putin’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine and Xi’s decision to back Russia have, for now, scuttled hopes that the great powers will normalize restraint and the peaceful settlement of disputes. Meanwhile, Trump’s “America first” statecraft shuns joint action and mutual gain in favor of unilateralism.

Yet even though Trump, Xi, and Putin are the wrong leaders at the wrong time to establish a great-power steering group that other major players would eventually join, Trump’s iconoclastic diplomacy sets a useful precedent. His Alaska summit with Putin last year and two summits with Xi this year were admittedly long on pageantry and short on substance. But by taking the controversial step of pursuing joint talks with the two leaders, Trump is widening the diplomatic Overton window and expanding what is politically possible for future U.S. presidents. Avoiding great-power conflict and taming a world in increasing disarray requires the United States to build constructive relations with both China and Russia. Trump deserves credit for starting a trialogue that his successors can carry forward.

If they meet in Shenzhen, Trump, Xi, and Putin should focus on easing tensions at key geopolitical flash points. The Korean Peninsula presents the best opportunity for the three leaders to collaborate. At the UN General Assembly in September, South Korean President Lee Jae-myung laid out an ambitious plan for normalizing relations with Pyongyang, including a proposal to replace the peninsula’s 1953 armistice with a “peace regime” that promotes “respect for each other’s systems and acknowledgement of our differences.” As part of that plan, Lee has advocated a phased approach to North Korean denuclearization, with an initial freeze on weapons development followed by gradual reductions in Pyongyang’s arsenal of nuclear weapons and ballistic missiles until the peninsula is denuclearized.

Lee’s bold proposal could bring U.S., Chinese, and Russian interests into alignment. Trump has made it clear that he wants to de-escalate tensions on the Korean Peninsula and is already seeking a meeting with North Korean leader Kim Jong Un. Beijing and Moscow are competing for influence over Kim, but both want a stable relationship between North and South Korea, so they might be willing to cooperate on proposals to halt nuclear weapons production and encourage talks between Pyongyang and Seoul. Trump, Xi, and Putin should aim to emerge from their meeting with a plan for advancing Lee’s peace agenda; jointly supporting a freeze on North Korean nuclear weapons development is a good place to start.

The prospects for progress toward ending the war in Ukraine are much dimmer but not nonexistent. Trump should be striving to weaken Russia’s ability to sustain the war, but he has long resisted tightening sanctions on Moscow. He talks up potential business ventures with Russia and, in recent weeks, even struck a deal with Putin to get more Russian diesel into global markets and waived a new round of congressional sanctions against Moscow. Trump evidently cares more about trying to bring down fuel prices than about curbing Russia’s ability to fund its war against Ukraine.

A summit offers Trump the opportunity to change course and try to convince Xi to restrain Putin. Trump has vowed to bring the war in Ukraine to an end, and the Chinese leader can help him achieve that goal. Xi has enormous leverage over Putin: China backs the Kremlin diplomatically, is the largest buyer of Russian oil and gas, and is the main supplier of dual-use items such as semiconductors and machine tools that are critical to Russian weapons manufacturing.

Xi has played along with Putin’s war against Ukraine so far, presumably because the conflict diverts U.S. attention and resources away from Asia, divides NATO, and allows Xi and Putin to cast themselves as leading a global resistance to Western hegemony. But when Xi and Trump met this May, the leaders agreed to pursue “a constructive relationship of strategic stability” between China and the United States, which Xi could advance by pressing Putin to de-escalate. As more Russians feel the effects of the war, the Kremlin may be newly receptive to such pressure. Ukrainian drone strikes are taking a significant toll on Russia’s oil sector and logistics networks, which means Russian citizens face mounting gas and commodities shortages. Meanwhile, some 35,000 Russian soldiers per month are losing their lives or sustaining severe injuries in eastern Ukraine, forcing Putin to consider the unpopular move of ramping up military mobilization. With no battlefield victory in sight, Putin has reasons to entertain a diplomatic off-ramp.

Trump and Xi could press Putin to accept a set of limited but consequential steps to de-escalate the conflict. These could include an agreement between Russia and Ukraine—brokered and monitored by Washington and Beijing—to end attacks on energy installations and infrastructure, civilian targets, and commercial shipping. Trump and Xi could also jointly press Putin to refrain from actions that could broaden the war, such as penetrating NATO airspace with Russian drones, sabotaging European infrastructure, and threatening to use nuclear weapons. This pressure, combined with the allure of improving diplomatic and commercial ties to the United States, might convince Putin to relent on at least some of these issues.

Trump could also use a trilateral summit to seek help extricating the United States from its war in Iran—a quagmire that has produced regional mayhem and soaring oil prices. As Tehran continues to issue maximalist demands and the war disrupts shipping through the straits of Hormuz and Bab el Mandeb, Trump needs an out.

Neither Xi nor Putin has yet been inclined to lend a hand. China is still buying Iranian oil and providing Tehran with military intelligence and dual-use items that end up in Iranian drones and missiles, and Russia is providing satellite targeting data, munitions, advanced drone technology, and logistical support. Both Beijing and Moscow are happy to see the United States mired in a conflict that is exacerbating divisions within the American electorate and fracturing NATO.

As a major energy exporter, Russia benefits from the spike in oil prices that the conflict has triggered, so pressing Putin to cease supporting Iran is likely a lost cause. But China is the world’s largest oil importer, and Beijing has a vested interest in lowering energy prices by stabilizing exports from the Middle East. Although China’s large oil reserves have buffered its economy from rising oil prices so far, those reserves have been significantly depleted. China’s crude oil imports have begun to climb again, leaving its economy exposed to high energy prices if the conflict drags on. And as the world’s largest exporter of goods, China suffers from the dampening effect high energy prices have on global growth.

Xi might therefore be amenable to helping Trump end the war. Trump should ask Xi to stop or at least slow the flow of Chinese dual-use items to Iran. Washington’s naval blockade and financial and commercial sanctions on Tehran have already taken a toll on Iran’s economy and military supply chains. By cutting off Chinese technology shipments to Iran, Beijing could deepen this economic isolation and help convince Tehran to negotiate an end to the conflict.

Xi could also press Iran to drop its unrealistic demands for ending the war, including that the United States withdraw its forces from the Gulf, release frozen Iranian assets, and remove economic sanctions. Washington might well be willing to offer economic relief, but only as part of postwar negotiations toward a deal that reins in Iran’s nuclear ambitions. Xi could help clear the way for those talks by urging Iran to accept credible terms for ending the immediate conflict.

Beyond working to resolve current conflicts, Trump should promote future strategic stability by pursuing arms control negotiations with Xi and Putin. Every arms control agreement negotiated between Washington and Moscow has expired; their final nuclear pact, New START, ended in February. There are currently no caps on the American and Russian strategic arsenals and no regular inspections, data exchanges, or notifications of nuclear exercises. This nuclear free-for-all is particularly dangerous given Russia’s nuclear saber rattling amid the war in Ukraine and the Kremlin’s fondness for provocations such as sending explosive-laden drones into NATO airspace.

In October, Trump appropriately proposed that a trilateral summit include a discussion of nuclear arms control. China has long resisted engaging in arms control talks, arguing that it should not have to limit its nuclear program when its arsenal is much smaller than that of the United States and Russia. But Beijing is rapidly expanding and modernizing its nuclear arsenal. The Pentagon has projected that China will possess more than 1,000 operational warheads by 2030, putting it in nearly the same league as the United States and Russia, which have stockpiles of roughly 1,800 operational warheads each. With the uncertain trajectory of U.S.-Chinese relations, instability on the Korean Peninsula, and China’s tense relationship with Taiwan heightening the risk of conflict in East Asia, Beijing must be part of high-level nuclear arms control discussions.

Realistically, Trump can do little more than start this conversation and invite Xi and Putin to continue it in future trilateral negotiations led by teams of diplomats and arms control experts. In future talks, the three countries should discuss not only caps on nuclear weapons stockpiles but also military-to-military contacts, crisis management protocols, notifications of military exercises, counterproliferation, and other measures that can contribute to strategic stability.

Trump could add a host of other topics to the summit’s agenda, including trade, energy security, and AI regulation, but he should avoid a wide-ranging conversation that goes nowhere. He might, however, notch an easy win by proposing a three-way initiative in an area in which cooperation is relatively straightforward—for example, a joint medical mission to the Democratic Republic of the Congo to help fight Ebola or a joint aid operation to deliver humanitarian relief to Gaza or Yemen. A concrete, public effort along these lines would show that trilateral dialogue can yield viable cooperation.

Yet Trump should guard against being so eager for a win that he lets Xi and Putin take advantage of him. The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit takes place only two weeks after American voters dealt Trump and his party a historic rebuke in the U.S. midterm elections. The notoriously thin-skinned president could be tempted to make concessions to Beijing and Moscow to score a foreign policy breakthrough. He must avoid making a behind-closed-doors compromise on Ukraine that he then tries to force on Kyiv and its European supporters. Similarly, he must hold the line on Taiwan even as he seeks Xi’s help on North Korea, Ukraine, and Iran. Otherwise, Trump will leave the trilateral meeting confirming the worst fears of those who foresaw a new Yalta in the making. Capitulating to Xi and Putin would alienate Washington’s allies, project American weakness, and erode global deterrence.

Trump must also carefully manage the optics of a trilateral summit. Putin is an indicted war criminal who continues to prosecute a ruthless war of aggression against Ukraine. It is far too soon to let him out of the penalty box. And although Washington is right to seek a good working relationship with Beijing, it is time for Trump’s “truly great friendship” with Xi to bear fruit. Trump must approach talks with sobriety and pragmatism, seeking to demonstrate that Washington, Beijing, and Moscow can work together to help promote global peace and stability.

A summit next week will not usher in a new era of great-power collaboration anytime soon. It might not even yield concrete deliverables. Still, the meeting would set a valuable precedent. Simply by sitting down with Xi and Putin, Trump would begin a much-needed trilateral conversation.

#5906. Massie's push to impeach Hegseth splits constituents in his own backyard: 'Pure spite'

Northern Kentucky voters talk to Fox News Digital and give differing opinions of their firebrand libertarian representative, who was defeated in the Republican primary and will leave office.

KENTON COUNTY, Ky. — Rep. Thomas Massie's push to impeach War Secretary Pete Hegseth over the Iran war drew sharply different reactions from northern Kentucky residents interviewed by Fox News Digital, who offered competing assessments of the outgoing Republican congressman — and whether his latest confrontation with the Trump administration reflects principle or political payback.

Fox News Digital visited Covington, the population center of Massie’s district, along with Newport, Erlanger and other communities across the river from Cincinnati, to ask residents about their outgoing congressman and his efforts to impeach the Pentagon chief.

Richard Hassebrock, a Covington resident who said he has not always agreed with Massie, welcomed the impeachment effort.

"I'm not always a huge fan of Thomas Massie, but on this point I am," Hassebrock told Fox News Digital.

TRUMP DEFENDS PENTAGON CHIEF AS MASSIE TARGETS HIM: 'HEGSETH SHOULD BE PRAISED, NOT IMPEACHED'

Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth takes questions from reporters during a news conference at the Pentagon on Nov. 10 in Arlington, Va. Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., introduced articles of impeachment against Hegseth on Monday, alleging he violated the Constitution by waging war in Iran without congressional authorization, among other charges. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

Craig Cuskey, who works in the Covington area but lives just outside the bounds of Massie’s district in Louisville, described the impeachment push as "pure spite" and "petty," saying his opinion of the congressman had deteriorated.

The contrasting reactions came after House Republicans voted to refer Massie's impeachment effort against Hegseth to committee, adding another confrontation to the Kentucky Republican's long-running disagreements with President Donald Trump and his party's leadership.

Democrats poised to take the gavel in January, however, signaled they will likely take up Massie’s mantle and seek the Pentagon chief’s impeachment.

Hassebrock said he was disappointed by Massie's electoral defeat and praised the congressman's independence.

"He stands up for himself, he stands up to what he believes in, he's got principles unlike 99% of the other congress people out there," he said.

Rilla, a Fort Wright resident who provided only her first name, offered a more complicated assessment.

"I think he's just trying to get back at Trump," she said, while adding that she had been proud of Massie for standing up to the president and was disappointed by his defeat.

7 REPUBLICANS REBEL AGAINST TRUMP, VOTE WITH DEMS TO REIN IN IRAN WAR POWERS

Rilla said she opposed the Iran war, particularly the prospect of military action without congressional involvement.

"I don't think anyone should just go in and declare war without Congress being a part of it," she said.

Phil Wiseman, an Independence resident, said he doubted the impeachment effort would accomplish much, even as he questioned the administration's military strategy.

"I don't see a definable outcome," Wiseman said. "And if you're going to fight a war, there needs to be a definable outcome."

Wiseman said he identifies as a "RINO" — a term often used derisively by conservatives for "Republican in Name Only" — and voted for an independent instead of supporting Rep.-elect Ed Gallrein, the Trump-endorsed Republican who defeated Massie in Kentucky's primary earlier this year.

He said his family has discussed Hegseth, noting that his father was a 27-year military veteran and his son served in the Marine Corps.

"We all had the same discussion," he said. "Who is advising him? Because this [war] is very ill-thought-out and not well-planned."

Wiseman compared that aspect of the Iran conflict to how the U.S. previously became mired in Afghanistan.

"So I seriously question the military advisors that are giving them this advice, or maybe they gave them contrary advice and it was ignored," he said.

Anthony Tuemler of Covington supported impeachment and accused Hegseth of violating the Constitution through his conduct of the war.

Tuemler's assessment of Massie was similarly mixed. Although he said he was glad the congressman was leaving office, he credited Massie with speaking up when he believed it was necessary.

"I'm glad he's gone," Tuemler said of Massie. "But the only thing I'm distressed about or sorry about is like when it came time to say the right things, Massie was the one who did it."

Randall Thompson of Newport, dressed in a military uniform for Veterans Day as he walked with his children near Covington's Mainstrasse Village corridor, said he supports the defense secretary and pointed to their shared military experience in the Middle East.

"I support the troops and Pete Hegseth," Thompson said, adding off-camera that he was in the same area of Iraq as the former Fox News host when they were both deployed.

Cuskey also defended Hegseth, saying he had initially been skeptical of his nomination but had grown more impressed with his performance.

"I think for his background, he's done a remarkable job," Cuskey said, arguing that Hegseth was carrying out directives from the commander in chief.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., has voiced support for impeaching the Pentagon chief, while other Democrats have signaled plans to revisit his conduct when they take control of Congress in January.

Some lawmakers are also eyeing investigations and potential impeachment proceedings involving other senior Trump administration officials, including Secretary of State Marco Rubio, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller and border czar Tom Homan — setting the stage for a broader confrontation between congressional Democrats and the White House.

Born and raised in Allentown, Pennsylvania, worked in both the New York and Washington bureaus for Fox News since 2013.

#5902. Democrats seize on midterm wins to push massive minimum wage hikes, but economists see trouble ahead

Research Director at the Employment Policies Institute, Rebekah Paxton, warns local officials about raising the minimum wage as Alameda County advances highest mandate in U.S. history.

Democrats are pushing major minimum-wage increases after their midterm victories, but economists warn the proposals could come with unintended consequences for small businesses and inexperienced workers.

Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro is pledging to more than double his state's $7.25 minimum wage, while Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker is calling for a federal increase as Democrats make affordability a central part of their governing agenda.

The renewed push comes as Seattle, which has one of the nation's highest minimum wages, faces concerns about rising business costs and employment opportunities — raising questions about the tradeoffs of higher wage mandates.

"We’re going to more than double Pennsylvania‘s minimum wage," Shapiro wrote in an X post this month. "Codify abortion rights into our state Constitution. Fund mass transit. Build more housing. These are the commonsense things that Pennsylvanians want done."

AMERICA SAW THE BLUE WAVE COMING — BUT DRASTICALLY UNDERESTIMATED THE EXTENT OF THE DEM TSUNAMI

Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro speaks during a re-election campaign kickoff event in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, on Thursday, Jan. 8, 2026. (Rachel Wisniewski/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

"And with a Democratic trifecta, we will deliver on all of them," he continued.

Shapiro's proposal would raise Pennsylvania's minimum wage from $7.25 to $15 an hour, an increase of roughly 107%, prompting questions about how small businesses would absorb the additional labor costs.

Tom Savidge, an economist with the American Institute for Economic Research, warned in an interview with Fox News Digital that steep wage increases could force small businesses to raise prices, reduce workers' hours or cut back on hiring.

"A federal minimum wage hike will worsen the affordability crisis," Savidge told Fox News Digital. "A one-size-fits-all mandate ignores regional differences in wages and business costs. It risks higher prices, fewer hours, and fewer jobs, especially for inexperienced workers. Politicians can mandate higher wages, but they cannot mandate that businesses can afford to pay them."

"Small businesses employing minimum-wage workers cannot absorb such a sharp increase without adjusting elsewhere," he added. "Some may raise prices, cut hours, or stop hiring. A higher minimum wage means little to workers who cannot find a job."

The debate comes as Democrats seek to deliver on campaign promises to make life more affordable, even as businesses in cities with higher minimum wages report growing financial pressures.

Seattle, where the minimum wage is currently $21.30 an hour and will rise to $22.14 in January, offers one example of the economic questions surrounding such policies. While proponents have asserted that minimum wage increases will be a boon to employees, some Seattle business owners have previously raised concerns about facing additional cost burdens, KOMO News reported in December 2025.

Rising labor costs have been among the financial pressures cited by Seattle restaurant owners who have closed locations, alongside higher food prices, declining customer demand and other operating expenses, Fox News Digital previously reported.

A January survey by The Intentionalist found that 67% of 136 independent small businesses surveyed in Seattle and surrounding Washington communities reported greater financial strain than during the pandemic, with respondents pointing to rising labor costs, rent, insurance and weaker customer demand.

A 2022 study published in the American Economic Journal: Economic Policy found that Seattle's 2015 and 2016 minimum-wage increases boosted pay for longer-tenured workers but reduced hours worked, particularly among less-experienced employees, and limited opportunities for people entering low-wage jobs.

However, a separate 2017 study by University of California, Berkeley researchers found that Seattle's earlier minimum-wage increases raised wages in the food-service industry without a statistically significant decline in restaurant employment.

Savidge went on to say that steep minimum wage increases often benefit major corporations over Main Street businesses.

"Higher minimum wages can favor big corporations over small businesses," he explained. "Large corporations can spread higher labor costs across more sales, invest in automation, or relocate operations. Small businesses have fewer options."

FOX NEWS VOTER POLL: HOW DEMOCRATS TOOK CONTROL OF THE SENATE

Gov. Josh Shapiro of Pennsylvania, at the Democratic National Convention, speaks with activists from New Hampshire at a delegation breakfast, on Aug. 21, 2024 in Chicago, Illinois. (Paul Steinhauser/Fox News)

Despite concerns about the potential economic consequences, Shapiro has made raising Pennsylvania's minimum wage one of his first priorities after Democrats won control of both chambers of the state legislature.

"Half a million workers in Pennsylvania still make less than $15 an hour — you can’t live off of that in 2026," Shapiro said. "Now, we’re finally able to change that. The very first bill that I will sign into law with our new Democratic majority this January will be to raise our minimum wage — and make sure every Pennsylvania worker has a livable wage. It’s long overdue."

His comments come after Democrats secured a trifecta in Harrisburg in the 2026 midterms after flipping the Pennsylvania state Senate.

$25 AN HOUR? MAJOR CITIES EYE MASSIVE WAGE HIKES AS LABOR ACTIVISTS SEEK FEDERAL MANDATE

When reached for comment, Shapiro's office referred Fox News Digital to the governor’s comments on raising the minimum wage during an interview with former CNN host Don Lemon released on Sunday.

"Some of your some of your viewers and listeners are going to think it's crazy, but job number one is going to be raise the minimum wage to 15 bucks an hour, and now your listeners like, 'What do you mean 15 bucks an hour?'" Shapiro said during the interview. "Everybody's at 15 bucks an hour. Well, we're not in Pennsylvania. We've been stuck at $7.25 an hour for decades, right? Because the Republicans in the state senate, every time we've sent a bill over there, have blocked it."

Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker speaks during a press conference at the UI Health Mile Square Health Center on Feb. 28, 2025. (Eileen T. Meslar/Chicago Tribune/Tribune News Service via Getty Images)

The minimum-wage push is also gaining traction beyond Pennsylvania.

Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker, who signed legislation in 2019 gradually raising his state's minimum wage to $15 an hour, is now calling on Congress to increase the federal minimum wage as part of his post-election economic agenda.

"Americans made their voices heard and this is an opportunity for Congress to immediately address the challenges of working people – higher wages, lower costs, rooting out corruption," Pritzker said in a statement. "The people of Illinois cannot afford to wait."

AOC-BACKED $25 MINIMUM WAGE PLAN SOUNDS GREAT — BUT AT WHAT COST?

Savidge told Fox News Digital that Pritzker and Shapiro's minimum wage proposals "risk giving large corporations a competitive advantage while squeezing out smaller competitors."

Critics question whether higher wage mandates translate into better overall economic outcomes, particularly for workers with little job experience.

Adam Gorcyca, a policy researcher at the Illinois Policy Institute, warned that employers may respond to higher labor costs by reducing hours, limiting hiring or raising prices.

"Mandating higher wages doesn't immediately guarantee workers will take home more money," Gorcyca added. "Employers often respond to these policies by cutting back hours, slowing hiring or raising prices. It can help select workers, while also making it harder for entry-level or younger workers."

Gorcyca also questioned whether Illinois' minimum-wage increases had delivered broader employment gains, pointing to the state's slow pandemic recovery as evidence of its economic struggles.

"In Illinois, Gov. J.B. Pritzker passed a minimum wage hike in 2019, slowly increasing the state’s minimum wage to $15 an hour by 2025," he said. "While he has campaigned that it was the ‘right thing to do,’ the state doesn’t have a stronger labor market for workers. In fact, Illinois was one of the slowest to recover from the pandemic."

Pritzker's office did not respond to Fox News Digital's request for comment on Tuesday.

The push is also emerging as a priority for congressional Democrats.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., said Monday that the federal minimum wage needs to be raised and that he plans to discuss the issue with members of both chambers of Congress.

"It's a conversation that I look forward to having with not simply the members of the House Democratic caucus, but with the Senate as well, to see what is possible," Jeffries said. "It certainly needs to be increased. It's a disgrace that the minimum wage hasn't been raised in 20 years and is currently $7.25 per hour."

#5900. Socialist LA mayor's bold homelessness promise ahead of 2028 Olympics hit with reality check: 'Not optimistic'

John Alle, co-founder of the Santa Monica Coalition, spoke to Fox News Digital about potential gaps in incoming Mayor Nithya Raman's plan to cut the number of people sleeping in tents and encampments by at least 50% before the 2028 Olympics.

Incoming Los Angeles Mayor Nithya Raman has promised to cut the number of people sleeping in tents and homeless encampments by at least 50% before the 2028 Summer Olympics, but one local activist believes her vision may not be realistic.

The blue city’s growing homeless population became a major focus of Raman’s mayoral campaign. A member of the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), she plans to expand shelter and housing options while stretching "every dollar as far as possible."

John Alle, a business owner and co-founder of the Santa Monica Coalition, told Fox News Digital Raman won’t be able to "hide" the city’s homelessness problem from tourists arriving for the Games and raised concerns about potential gaps in in her plan to deliver lasting results.

"I don't think we're going to be able to hide that before the crowds," Alle said, adding that the city has become a "third-world country."

4 OF NATION'S LARGEST CITIES NOW LED BY SOCIALIST MAYORS AFTER TUESDAY'S ELECTION, AFFECTING TENS OF MILLIONS

John Alle, co-founder of the Santa Monica Coalition, told Fox News Digital he has concerns that incoming Mayor Nithya Raman's homelessness plan could be a temporary fix ahead of the 2028 Olympics. (Mario Tama/Getty Images; Allen J. Schaben / Los Angeles Times via Getty Images)

"We're not hiding it now. We're seeing tourists being attacked in Santa Monica, we're seeing tourists being attacked in LA. Our tourism, everything that funds our general fund, our retail sales taxes are all down. The morale of the police, which we need, is down."

Alle warned that Raman’s plan will be a temporary solution that will have homeless people moved off the streets ahead of the Olympics and returning once the Games are over.

"When the Olympics come, they'll be picked up, they'll be bused, they’ll be sent elsewhere, and they'll wander right back after the three-week Olympic and Paralympic events."

Former Mayor Karen Bass accepted the Olympic flag during the closing ceremony in Paris two years ago, marking the handoff of hosting duties. This will be the first time LA has hosted the Games since 1984.

According to the Los Angeles Homeless Services Authority’s 2026 annual count, homelessness rose 3.4% in LA and 1.2% across the broader LA area, undoing two consecutive years of declines under Bass.

But how does Raman plan to follow through on that promise?

By the end of her first term, Raman has pledged to expand housing, shelter capacity and rental assistance to eliminate long-term encampments. Raman has previously called the city's "desensitization to homelessness" a "moral failing." Her plan calls for sending medical teams directly into encampments to provide mental health and addiction treatment and help with transitions into long-term housing or care within 90 days.

Raman has also criticized Bass' Inside Safe program, which often places homeless people in costly hotels and motels. Instead, Raman wants to shift funding toward less expensive alternatives, like rental vouchers.

SEATTLE MAYOR KATIE WILSON'S SOCIALIST AGENDA STALLS AMID HOMELESSNESS AND PUBLIC SAFETY CONCERNS

A woman pushes a stroller past a homeless encampment on Cesar E. Chavez Avenue in downtown Los Angeles on Friday, Aug. 21, 2026. (Christina House / Los Angeles Times via Getty Images)

Other parts of Raman’s proposal include sending unarmed crisis response teams rather than police officers to certain emergencies, auditing stalled housing projects and increasing oversight of homelessness spending.

Despite Raman’s ambitious vision, Alle said he isn't convinced her plan will bring meaningful change and argued that city leadership has gone "from bad to worse."

"It's not that much different than Mayor Karen Bass's plan," Alle said.

Alle, who manages roughly 1 million square feet of commercial property across Los Angeles, said he sees the homelessness crisis firsthand and raised concerns about its impact on local businesses and workers.

He warned that treating homeless people on the streets will only prolong the crisis, saying that many Angelenos avoid parts of the city that have been left untouched.

"Pick up the individuals and take them to their facilities away from the parks, away from kids, away from seniors," he said.

Alle said there must be more transparency over how the city has handled more than $1 billion in homelessness funding before allocating more taxpayer dollars.

"She’s got a billion-dollar problem on her hands right now, and she knows," he said.

FURIOUS RESIDENTS CONFRONT HOMELESS HOUSING LEADERS AS POPULAR BEACH COMMUNITY HITS BREAKING POINT

Los Angeles City Council member Nithya Raman, left, talks with Mayor Karen Bass, right, at Hazeltine Park in Sherman Oaks on Feb. 10, 2024. (Mel Melcon/Los Angeles Times via Getty Images)

Raman chaired the City Council's Housing and Homelessness Committee from 2023 until her removal this year amid her mayoral campaign. Alle said Raman will have to explain where the money went but acknowledged her push to investigate the spending.

"To her credit, she wants to audit every single non-profit and every single agency that spent money, including the non-profits that are supported by the city with taxpayer funds for programs," he said.

One of the biggest red flags in Raman’s plan is the proximity of supportive housing to families and children, according to Alle. He added that some facilities don't require residents to remain sober.

"There is no commitment for anyone not to use drugs while they're there," he said.

Pointing to her past opposition to restrictions on camping in public spaces and near schools, Alle argued Raman's approach isn’t "aggressive enough."

"I don't see the solutions that have been put forth that could work being used and so I'm not optimistic," he said.

Alle described LA as having a drug and mental health problem, not a homelessness problem. He said the city needs to stop making life on the streets more comfortable for those who refuse help.

"Until those two issues are addressed, we're not gonna solve homelessness," he said.

He urged Raman to find a more practical solution by moving outreach services off the streets and ending LA's sanctuary city status for good.

"Of course I want her to succeed," he said. "If Nithya succeeds, we all succeed."

Raman ousted Bass in the midterm elections and will take office in December as mayor of Los Angeles, a city of roughly 4 million residents.

Fox News Digital reached out to Raman's team and LA28 for comment.

#5896. Trump keeps signaling an end to Iran war, but expert warns a crucial piece of his strategy is missing

During a speech at Arlington National Cemetery on Wednesday, President Donald Trump thanked U.S. service members for their ongoing sacrifices and argued that the U.S. has been pitted against Iran since 1979, when the Islamic Revolution happened.

President Donald Trump declared Thursday that the United States no longer needs a deal with Iran, abruptly shifting his diplomatic message just three days after expressing optimism about negotiations and directing Vice President JD Vance to focus on domestic issues instead.

"We don’t need a deal with Iran. Those days have long since passed," Trump wrote on Truth Social. "We’ve got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we’ve got their Strait!"

Trump's latest declaration came just one day after he proclaimed victory over Iran during a Veterans Day ceremony at Arlington National Cemetery, while acknowledging that the conflict had not yet formally ended.

"We won," Trump said Wednesday. "It’s just a question of when we end it. I want to make sure we have a total victory, not a 93 percent victory."

TRUMP DEFENDS IRAN WAR DURING VETERANS DAY ADDRESS, SAYS HE IS MAKING THE WORLD 'SAFER'

President Donald Trump and Vice President JD Vance salute during a ceremony at Memorial Amphitheater at Arlington National Cemetery to mark Veterans Day on November 11, 2025 in Arlington, Virginia. (Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)

The president also thanked veterans "working to ensure the world’s number one sponsor of state terrorism, the Islamic Republic of Iran, will never have a nuclear weapon."

An administration official defended Trump's strategy, arguing that the United States has substantial leverage over Iran through its naval blockade and continued military pressure, even as questions remain about how the conflict will end.

"The United States is in a very strong position with control of the Strait of Hormuz and the Iranian economy collapsing. The President has been clear that the greatest naval blockade in history, Operation Economic Outcast, and the new Operation Iron Gate will continue with full effect," a U.S. official told Fox News Digital.

"President Trump holds all the cards, will do what is best for the United States, and will make sure that Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the official added.

Trump's latest words follow months of predictions that an agreement could be reached soon, including comments earlier this week that Tehran "would love to make a deal."

Trump's declaration raises questions about the future of U.S.-Iran negotiations, including whether diplomatic efforts will continue through other administration officials as Washington maintains its military and economic pressure on Tehran.

AFTER 47 YEARS OF CONFRONTING IRAN, DOES AMERICA HAVE A STRATEGY FOR ENDING THIS WAR?

An Iranian flag is displayed at Iran's Bushehr nuclear power plant during an official ceremony on Nov. 10, 2019, to kick-start work on a second reactor at the facility. Bushehr is Iran's only nuclear power station and currently operates on imported fuel from Russia that is closely monitored by the UN's International Atomic Energy Agency. (AFP via Getty Images)

Trump has suggested on at least a dozen occasions since February that Iran wanted to negotiate or that an agreement could be reached soon, according to a preliminary review of his public remarks.

Ludovic Hood, a senior fellow at the Hudson Institute, said the administration's combination of economic coercion, military force and regional partnerships is sensible, but warned that a crucial diplomatic component remains underdeveloped.

"What’s lacking is disciplined diplomacy to complement the economic squeeze and possible airstrikes," Hood told Fox News Digital. "Our Gulf partners are singing from different hymn sheets and we need to draw European partners in more meaningfully."

Trump's optimistic assessments began before U.S. military operations against Iran commenced. On Feb. 13, he suggested Tehran could avoid an attack by accepting Washington's terms. After military operations began, he increasingly emphasized Iran's weakened capabilities and the prospect of ending the conflict.

IRANIAN NEGOTIATORS KICKED OUT OF US AS TALKS STALL, TRUMP LAYS OUT 'POSSIBLE' TIMELINE FOR MORE STRIKES

President Donald Trump arrives to deliver remarks at the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool in Washington, D.C., on Nov. 2, 2026. (Umit Bektas/Reuters)

On March 20, Trump said the United States was "getting very close" to achieving its military objectives.

The administration agreed to a ceasefire in April and continued pursuing a diplomatic settlement, but subsequent military actions and disagreements over Iran's nuclear program and regional shipping have left the broader conflict unresolved.

Trump continued expressing confidence that Iran wanted an agreement, including in May, when he suggested Tehran was eager to negotiate because of the damage inflicted on its military.

During an unscheduled appearance in the White House briefing room Monday, Trump said, "Iran, militarily, is effectively over," while warning that U.S. forces remained "locked and loaded" should circumstances change.

Hood said Trump's seemingly contradictory messages could be part of a deliberate negotiating strategy.

"The administration is pursuing a sensible hybrid strategy of economic coercion, selective use of force, and reliance on capable regional allies," Hood said.

TRUMP SAYS IRAN IS 'NEGOTIATING ON FUMES,' BELIEVES REGIME THOUGHT THEY COULD OUTWAIT HIM

Senior military leaders listen as U.S. Secretary of War Pete Hegseth speaks at Marine Corps Base Quantico on September 30, 2025 in Quantico, Virginia. In an unprecedented gathering, almost 800 generals, admirals and their senior enlisted leaders have been ordered into one location from around the world on short notice. (robynmac)

"Trump’s messaging seems designed to both signal his preference for a diplomatic solution while preserving the option of force," he added.

Hood defended the president's willingness to maintain military pressure.

"Bluster, bluffing and brinkmanship are inherent in the public dimension of negotiations like this; Trump does it, Rubio does it, and senior figures in Tehran do it," he said.

"Strategic patience is essential in this standoff, which has essentially been 40 years in the making."

But Hood also suggested that securing meaningful nuclear concessions or a more cooperative government in Tehran would require coordination with regional partners.

Ashley J. DiMella reports on politics for Fox News Digital.

#5893. Inside the House GOP strategy to capitalize on Democrats' warring factions in the new Congress

Incoming House Minority Whip Rep. Tom Emmer, R-Minn., joins ‘The Ingraham Angle’ to discuss the GOP’s legislative priorities, efforts to support American manufacturing and whether Democrats will work with Republicans to advance their agenda.

EXCLUSIVE: House Republicans are heading into the next Congress ready to brawl.

Rep. Tom Emmer, R-Minn., who is set to go from the No. 3 most powerful House Republican to No. 2 next year, told Fox News Digital that the GOP will hold the new Democrat majority "accountable" for their far-left faction's ideas over the next two years, even if they cannot control what bills get a vote.

Emmer, who has served as House majority whip for the past four years, was elected Tuesday to serve as minority whip alongside incoming House Minority Leader Jim Jordan, R-Ohio. The Speaker of the House position turns over to Democrats, and will likely be won by current minority leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y.

And despite Jeffries poised to take control of what legislation gets to the House floor, Emmer signaled House Republicans would find ways to push their agenda as well.

JIM JORDAN BECOMES NEW HOUSE GOP LEADER AFTER CLOSED-DOOR VOTE, DEFEATING STEVE SCALISE

U.S. House Majority Whip Tom Emmer speaks during a press conference after the House GOP elected new leadership on Capitol Hill on Nov. 10, 2026 in Washington, D.C. (Aaron Schwartz/Getty Images)

"What's their answer to dealing with costs and wages? We're going to investigate Trump," Emmer said in reference to Democrats. "That's not going to solve the problems that they created. So that's going to be the job of the whip, is to make sure that we have a coordinated process — committee hearings, floor votes — to expose and define exactly who these people really are."

PROGRESSIVE GROUP'S 182-PAGE BLUEPRINT MAPS OUT POTENTIAL DEM INVESTIGATIONS INTO TRUMP, CORPORATE ALLIES

Emmer, in his first print interview since winning the House minority whip job, would not go into detail about how Republicans would utilize floor votes, however.

"There's a toolbox with a bunch of different tools, and you're going to have to build that strategy as you go from week to week," he said.

The Minnesota Republican also signaled there would be little appetite for bipartisanship over the next two years, a sentiment he suggested would come from the other side of the aisle.

"Well look, they're not going to work with us, that's pretty clear, and I don't even know if they're going to be able to work with each other," Emmer said.

HAKEEM JEFFRIES FACES PROGRESSIVE HOLDOUTS BUT NO RIVAL AS HOUSE SPEAKER RACE TAKES SHAPE

Congressman Gregory W. Meeks, D-N.Y., and House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., address a press conference during the congressional delegation's visit to Denmark, in Copenhagen on Friday, April 25, 2025 (EMIL NICOLAI HELMS/Ritzau Scanpix/AFP via Getty Images)

He repeated a short time later, "I see nowhere where they're going to work with Republicans. And Republicans who think they are should think again, because they will tell you nice things, and then they will just go do what they do."

Meanwhile other House Republicans are already coming up with ideas on how to put Democrats on the spot on the House floor in the new Congress.

"We'll file amendments," said Rep. Randy Fine, R-Fla. "And I think that'll be where having good debaters and communicators would be important."

Holding amendment votes is commonplace before large pieces of legislation like the National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA), which Congress has enacted annually for decades to update and reauthorize critical national security policies.

Rep. Nick LaLota, R-N.Y., suggested an emphasis on a parliamentary procedure called the "motion to recommit," which allows the minority party to get a vote on altering or even killing a bill in its tracks.

"I expect we'll use that smartly," LaLota said. "But as well, a Speaker Hakeem Jeffries will be wise to work these problems backwards, to know that anything he puts on the floor that he wants to become law or budget needs President Trump's signature and probably 60 votes in the Senate."

Rep. Mark Harris, R-N.C., said "there's been a lot of conversation about going on offense."

"We want to make sure that they know when they come to the floor, they're gonna dread it, I hope," Harris said.

DEMOCRATS RALLY BEHIND GOP LAWMAKER'S HEGSETH IMPEACHMENT PUSH, SIGNAL MORE TO COME IN JANUARY

But there's another mechanism for forcing a House vote that's been used more and more often in recent years — often by their fellow Republicans.

Privileged resolutions allow lawmakers on either side to get a vote on a given bill within two days of its introduction. It generally begins with a procedural vote on whether to "table" the bill or refer it to committee in order to stop it, before a vote on the measure itself.

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., most recently invoked it to force the House to consider impeaching Secretary of War Pete Hegseth. But that was defeated when the rest of the House GOP banded together to refer the bill to committee.

The GOP lawmakers who spoke with Fox News Digital, however, signaled little appetite for using it.

U.S. Rep. Jim Jordan speaks during a press conference after House Republicans elected new leadership for the upcoming session of Congress at the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 10, 2026 in Washington, D.C. (Aaron Schwartz/Getty Images)

"I think either party would be wise to build consensus around things that help actually deliver for the American people," LaLota said. "The privileged resolutions have been abused in my time here, and there's a better way to do business."

And Emmer said his main goal for the next two years would be winning back the House majority in 2028 — which he insisted was a realistic goal.

He said at least 38 seats flipped by Democrats would be "ready to return to Republican hands," and could see some old faces return.

Asked whether he wanted to see some of those losing GOP incumbents run for their seats again, Emmer said, "I absolutely hope so, I've talked to several already."

Former local TV news anchor Ryan Elijah unseated Rep. Cory Mills in a high-stakes Florida GOP primary race. (Mike McClain/Ryan Elijah for Congress)

#5887. One industry surged DC lobbying 337% under Trump — here’s what’s driving the push

Trump’s escalating trade war with Canada could raise costs for businesses and consumers in key battleground states before voters head to the polls.

FIRST ON FOX: President Donald Trump has made tariffs a centerpiece of his trade agenda, and businesses are spending more on D.C. bureaucrats to help navigate those rules.

Rachel Greszler, who authored a new report from Advancing American Freedom (AAF), said companies are paying lobbyists to seek exemptions from tariffs instead of using that money to grow their businesses, hire workers or raise wages.

A new report from former Vice President Mike Pence's group found that lobbying disclosures mention tariffs rising 239% in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the same period in 2024. The total spending reported on those filings jumped 337%, though it may include lobbying on issues beyond tariffs.

A MUST-WIN MIDTERM STATE IS THE REAL LOSER OF TRUMP'S CANADA TRADE WAR

Businesses are increasingly turning to Washington lobbyists to navigate President Donald Trump’s shifting trade policies, with tariff-related lobbying disclosures surging 239% in the third quarter. (Kevin Carter/Getty Images)

"Affordability is top of mind for many Americans," Greszler told Fox News Digital, arguing that changing tariffs are raising costs, threatening jobs and squeezing paychecks.

Businesses are responding by stepping up their lobbying efforts. Fourteen organizations spent more than $1 million on lobbying in the third quarter, up from two in the same quarter of 2024. Another 105 spent more than $100,000, compared with 26 two years earlier.

Tariffs may also be affecting prices consumers pay.

TRUMP BET TARIFFS WOULD BRING BACK AMERICAN FACTORY JOBS. NEW REPORT SAYS IT DIDN'T WORK

Federal Reserve Bank of New York researchers estimated they added 2.9 percentage points to prices across the consumer goods they studied, including imported and U.S.-made products.

Greszler warned that small businesses unable to afford lobbyists to seek tariff exclusions could be forced to scale back or close.

Trump has defended tariffs as a central part of his trade agenda, arguing they will strengthen U.S. manufacturing and bring jobs and investment back to the country. The White House has also said foreign exporters will ultimately bear the cost.

A separate analysis from Advancing American Freedom estimated tariffs resulted in about 75,000 fewer manufacturing jobs in their first year and up to 1 million fewer jobs nationwide than would have been expected under pre-tariff trends.

The group said manufacturing was among the hardest-hit sectors, despite being a central target of the administration’s effort to bring factory work back.

Trade has been a central focus of the Trump administration, which has used tariffs to reshape U.S. trade relationships and encourage more manufacturing at home. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

The group also said U.S. trade with Canada fell by $50 billion, or 6.5%, before the latest round of Section 338 tariffs took effect. During that period, imports from other countries rose instead of U.S. manufacturing expanding.

As the administration continues to reshape trade policy, businesses will have to decide whether to keep spending on lobbyists and tariff planning or delay investments, hiring and expansion until the rules become clearer.

Amanda Macias covers the intersection of business, economics and politics, with a focus on how policy decisions shape markets, businesses and American workers.

#5883. GOP midterm bloodbath exposes cracks in Vance's 2028 White House ambitions

President Donald Trump said he was fine with Vice President JD Vance's post-midterm critique on the political unpopularity of the Iran conflict, but he would have wanted to hear Vance add that if they had to do it again, they would.

Vice President JD Vance has long been viewed as President Donald Trump's heir apparent, but a string of developments following Republicans' bruising midterm losses is raising new questions about his potential path to the White House in 2028.

Vance is facing criticism from some Republicans over his explanation for the party's election defeats, particularly his decision to blame the Iran war. Meanwhile, other prominent Republicans are beginning to draw support for potential 2028 bids, complicating the political picture for the vice president.

The political debate follows a devastating election for Republicans, who lost their majorities in both chambers of Congress beginning next year, with at least 30 GOP House members losing their races. The losses have prompted competing explanations from within the party over what went wrong and what Republicans should do differently.

Some of Vance's allies have reportedly urged him to consider sitting out the 2028 race following the midterm losses, The Wall Street Journal reported Nov. 7. However, others close to the vice president still expect him to run, The Washington Post reported Friday, underscoring the uncertainty surrounding his political future.

TRUMP SAYS HE DOESN’T DISAGREE WITH VANCE THAT IRAN WAR FUELED GOP LOSSES

Vice President JD Vance speaks during a press briefing at the White House in Washington D.C. on Sept. 3, 2026. (AP Photo/Mark Schiefelbein)

A Vance ally pushed back on suggestions that the midterm losses have diminished his 2028 prospects, saying the vice president will decide whether to run on his own timeline. The ally also argued that Vance would be better served by winning a competitive Republican primary than being handed the nomination, expressing confidence in his ability to prevail if he runs.

In a Nov. 4 post on X, Vance identified several concerns among voters who abandoned Republicans, including inflation, America First priorities and the U.S. relationship with Israel, but singled out the Iran war as the main reason for the party's defeats.

"The reason we lost is the war. The president himself said so last night," Vance wrote, adding that Republicans needed to acknowledge the political cost of the conflict as they consider where to go next.

The post drew sharp criticism from Rep. Randy Fine, R-Fla., who told Fox' LiveNOW in a Nov. 8 interview that he would not support Vance in a potential 2028 presidential primary.

PENCE DELIVERS SCATHING REBUKE OF VANCE'S IRAN WAR ASSESSMENT, BLAMES TRUMP POLICIES FOR GOP LOSSES

"I definitively will not be supporting the vice president in that nominating contest," Fine said, comparing Vance's remarks to rhetoric from "1932 Germany."

Fine, who is Jewish, accused Vance of blaming Jewish people for the Republican losses, pointing to his criticism of the war and the pollster's finding that some voters wanted a change in America's relationship with Israel.

Vance did not explicitly blame Jewish people in his post, and his office previously declined to respond to Fine's characterization.

Vice President JD Vance walks to board Air Force Two at Joint Base Andrews, Maryland on Nov. 2, 2026. (Tom Brenner/Getty Images)

VANCE COURTING DISGRUNTLED TRUMP VOTERS TO FORM ‘NEW RIGHT’ ALLIANCE AFTER REPUBLICAN BLOODBATH, SOURCES SAY

Some Republicans are already expressing support for possible alternatives to Vance. Rep. Marlin Stutzman, R-Ind., told Fox News Digital after the midterms that he was "all in" for Secretary of State Marco Rubio, describing him as a potential unifying figure for Republicans following their election losses.

Rubio is among several prominent Republicans whose names have surfaced as possible 2028 presidential contenders, along with Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis and Sen. Ted Cruz of Texas.

From left to right: Vice President JD Vance, Sen. Ted Cruz of Texas, and Gov. Ron DeSantis of Florida (Alex Brandon/Pool via REUTERS; Eric Lee/Bloomberg via Getty Images; Joe Raedle/Getty Images)

Former Vice President Mike Pence also disputed Vance's explanation for Republicans' midterm losses, arguing in an exclusive Fox News Digital interview Thursday that voters had grown frustrated not because Trump launched the Iran war, but because his administration failed to finish it.

"There's no question that the war in Iran became a frustration for millions of Americans, but it wasn't because President Trump started the war or took on Iran. It's because we stopped short and didn't finish it," Pence said. "As Americans saw the politics of appeasement that the administration embraced, one negotiation after another, I think people became ... frustrated with the lack of progress, frustrated with the price of fuel at the pump."

Trump, meanwhile, has publicly defended Vance's assessment of the midterms, rejecting suggestions that the remarks damaged their relationship.

"No, not at all," Trump told reporters Sunday when asked whether Vance's comments had strained their relationship, according to The Hill.

But the president also stood by his decision to launch the war, arguing that he would make the same choice again despite the political fallout.

Donald Trump Jr. also backed Vance's assessment, writing "Every word of this is [100 emojis]" in response to his Nov. 4 post on X.

On Thursday, Trump directed Vance to focus his attention on alleged corruption and crime in Democratic-led cities and states rather than pursuing negotiations with Iran, declaring that the United States no longer needed a deal with Tehran.

Vance had played a central role in diplomatic efforts involving Iran, including negotiations over a short-lived truce earlier this year.

Vice President JD Vance praised President Donald Trump's leadership style and work ethic during the interview. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

"The Blue States, such as Minnesota, California, Illinois, and many more, are all poorly run, corrupt disasters," Trump wrote in the post. "Billions of Dollars is being stolen each and every month. Our Budget could be quickly BALANCED with the correction of this large scale criminal activity. CORRUPTION is in the Democrat Leadership’s blood!"

"We don’t need a deal with Iran. Those days have long since passed. We’ve got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we’ve got their Strait!" the president continued.

VANCE ‘PRIVATELY’ BLASTS NETANYAHU AS IRAN WAR DIVIDES TRUMP ADMINISTRATION AND 2028 AMBITIONS LOOM

Trump has previously signaled support for Vance as his potential successor, telling donors during an Oval Office meeting over the summer that Republicans needed to elect the vice president in 2028, Fox News Digital reported at the time.

But Trump has stopped short of publicly endorsing Vance, and the president has continued to leave open the possibility of other Republicans entering the race.

When asked on Nov. 4 whether he had talked about a prospective 2028 White House run with Vance, Trump replied that the two discuss "a lot of things," Axios reported. Trump added: "Maybe he [Vance] won't run. I don't know who's going to run."

Still, Vance told Mark Halperin in an October interview that he believes Trump would back him if he jumps into the presidential race, ABC 7 reported.

"I really have not made up my mind about what to do in 2028. I just haven't focused on it. We, you, and I have talked about that. Look, I think if I run, the president will endorse me," Vance said during the interview.

#5869. GOP state officials push to revive Trump's mail voting restrictions for 2028 election

An election worker sorts mail-in ballots at the Miami-Dade County Supervisor of Elections Office in Doral for Florida's primary election in August. Lynne Sladky/AP hide caption

Voting for this year's midterm elections just concluded, but Republican officials in a number of states are already looking to shape how votes are cast in 2028.

Twelve GOP state attorneys general are pushing to revive the president's bid for mail-in voting restrictions in time for the 2028 election.

On Thursday, they started the process of appealing a September order by U.S. District Judge Carl Nichols, one of the two judges who blocked the U.S. Postal Service from carrying out a plan released in response to an executive order by President Trump.

The Republican state attorneys general represent Alabama, Florida, Indiana, Kansas, Louisiana, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, Oklahoma, South Carolina, South Dakota and Texas. It's not clear, however, how long some of these states will continue this case, given that Democrats won up and down the ballot in the midterms. In Texas and Kansas, Democratic candidates are set to take control of their states' attorney general's offices in January.

In September, the Supreme Court refused to pause a similar ruling by U.S. District Judge Indira Talwani, who is based in Boston. That order stopped USPS from working on its revised rule for federal ballot mail, but it only applied to this year's midterm elections.

Nichols' preliminary injunction blocks USPS from implementing the plan for all federal elections, including the 2028 race.

In a court filing, Justice Department attorneys confirmed the Trump administration is not appealing Nichols' ruling. The appeal deadline was Thursday.

The blocked rule — which the Postal Service released in late August — would require states to turn over lists of voters' names and addresses in order to have them delivered by USPS.

Nichols, a Washington, D.C.-based judge who was nominated by Trump, found that the mailing agency likely has no power to impose that requirement on states.

"As the Government concedes, the Postal Service could issue (and enforce) the Rule only if Congress gave it the authority to do so, but here no statute grants the Postal Service the power to issue key parts of the Rule," Nichols wrote.

The administration's earlier push to put these new restrictions in place for the midterms alarmed many election officials, who were concerned about last-minute changes causing major disruptions to the tens of millions who regularly vote by mail. Some voters with disabilities and those in places without polling sites, they feared, could have lost the ability to exercise their right to cast a ballot.

A whistleblower report claimed the online system officials would need to use to follow USPS' revised ballot requirements had not been properly tested and could have resulted in many eligible voters not getting their mail-in ballots in time or at all.

While the Supreme Court ultimately stopped implementation of the USPS rule for the midterms, Justices Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas dissented from that ruling.

Fellow conservative Justice Brett Kavanaugh said in a concurring opinion that he believed state and local election officials did not have enough time to "reasonably implement the rule" this time. But Kavanaugh noted that "there is at least a fair prospect that the final rule falls within the Postal Service's statutory authority."

A previous version of this story incorrectly said the deadline for the Justice Department to appeal a court order was Nov. 13. The deadline was Nov. 12.

#5856. Democrat Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins wins race for Alaska governor, replaces a Republican

Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, a Democrat, has won the race for governor of Alaska and will replace the outgoing Republican. Here he's seen at a polling station at the University of Alaska Anchorage on Election Day. Eric Stone/Alaska Public Media hide caption

Democrat Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, a former state representative from the small island community of Sitka, won the race for Alaska governor, according to a race call by the Associated Press. At 37, he'll be the youngest governor in state history.

Kreiss-Tomkins will replace outgoing Gov. Mike Dunleavy, a term-limited Republican who has been governor since 2018. The state's last Democratic governor was Tony Knowles, who left office in 2002.

Kreiss-Tomkins, known by his initials JKT, made his "high-energy, bipartisan, problem-solving approach" a centerpiece of his campaign.

He's said he plans to build an administration of Democrats, Republicans and independents and described himself at a debate as a "man of the middle." He was endorsed by three former governors — a Democrat, a Republican and an independent — along with Republican Sen. Lisa Murkowski.

Alaskans have had enough of zero-sum partisan politics, he said in an interview just before voting at a precinct in Anchorage on Election Day.

"I strongly believe people just want things to work, and want people to work together," Kreiss-Tomkins said.

The AP race call came after over a week of counting votes, including those coming in by mail and from remote parts of the state. In the ranked-choice system the state uses, he faced three Republican candidates, including one backed by President Trump. Winning requires getting a majority of the votes.

Voters were also facing a question on the ballot about whether to scrap that unique, ranked-choice system. That proposal failed, according to another AP race call, keeping the system in place.

With Kreiss-Tomkins' win and others in the midterms, Democrats will hold 30 governors' offices across the country, up from 24 now.

Kreiss-Tomkins will take office in a state where voters have loudly complained that things aren't working as they should.

Rising costs and relatively flat state funding have forced schools to close and class sizes to rise. Already-high costs for food, fuel and housing have risen sharply, and a looming shortage of easily accessible natural gas near the state's population centers is threatening to push prices higher. And Alaskans have been migrating out of the state for more than a decade.

The Republicans in the race were Trump-backed business owner Bernadette Wilson, former Anchorage Mayor Dave Bronson and former Alaska Attorney General Treg Taylor.

Kreiss-Tomkins is set to take office at noon Dec. 7.

Eric Stone covers state government for Alaska Public Media.

#5853. Hegseth's failed impeachment was a warning to Democrats. Where will they go from here?

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., speaks during a news conference on Nov. 4, 2025, on Capitol Hill in Washington, D.C. Tom Brenner/Getty Images hide caption

Democrats are not in the majority yet, but the first post-election test of their ability to get things done was a failure.

The House was unable to advance an impeachment effort of Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth. Eleven Democrats were no-shows for the vote. Based on the math in this vote: If those 11 had been there and if no more Republicans showed up, the attempt would have gone forward.

Now, for those who want to see Hegseth impeached, it's fair to ask why only one Republican was in favor of it, Rep. Thomas Massie of Kentucky, who led the effort, especially after an election that saw GOP wipeouts in no small measure because of the Iran war? The GOP still controls the floor, so a Democratic speaker would have been able to leave the vote open longer for the 11 to be able to cast their votes.

But it's also fair to ask if this would have happened under Nancy Pelosi's watch? (Ironically, she was one of the 11 who didn't vote for advancing Hegseth's impeachment because she had flown back to San Francisco for a veterans' event.) Or if this kind of effort is what people want in the first place.

First, on the Pelosi point: The former House speaker and retiring congresswoman was known for the discipline she kept in her caucus. She knew where the votes were, who was voting how and why – and, most importantly, did not move forward with efforts that didn't have the votes.

Hakeem Jeffries, the heir apparent Democratic leader, is going to have the largest majority in Congress since the early 1990s. His leadership and agenda priorities are going to be scrutinized and compared to Pelosi – and this wasn't a great start to this new era.

Second, on whether impeachments are what people want to see: it depends on which people. Certainly, the Democratic base — and some beyond that — wants accountability for the Trump administration.

Voters more broadly also said corruption was a top issue in the campaign. But walking the line between reasonable accountability and oversight versus impeachments is a potential tight rope.

Democrats will have to balance the mandate they were given to bring down prices (sound familiar?) and end the war with the accountability of the Trump administration that many in the Democratic base feverishly wants and expects.

A Reuters/Ipsos poll taken after the election, found that 8-in-10 people think Democrats' focus in the first 100 days should be on the cost of living, three-quarters said lowering health care costs, 7 in 10 said lowering gas prices, while 6 in 10 said ending the Iran war and eliminating President Trump's tariffs.

What about investigations and impeachment? Only 40% said investigating the Trump administration should be a priority in those early days. But 71% of Democrats do want this to be a first 100 days focus. Only 36% of independents said the same.

Almost two-thirds said they are concerned that Democrats will spend too much time on investigations, which included, by the way, half of Democrats.

When it comes specifically to pursuing impeachment of Trump, only 33% said they think it would be good for the country. On the other hand, 63% of Democrats do. Only 30% of independents said it would be good for the country.

More than half also said they think Democrats should at least make efforts to work with the Trump administration. That, however, only included 40% of Democrats.

So this is going to be tricky to navigate for Democratic leadership, but a major part of a speaker's job is messaging, particularly when that speaker's party does not control the White House.

People are going to be watching the new Democratic majority for what it focuses on, and even with the Hegseth effort, it was clear that many conservatives were thrilled with not only how it went but that Democrats were fueling Massie's effort.

It arguably backfired and strengthened Hegseth's position within the administration. The White House and Republicans rallied around him. Even if some Republicans on Capitol Hill might have misgivings with Hegseth for one reason or another, and they and Vice President Vance think the war in Iran was the main reason for the midterm losses, opposing Democrats is far more unifying.

Politics is often (some would say too often) a team sport, and no group is more conditioned to put the T-shirt on and run the playbook than the red team.

It certainly gave Republicans more of a chance to go on offense than talking about health care, the cost of living or the war and gas prices.

Democratic leadership is certainly aware of the line it has to walk, but Jeffries is going to have to make some decisions. Asked on PBS NewsHour this week if Democrats would be impeaching more Trump officials, he said, "We will cross that bridge when we get to it."

But a House speaker is the one who builds the legislative bridges. He or she controls the agenda.

Jeffries, of course, also noted, as he has elsewhere, that affordability is a priority.

"We're committed to driving down the high cost of living in every area," he said. "That's housing, that's health care, that's groceries, that's gas, that's caregiving – to making this country affordable again for everyone."

Democrats have been given a huge opportunity with majorities in both the House and Senate — but the balance of priorities, timing, messaging and discipline are all going to be major tests.

The stakes couldn't be higher. Arguably, what Democrats focus on will lay the foundation for views of the party's competence heading into the 2028 presidential election, and they don't want to squander the advantages they have.

The Ipsos poll showed that Democrats have big leads on which party has a better plan, policy or approach for health care (42%-23%), the cost of living (40%-25%) and the economy (37%-30%).

But they have to be careful. A lot of people are not convinced that Democrats are focused on the issues that are important to them. Only 47% in the Ipsos poll said that, including only 40% of independents.

Only 44% said they're confident that Democrats are putting the best interests of the American people above their own personal interests.

So while Democrats have the wind at their backs, the jury is still out on whether people are confident in their leadership.
